​"The Peace Officers' Civil Service Association of California is an independent, non-profit organization of active peace officers, other public employees and public-spirited citizens. It is free of entangling official connection with any police or sheriff's department, cooperating with all city, county and state law enforcing agencies. It serves exclusively for the betterment of public service through the protection of the rights of peace officers and all public employees."

PARTS I and II

PREFACE 

In keeping with our belief, education of the peace officer means knowledge which has practical value. I have mapped out these examinations for home study after consultation with many police chiefs, police officers and law enforcement agencies.

My conviction is that the days of the “hitching post" type of law enforcement are long past, when an officer was provided only a badge and a gun as the tools of the profession, with no knowledge of vital police science. Law enforcement officers, investigators and police administrators must not only be equipped with modern physical weapons, but must also be trained in the latest methods of crime detection and crime prevention for the enforcement of the law and the administration of justice. 

Enforcement of the law is a profession, not just a job, which requires intelligence, skill and everlasting study and training. 

It is my hope that every police officer and peace officer in this nation will some day have this book in his home library, to be used as reference to brush up on the rusty spots in connection with his law enforcement work and to assist in preparing himself for promotion to higher rank and greater opportunity.

The peace officer must have a clear perspective of himself in relation to his chosen profession. Peace officers cannot allow themselves to stagnate while science makes progressive strides into new fields of criminology. The peace officer's objective of honest, intelligent fulfillment of duty, designed to give more adequate protection to the citizens of his community, must be accomplished by raising his own standards in the police profession. With it will come a sense of accomplishment, a higher morale, a greater satisfaction, as well as renewed courage, plus wholesome respect for the effort, and just reward.

The use of this home study book, together with the Penal Code and other available sources of information, will give any peace officer the right start in his chosen career. To hold this position of responsibility and command the respect that a courteous, trained, tactful and competent peace officer is entitled to, depends directly upon the sincerity of purpose behind that officer's efforts.

Study alone will not fit you for the responsibility of protecting lives and property, but study, along with practical knowledge, common sense and fortitude, will prepare you to represent the finest profession in the world . . . 

Law Enforcement. 

Sincerely,

A. BELDON GILBERT, Editor, State Director Peace Officers Civil Service 

Association of California, Inc.

INTRODUCTION 

In presenting this latest addition to its many previous publications, the Peace Officers' Civil Service Association of California seeks to further its sole purpose in existing-to work constantly for the betterment of public service and all public employees. Only by raising the standards of service rendered by public employees to constantly higher levels and by extending the full protection of the merit system of civil service to these employees will the public realize the benefits to which it is entitled. 

High standards of public service by employees cannot be given unless and until the public employees in the police, fire and other departments are protected in that employment from the wasteful evils of political domination.

The Association presents this publication with pride in the knowledge that it will prove invaluable not only to the individual police officer, but will aid governmental agencies in raising the qualifications of the law enforcement professions of their entire groups. Compiled by Mr. Gilbert after a very long period of labor in selection of material, this work is another example of the efforts of this nonprofit Association in its program over the years for the improvement of the employment conditions of the peace officer and other public employees by aiding them in giving the highest possible type of public service.

Good public service cannot exist without the protection of the merit system of civil service. 

PAUL SHEEDY, Counsel, Peace Officers Civil Service 

Association of California, Inc. XVI 

---

Peace Officers Home Study Book 

1. Q. What is the origin, historically, of the office of Sheriff?

A. The word "sheriff" is derived from a combination of the 

Saxon words "scir" (shire or county) and "gerefa" (reeve or bailiff). The sheriff (or shire-reeve) in early England was the King's bailiff, keeper of the peace, and ministerial officer of the King's courts in the shire or county. His ministerial and judicial duties were those originally committed to an earl in each shire. The sheriff, today, is similarly the chief executive officer of his county, and the ministerial officer of the superior court. 

2. Q. What is a Deputy Sheriff? 3. 

A. A person duly appointed and sworn to enforce and execute the criminal laws of the state and ordinances of the county. 

Memorize the general duties of a sheriff or deputy, as enumerated by Harlow and defined in Section 93, Stautes of 1907. 

1. To preserve the peace; 

2. Arrest and take before the nearest magistrate, for examination, all persons who attempt to commit or who have commited a public offense. 

3. Prevent and suppress any affrays, breaches of the peace, riots, and insurrections which may come to his knowledge. 

4. Attend all SUPERIOR courts held within his county and obey all lawful orders and directions of ALL courts held within his county. 

5. Command the aid of as many male inhabitants of his county as he may think necessary in the execution of his duties. (The posse comitatus). 

6. Take charge of and keep the county jail and the prisoners therein. 

7. Release on the record all attachments of real property when the attachment placed in his hand has been released or discharged. 

8. Indorse upon all process and notices, the year, month, day, hour and minute of reception, and issue therefor to the person delivering it, on payment of fees, a certificate showing the name of the parties, title of paper, and time when received. 

9. Serve all process and notices in the manner prescribed by law.

10. Certify under his hand, upon process or notices, the manner and time of service; if he fails to make the service, the reason for his failure and return the same without delay. 

11. And such other powers and duties as may be imposed upon him by any other statutes in the execution of which his services may be required. 

Q. What is meant by "process"? 

A. "Process" includes all writs, warrants, summons, and orders of courts of justice or of judicial officers. 

Q. What is meant by "notice"?

A. "Notice" includes all papers and orders (except process) required to be served in any proceeding before any court, board, officer, or when required by law to be served independently of such proceeding. 

4. Q. What relation does the Peace Officer bear toward the com munity?

A. He is a servant of the people and should never aspire to be their master. The power that created the offices of Deputy 

Sheriff and Police Officer and clothed them with authority, comes directly from the people, each of whom, be he or she ever so humble, is entitled to kindness and official courtesy from public servants. 

5. Q. What are the qualities most required in a Peace Officer?

A. The qualities most required in a Peace Officer are broad intelligence, firmness with kindness, good judgment, good character, sympathy with his kind, and above all, a thorough knowledge of the laws and the people to whom he must apply them. 

6. Q. How should a Peace Officer conduct himself toward citizens generally.

A. Always as a gentleman, kind and pleasant in language and ready to assist them and never under any circumstances hold controversy upon any subject whatever, but afford them all the information in his power with as little delay as possible. 

7. Q. When an officer takes a prisoner into custody, what is his duty?

A. TO SEARCH THE PRISONER FOR WEAPONS AND EVI- 

DENCE and conduct him directly to the station, using all necessary care to prevent his escape, record and book prisoner and take him before the nearest court without unnecessary delay.

8. Q. How should a prisoner be treated? more.

A. With kindness, always as much restraint as necessary, and no 

Prisoners should be taken direct to the County or City Jail, unless it is desired to question them and obtain statements, in which case they may be brought to the office. 

DO NOT ALLOW PRISONERS TO GO TO THEIR ROOMS FOR ANY PURPOSE. 

9. Q. When a person is taken into custody, is it necessary to guard against his disposing of weapons, or articles of property in his possession?

A. Yes. The person must be searched and all weapons and evidence taken from him. If the person is a female, care must be taken that she does not dispose of the evidence before she can be searched by the matron. If necessary, the matron may be called to the place and thus avoid the chance of transporting the prisoner with the evidence in her possession. 

10. Q. What is the duty of a sheriff or deputy when acting as court bailiff?

A. The sheriff, in attendance upon court, must act as the crier thereof, call the parties and witnesses and all other persons bound to appear at the court, and make proclamations of the opening and adjournment of the court and of any other matter under its direction. He must care for the jury in such a way as to prevent misconduct. Section 1128 of the Penal 

Code says; "When a jury retires to deliberate, an officer (the bailiff) must be sworn to keep them together in some private and convenient place and not permit any person to speak to or communicate with them, nor to do so himself unless by order of the court or to ask them whether they have agreed upon a verdict, and to return them into court when they have so agreed, or when ordered by the court.' 

11. Q. May a sheriff or deputy ever act as an attorney?

A. No. Under Section 4121 of the Political Code, sheriffs and their deputies are prohibited from practicing law or acting as attorneys or counsellors at law, or from having as a partner a lawyer or one who acts as such. Violation of this section is considered a misdemeanor in office. 

12. Q. What is the penalty for embezzlement or falsification of accounts on the part of a sheriff or deputy? 

A. One to ten years' imprisonment and disqualification from holding any public office in the state.

13 Q. Do you know that a Peace Officer is liable to punishment for neglect of duty? 

A. Yes. Every Sheriff, Coroner, keeper of a jail, Constable or other peace officer, who wilfully refuses to receive or arrest any person charged with a criminal offense, is punishable by a fine not exceeding five thousand dollars, and imprisonment in the County Jail not exceeding five years. 

14. Q. Since the sheriff is the chief executive officer of the county, is there any other officer whose power supersedes his, at any time?

A. Yes. The Coroner must act in the sheriff's stead in executing any process of the court to which the sheriff is himself a party. 

15. Q. Under what circumstances may an officer legally use force?

A. In self-defense, when assaulted by a person of superior physical strength and in effecting arrests where force is necessary to overcome the resistance of the person arrested. 

16. Q. Under what circumstances is a Peace Officer legally justified in using his pistol? 

A. The pistol should never be used except, as follows: 

1. In self-defense, when assaulted by a person using a deadly weapon or means likely to produce great bodily harm. 

2. To prevent the commission of a felony. 

3. To effect the arrest of a person accused of a felony. 

4. To prevent the escape of a person arrested for the commission of a felony. 

5. To retake felons who have been rescued or who have escaped. 

Therefore, if a Peace Officer, sworn to execute the criminal laws of the State, should through cowardice, passion, or malice, shoot at, wound or kill another, he would be criminally liable in a double sense, for the reason that in so doing, he would be guilty of violating his oath of office as well as committing a crime. 

17. Q. Should prisoner escape from custody, has a Peace Officer power to pursue and re-arrest him? 

A. Yes, and in case the refugee takes refuge in a house, the doors may be broken open after a demand for admission is made, and the officer gives notice to the occupants of his office and business.

18. Q. Under the division of Section 196 Penal Code, when is homi cide by a public officer justifiable?

A. 1. In obedience to any judgment of a competent court. 

2. When necessarily committed in overcoming actual resistance to the execution of some legal process, or in the discharge of any other legal duty. 

3. When necessarily committed in retaking felons who have been rescued or have escaped, or when necessarily committed in arresting persons charged with felony and who are fleeing from justice or resisting such arrest. 

19. Q. As a Peace Officer, give a safe interpretation of the above provisions, a general rule by which your decision upon a mode of action in any such case would not jeopardize yourself with the law.

A. The rule deducible from the authorities is that an officer, in arresting or preventing the escape of a person charged with a felony, may use such force as is reasonably necessary, even to the taking of life. BUT IF THE FELON CAN BE TAKEN OR HIS ESCAPE PREVENTED WITHOUT KILLING THE OFFENDER AND THE OFFENDER BE SLAIN, THE OF- FICER IS GUILTY OF, AT LEAST, MANSLAUGHTER. 

MOREOVER, EXCEPT IN NECESSARY SELF-DEFENSE, AN OFFICER IS NOT PROTECTED IN HOMICIDE COMMITTED IN ARRESTING OR RECAPTURING ONE CHARGED WITH A MISDEMEANOR AND NOT A FELONY. 

20. Q. What section of the Penal Code sets forth the main provi sions of the law of arrest by which you, as a Peace Officer, will be governed? 21. 

A. SECTION 836. 

Memorize its provisions as follows, particularly the five subsections pertaining to arrests WITHOUT WARRANTS and the sixth provision for arrest without warrant. (The latter is not in the penal code, but is so-called "Judge-made law," i.e., accepted decisions of the courts):

A Peace Officer may make an arrest in obedience to a warrant delivered to him, or may without a warrant arrest a person: 

1. For a public offense, either felony or misdemeanor, committed or attempted in his presence. (836-1 P.C.)

2. When the person arrested has committed a felony, although not in his presence. (836-2 P.C.) 

3. When a felony has, in fact, been committed, and he has reasonable cause for believing the person arrested to have committed it. (836-3 P.C.) 

4. On a charge made, upon reasonable cause (i.e., by a creditable authority,) of the commission of a felony by the one arrested. (836-4 P.C.) · 

AT NIGHT, when he has REASONABLE CAUSE to believe that he HAS committed a felony. (836-5 P.C.)

AT NIGHT, when he has REASONABLE CAUSE to believe that he IS ABOUT to commit a felony. ("Judge-made law"). 

22. Q. When may arrests be made in civil (non-criminal) actions? 

A. SECTION 479 CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE as follows: 

1. In an action for recovery of money or damages, in an action arising upon contract, express or implied, when defendant is about to depart from the state with intent to defraud his creditors. 

2. In an action to recover possession of personal property when the property has been concealed, removed or disposed of to prevent its being taken by the sheriff. 

3. When defendant has been guilty of fraud in incurring the obligation for which action is brought or in concealing or disposing of the property for which the action is brought. 

4. When defendant has removed or disposed of his property, or is about to do so, with intent to defraud his creditors. 

5. On bench warrants, for failure to appear, or for any contempt of court in civil, as in criminal, actions. 

23. Q. In making arrests in civil actions, must the officer be protected by a warrant or can he arrest without a warrant? 

A. Must arrest by warrant only, since none of these instances fit the provisions of No. 21 above, for arrests without warrants. 

That is, none of them is a PUBLIC offense committed in the officer's presence, and none of them is a charge of felony, since the actions are civil and not criminal. 

24. Q. Has a Deputy Sheriff or Police Officer authority to arrest for a violation of the state laws? 

A. Yes. It is the duty of a Deputy Sheriff or Police Officer to enforce the criminal laws of the state as well as the county or city ordinances.

25. Q. What persons are exempt from arrest?

A. Penal Code Section 133.4-133.5. 

1. Electors (voters) on days of election, while in attendance or while going to or from elections. felony, or breach of the peace). (Except for treason, 2. Members of the legislature, during sessions and for 15 days before and after sessions. (Except for treason, felony, or breach of the peace). 

3. Persons belonging to the military forces, while going to, remaining at, or returning from military duty. 

4. Persons who have been subpoenaed as witnesses in some other court, while they are going to, remaining at, or returning from such other court. (These are in the broadest sense of interpretation of the 

Sections.) 

26. Q. Should a prisoner be desirous of making a statement or confession ought the officer caution him against incriminating himself?

A. Certainly not; the officer must not induce or prevent the prisoner from making any statement but attentively notice every expression used which may further the ends of justice and be prepared to repeat the same when required. 

27. Q. Besides warrants of arrests, what two other familiar processes are issued to require attendance in court, and is serving them the same as making an arrest?

A. Subpoenas and citations. Their service is not an arrest, but failure to obey them is punishable as contempt of court. 

28. Q. What is the proper mode of giving evidence on the witness stand?

A. Always maintain a respectful attitude toward the court, attorneys, or witnesses. Never color testimony to appear different from real facts; answer positively and always tell nothing but the truth, and if situations or conversations are repeated, tell the court just what the situation was as the case may demand. 

29. Q. Of what interest is it to an officer if a complaint is dismissed by the court or his superior officer?

A. Of no interest whatever; he has done his duty when he has arrested his man and made an efficient investigation and preparation of the case. However, it is well to learn the reason for the dismissal in order that the officer may know whether it was proper for him to make the arrest in such case to determine his conduct in future cases and to learn wherein, if at all, he made a mistake. 

30. Q. How are crimes classified? 

A. As felonies and misdemeanors. 

31. Q. What is a felony? (17 P.C.) Read the Code. 

A. A felony is a crime which is punishable by death or imprisonment in the state prison. 

32. Q. What is a misdemeanor? (17 P.C.)

A. Misdemeanors include all crimes less than a felony; crimes punishable by imprisonment in a city or county jail or by fine or both such fine and imprisonment, removal from public office, disqualification to hold office. 

33. Q. When a felony has been committed, what is a peace officer's first duty?

A. To arrest the person committing it at all hazards. 

34. Q. What are State Misdemeanors? 

A. Offenses against the State Laws, the punishment of which is by fine or imprisonment in a jail or other than the state prison. 

35. Q. What are County Misdemeanors? 

A. Violations of County Ordinances punishable by a fine or imprisonment. 

36. Q. What are City Misdemeanors? 

A. Violations of City Ordinances punishable by fine or imprisonment. 

37. Q. In the event of a great crime or other casualty occurring on his post, how should a peace officer act? 

A. With decision and promptness; hasten to the scene thereof; notify his superior officer; secure the supposed criminal, if possible, then fully investigate the matter and make a complete report in writing to the superior officer at the office. 

Always set down the details of a case in a notebook. 

38. Q. Is there any specific percentage of women or men required for a trial jury?

A. None.

39. Q. Is a subpoena used in a criminal or a civil action? 

A. Both. 

40. Q. Define a subpoena. 

A. A subpoena is the process by which the attendance of a witness is required. 

41. Q. Define a subpoena DUCES TECUM. 

A. A subpoena requiring a witness to BRING WITH HIM any books, documents, or other things under his control which he is bound by law to produce as evidence. 

42. Q. How is a civil subpoena served?

A. It can be served by any person, and is served by showing the original and delivering a copy, or a ticket containing its substance, to the witness personally, giving or offering him at the same time, if demanded by him, the fees to which he is entitled for travel to and from the place designated and for one day's attendance. ($2 a day, and 20c a mile one way, in a court of record.) 

43. Q. How is a criminal subpoena served?

A. By any person, but if delivered to a peace officer must be served BY HIM, in his jurisdiction, by delivering a copy and showing the original, and informing the witness of its contents if requested. The officer must, without delay, make a written signed return of service, stating the time and place of service. 

44. Q. What is a citation? 

A. A direction issued by the Clerk of a court of record, under seal of the court, requiring the person cited to appear at a time and place specified. 

45. Q. How is a citation served?

A. Same as a civil summons (see below), and at least five days before the return date. 

46. Q. What is a summons? 

A. A civil writ for the purpose of giving the defendant authentic notice that an action has been commenced against him, to apprise him of the nature and amount of the claim of the plaintiff, and to compel his appearance in court to answer to these demands within a time stated, under penalty of forfeiting all subsequent right to dispute their validity or prevent their enforcement.

47. Q. How is a summons served?

A. By a sheriff, or any person 18 years or over who is not a party to the action, and by delivering to each of the defend ants PERSONALLY a copy of the summons and a COPY OF THE COMPLAINT. 

48. Q. For the peace officer, what is the distinction between a civil and a criminal action?

A. A civil action is an action between individuals or groups of individuals for a PRIVATE wrong, of omission or commission. 

A criminal action is one taken by the state as a whole against one or more individuals for a PUBLIC OFFENSE, i.e., a violation of the law which is considered a crime (an offense against all the people). 

49. Q. What is an arrest?

A. An arrest is taking a person into custody in a case and in the manner provided by law. 

50. Q. By whom may an arrest be made? (834 P.C.)

A. By a peace officer or by a private person. 

51. Q. How is an arrest made? (835 P.C.)

A. By ACTUAL RESTRAINT of the person of the defendant, after having informed him of the INTENTION to arrest him, the CAUSE of the arrest, and the AUTHORITY to make itexcept when the person to be arrested is then engaged in committing or attempting to commit the offense, or is being pursued immediately after its commission, or after an escape, in which case he is PRESUMED TO KNOW THE REASON 

for his arrest. (835 & 841 P.C.) 

52. Q. When may an arrest be made by a private person? 

A. SEE SECTION 837 P.C., as follows: 

An arrest may be made by a private citizen in the following three INSTANCES: 

1. For a public offense attempted or committed in his presence. 

2. When the person arrested HAS COMMITTED A FEL- 

ONY, though not in his presence. 

3. When a FELONY HAS IN FACT BEEN COMMITTED, and he has REASONABLE GROUND FOR BELIEVING THE PERSON ARRESTED to have committed it.

53. Q. From the above, does a crime have to have been IN FACT committed before a private citizen can make an arrest? 

A. Yes. (837 P.C.) 

54. Q. Is this also true of a peace officer?

A. No, for a peace officer may make an arrest in the following three additional instances (See Question 21): 

1. On a CHARGE MADE, upon reasonable cause, of the commission of a felony by the person arrested. (836-4 P.C.) 

2. AT NIGHT, when there is reasonable cause to BELIEVE 

HE HAS COMMITTED a felony. (836-5 P.C.) 

3. AT NIGHT, when there is reasonable cause to BELIEVE 

A FELONY IS ABOUT to be committed. ("Judge-made" provision)

Likewise, arrest on warrants is usually restricted to peace officers. 

55. Q. Who are peace officers?

A. 1. SHERIFF of a county; MARSHAL of a municipal court; 

CONSTABLE of a township; MARSHAL or POLICEMAN 

of a town or city. 

2. INSPECTORS OF CALIFORNIA STATE BOARD OF 

PHARMACY, not exceeding ten. 

3. Special agents and assistant special agents (not exceeding two each) of the STATE BOARD OF MEDICAL EXAM- INERS. 

4. Superintendent and special criminal investigators of

STATE BUREAU OF CRIMINAL IDENTIFICATION AND INVESTIGATION. 

5. Chief and inspectors of STATE DIVISION OF NARCOTIC ENFORCEMENT. 

6. Chief and officers of CALIFORNIA HIGHWAY PATROL. 

56. Q. What is a warrant of arrest? 

A. A warrant of arrest is "an order in writing, in the name of the people, signed by a magistrate, commanding the arrest of the defendant"-(814 P.C.). It is directed to any Sheriff,

Marshal, Constable or Policeman in the state, if issued by a court of record; or to any Sheriff, Marshal, Constable or 

Policeman in the county, if issued by a justice or police court.

57. Q. What force may be used in making an arrest?

A. The defendant must not be subjected to any more restraint than is necessary for his arrest and detention, but if he flees or resists, the officer may use all necessary means to effect the arrest, including the breaking of doors or windows, after first having demanded admittance and explained the purpose for which admittance is desired. 

58. Q. May an arrest for a misdemeanor be made in the night time?

A. Yes, and no. Yes, if it is for a misdemeanor committed or attempted in the officer's presence. Yes, if the arrest is being made on a warrant bearing the magistrate's endorsement for night time service. No, when a misdemeanor warrant is not so endorsed. 

59. Q. What is the penalty for neglect of duty in connection with arrests?

A. Fine not exceeding $5000 and imprisonment in County Jail not exceeding five years, for refusal to receive or arrest parties charged with crime. 

60. Q. What are the penalties for excessive use of force?

A. 1. Fine not exceeding $2000 and removal from office for willful inhumanity or oppression toward any prisoner in officer's care or custody. 

2. Fine not exceeding $5000 and imprisonment in County 

Jail not exceeding five years for assaulting or beating any person, under color of authority, without lawful necessity. 

61. Q. Does a warrant of arrest ever outlaw? 

A. No, a warrant is good forever until served. 

62. Q. What is meant by a crime becoming outlawed?

A. This means that the complaint, indictment or information was not filed within the period of the statute limitations-one year in the case of misdemeanors, and three years in the case of felonies, with the exception of murder, embezzlement of public money or falsification of public records, which never outlaw. 

63. Q. Before serving a warrant, should an officer inspect it carefully to satisfy himself concerning its regularity? 

A. Yes, since in serving a warrant irregular on its face, he might become liable for false arrest. A warrant, to be regular on its face, should contain the name of the defendant, if known, or a designation, such as John Doe, if unknown, the time of issuing it, the county, city or town where it is issued, and the signature and title of office of the issuing magistrate.

64. Q. May a warrant be served outside the county in which it is issued?

A. Yes, if issued by a judge of a Supreme Court, District Court of Appeal, Superior Court, Municipal Court, or other Court of Record and directed to any peace officer in the state; but if issued by any lower court, it cannot be served outside the county unless it bears the endorsement of a magistrate of the county of service that "This warrant may be served in the county of. . .," such endorsement to be predicated upon a certificate of the clerk of the county of origin as the official character of the issuing magistrate. (818 P.C.) 

65. Q. On what grounds are search warrants issued? 

A. 1. When the property is stolen or embezzled. 

2. When it was used as a means of committing a felony. 

3. When it is in possession of a person who intends to use it to commit a public offense; or in possession of a person to whom it has been delivered to prevent its discovery. 

66. Q. Has an officer any authority under a search warrant illegally secured or improper or insufficient in form? 

A. None whatever; he would be guilty of trespass in attempting to execute it. 

67. Q. What constitutes a search warrant legal in form?

A. It must show upon its face definitely the person or place to be searched anu the property to be searched for by specific description, and must be based upon a sworn complaint or affidavit which likewise must specifically describe the person or place and the property. 

68. Q. Does a search warrant outlaw? 

A. Yes, in ten days after date of issue. 

69. Q. May it be served at night?

A. Not unless the affidavits were positive and the magistrate so directed. 

70. Q. How is it served?

A. By the officer to whom directed or a person present and acting in his aid, by demanding admittance and stating his authority, breaking down doors or windows if admittance is then refused, seizing the property described, giving an itemized receipt therefor to the person from whom taken, and delivering the property to the magistrate for disposition in accordance with Sections 1530 to 1541, inclusive, of the Penal 

Code.

71. Q. Is a telegraphic copy of a warrant of arrest valid for service? 

A. Yes, it is just as effectual as the original warrant, provided telegraphic service has been authorized and endorsed by a Supreme Court justice or Superior Court judge, and provided a copy of the warrant and endorsement, certified as correct, have been filed with the sending telegraph office. 

72. Q. What should be an officer's foremost objective in conducting a criminal investigation and in his search for evidence?

A. To secure such conclusive evidence of guilt and identity that a confession or admission by the accused is not essential to a conviction. 

73. Q. To accomplish this objective, what general principles would you apply in a homicide investigation, for example?

A. 1. Keep the scene of the homicide intact until every possible fact and detail has been observed and recorded which can be observed without moving any object. This means that until then neither the body nor any object should be touched or moved, and that all persons except the officers detailed to the investigation should be excluded from the premises and material surroundings. 

2. Look for, develop and photograph all possible fingerprints. 

3. Take photographs, including a general view of the scene, a closeup of the body in the position in which it was found, and if possible, a picture showing the physical injuries. 

4. Record important measurements, including the size of the room, position of body, and location of furniture, or exact position of weapons, if any. 

5. Examine the scene and record its details minutely, even those which seem unimportant on the surface, bearing in mind that those details which would seem trivial to an ordinary observer, or would escape his notice, may provide some clew to the assailant's identity. 

6. Question, and record the names and addresses, of all persons at the scene, recording any information given as to other persons observed at or near the scene who are no longer there on your arrival. 

7. Carefully preserve and mark all exhibits for positive identification, later on, at the trial. Seal in envelopes those exhibits which cannot be directly marked without interfering with their use as evidence, writing the date, place and circumstances on the envelope and using a distinctive mark for each item, so that it cannot be confused with other

exhibits in the same investigation or with exhibits in any other investigation. 

8. Acquaint your fellow officers with every fact observed or step taken by yourself, so that the investigation may benefit by the combined and co-ordinanted efforts of the group. 

74. Q. What courts in Los Angeles County have jurisdiction in criminal matters, in felonies and misdemeanors? 

A. The Superior Court has jurisdiction in felonies and the Municipal, Justices or other inferior courts have jurisdiction in misdemeanors, except in the case of misdemeanors covered by the Juvenile Court Act, of which latter the Superior Court has jurisdiction. 

75. Q. The Code states that on making an arrest the officer shall take the prisoner before the nearest magistrate. What is a magistrate?

A. Magistrates are officers having power to issue warrants for arrest, and include Justices of the Supreme Court and District Court of Appeals, Judges of the Superior, Municipal and Justices Courts, and Police Magistrates in towns or cities. (P.C. 807, 808 and 818) 

76. Q. What are the four methods by which a defendant may be brought to trial for unlawful acts? 

A. 1. By COMPLAINT, filed with a magistrate. 

2. By an INFORMATION, filed by the District Attorney in the Superior Court. 

3. By a GRAND JURY INDICTMENT. 

4. By accusation or information to remove a public officer.

77. Q. What is the Grand Jury? 

A. The Grand Jury is composed of nineteen persons, impaneled and sworn to inquire into all public offenses committed or triable within the county. Its deliberations are secret, unauthorized persons are excluded, and it has no power to employ investigators. This body hears charges, examines witnesses and makes accusations against persons by indictment. 

78. Q. What method would you use to bring a defendant to trial in 

Justice, Police or Municipal Court?

A. File a COMPLAINT with the magistrate of such court.

79. Q. Give a definition of a complaint. 

A. The complaint is the allegation in writing made to a court or magistrate charging that a person has committed some designated offense. (P.C. 806, 811). It resembles an affidavit, and is intended as the basis for a warrant of arrest, and should clearly set forth the commission of the crime. 

80. Q. What is the name of the proceeding wherein defendant is first brought before the court following his arrest? 

A. The ARRAIGNMENT. 

81. Q. What is the purpose of this proceeding?

A. To inform the defendant of his constitutional rights, the charge against him, and to enter his plea, set the case for a hearing. 

82. Q. How soon after arrest must a prisoner be arraigned?

A. Forthwith, if possible, and before the judge who issued the warrant or the nearest and most accessible magistrate in the county. If immediate arraignment is impossible, it must take place, if any event, within TWO DAYS after the arrest. 

83. Q. What are you guilty of if you do not arraign him in this length of time?

A. Misdemeanor, under Sec. 145 Penal Code. 

84. Q. What happens to the prisoner if you arrest him on a FELONY charge and he pleads guilty at the arraignment? 

A. Under the new constitutional amendment (Article 1, Sec. 8) 

which now permits such pleading at the arraignment, he is 

ORDERED TO APPEAR IN SUPERIOR COURT IN NOT LESS THAN TWO NOR MORE THAN FIVE DAYS for sentence or probation. 

85. Q. What was the purpose of this amendment, permitting plea of guilty to felony charge at the arraignment? 

A. To eliminate the expense and delay of a PRELIMINARY HEARING between arraignment and trial. 

86. Q. What happens to the prisoner if you arrest him on a MIS 

DEMEANOR charge and he pleads guilty at the arraignment? 

A. Defendant is sentenced or he may apply for probation. 

87. Q. What happens to the prisoner if you arrest him on a MIS 

DEMEANOR charge and he pleads NOT GUILTY at the arraignment?

A. A date is set for TRIAL WITHIN 30 DAYS OF THE DATE 

COMPLAINT was filed, and pending this trial date, he is allowed his freedom on bail. 

88. Q. What happens to the prisoner if you arrest him on a FELONY charge and he does not plead guilty at the arraignment? 

A. A PRELIMINARY EXAMINATION is set for NOT LESS THAN TWO NOR MORE THAN FIVE DAYS thereafter. 

89. Q. What is the purpose of the PRELIMINARY EXAMINA- 

TION?

A. To determine (1) whether a FELONY HAS IN FACT been committed; and (2) if so, whether there is PROBABLE

CAUSE TO HOLD THE DEFENDANT TO TRIAL for the offense. 

90. Q. If, at preliminary examination, defendant is held for trial in

Superior Court, what is the next proceeding? 

A. Filing of the INFORMATION in Superior Court by the District Attorney. 

91. Q. How many days after the preliminary examination does he have in which to file the information? 

A. 15 days. 

92. Q. After that, how soon must the case come to trial?

A. Within 60 DAYS after arraigning defendant on the INFOR- 

MATION. 

93. Q. What is the order in which a TRIAL is conducted? (As a potential bailiff, the examiners may expect you to know this). 

A. 1. Jury is impaneled and sworn; 

2. District Attorney presents the People's evidence (some times presented by the justice or judge in justice or police courts); 

3. Defendant presents his evidence; 

4. Each side presents its rebuttal evidence. (Dist. Atty. has opening argument to jury, followed by defendant's rebuttal, then final argument by District Attorney). 

5. Judge instructs jury, who retire, and return with their verdict, whereupon the judge sets a date for sentence or hearing on probation application.

94. Q. May probation be granted in all cases?

A. No, the judge is restrained from granting probation; 

1. In certain serious felonies, where criminal was armed with concealed weapon at time of committing the crime, or with a concealed deadly weapon at time of arrest; 

2. When the crime was perpetrated with severe bodily injuries; 

3. When the criminal is a PEACE OFFICER OR PUBLIC OFFICIAL, and the crime involved EXTORTION, BRIB- ERY, OR EMBEZZLEMENT. 

4. When defendant has suffered a prior felony conviction. 

95. Q. How does the legal owner regain possession of personal property thirty days or more after it has been used as evidence in Superior Court?

A. By applying to the clerk of the Court and presenting proof that he is the legal owner and entitled to possession of the property. The clerk will issue an order, the Judge will sign the order; this order will then be presented to the Deputy 

Clerk in the County Clerk's Office, who has charge of such matters. The property will then be delivered to the owner a person entitled to possession and a receipt taken by the clerk. 

96. Q. What is a peace officer's duty with respect to giving a receipt for money or property taken from a prisoner at the time of his arrest?

A. Receipt must be issued in duplicate, itemizing and describing the money or property taken,-one copy to be delivered to defendant and the other filed with the Clerk of the court where defendant is to be tried. 

97. Q. You have been told that your duty upon making an arrest is to take the defendant forthwith before the nearest or most accessible magistrate in the county. This is, in part, to afford the defendant opportunity to be admitted to bail. Must you fulfill this same requirement if you arrest a defendant in LOS 

ANGELES COUNTY or on a felony warrant issued in SAN 

DIEGO COUNTY, or some other county?

A. No, the prisoner is not entitled to be admitted to bail before a magistrate of the county where he is located if the charge against him is a felony where the arrest is on a warrant issued on complaint before a magistrate of another county, but if arrest is on a bench warrant based on an indictment defendant

may be released on bail by a Superior Court judge of the county in which he is arrested. (821 P.C.) 

98. Q. What is a bench warrant? 

A. A process issued by the court itself (or from the bench) for the attachment or arrest of a person for a contempt of court or on indictment, etc. (See question 101.) 

99. Q. How does it differ from a warrant of arrest?

A. It is issued on the judge's own initiative, and not upon a com-. plaint filed with him. The procedure of examining informant or witnesses on oath before issuing a bench warrant is entirely eliminated. 

100. Q. Has an officer the same powers and duties with respect to a bench warrant as with respect to a warrant of arrest? 

A. Yes. 

101. Q. In what instances are bench warrants commonly issued?

A. 1. Disobedience of a subpoena by a witness, or of a summons by a juror; 

2. Failure of a defendant to pay a fine imposed within time allowed; 

3. Failure of a defendant to appear for hearing after having been released on bail. 

4. Failure of a defendant to obey any lawful order of the court; 

5. To bring in a defendant who is under indictment or information. 

102. Q. What is extradition?

A. The process by which a fugitive, charged with crime, is returned for prosecution from the state or country to which he has fled to the state or country from which he is a fugitive. 

Extradition is either international, and governed by treaties or international diplomacy, or interstate, in which case it is a matter of obligation under our constitution and not a matter of treaty or diplomacy. When the transaction is between two of the United States the proceeding is also called rendition. 

103. Q. If the Constitution makes rendition of fugitives obligatory, why is it that states are sometimes unsuccessful in obtaining the extradition of their fugitives from another state?

A. Because, in the revised statutes of the U. S. (Sec. 5278), the wording is less stringent, read in part: "-it shall be the 

DUTY of the executive authority of the state or territory to which such person has fled to cause him to be arrested."and to deliver him to the authorities of the demanding state. 

This revision substitutes DUTY for OBLIGATION, and there is no provision for the enforcement of such duty. Hence, the governor of the state of refuge may exercise his own discretion, and often does. 

104. Q. In interstate extradition, must a person be tried for the exact crime named in the extradition papers? 

A. No, upon his return, he can be tried for any other crime. He can be extradited for murder, for example, and then tried for robbery. 

105. Q. Is this true of international extradition?

A. No. The charge cannot be changed after a prisoner is brought back from a foreign country, nor can the prisoner be tried on any charge except the one issued in the extradition papers until he has had a reasonable time to leave the jurisdiction. 

106. Q. Enumerate the steps taken in interstate extradition. 

A. 1. Filing of COMPLAINT or finding of Grand Jury INDICT- 

MENT in county where crime was committed.

2. Issuance of WARRANT OF ARREST; 

3. Arrest of defendant in the jurisdiction where he is found. (This step may either precede or follow steps 1 and 2.) 

4. Preparation by District Attorney of APPLICATION FOR REQUISITION. The APPLICATION is made to the Gov ernor of California, who, if he honors it, makes the REQUI- SITION upon the governor of the state in question for the arrest and rendition of the person named. The application must be accompanied by: (a) Complaint; (b) warrant of arrest; (c) affidavit of complaint; (d) affidavit of officer who negotiated the arrest; (e) certification and exemplification of the foregoing by the County Clerk; (f) statement of District Attorney and designation of person to act as state agent to bring the prisoner back. 

5. The nominated State Agent takes three sets of the foregoing papers to the governor together with Requisition and State Agent's appointment for the Governor's signature. 

Two sets of the papers are returned to the State Agent with his Appointment and the Requisition.

6. Delivery of Requisition, by State Agent, to Governor of 

Asylum state (state of refuge). State Agent keeps one set of the papers upon which to make his return. 

7. Warrant of arrest upon Requisition issued by Governor of 

Asylum state. 

8. Officer delivers fugitive to State Agent. 

9. State Agent returns fugitive to the County Jail of this county, secures a receipt for the fugitive from the jailer, and files his Agent's Appointment (or Commission) with the District Attorney, with a return made thereon to the effect that as commanded, he proceeded to the state in question, received the body of the defendant from the officer having him in custody, brought him to this state and delivered him to the Sheriff of Los Angeles County. 

107. Q. Who, besides a magistrate, may issue a warrant of arrest?

A. The Coroner, in case of homicide under his jurisdiction, if the 

Coroner's jury at the inquest ascertain the identity of the person committing the act and he is not already in custody. 

In such circumstances, under Sec. 1517 P. C., the Coroner 

MUST issue a warrant. The warrant is however, returnable before the nearest and most accessible magistrate, not to the Coroner. 

108. Q. Is it served in the same way as an ordinary warrant of arrest? 

A. Yes, with the one exception that if served outside the county it need not be endorsed by a magistrate of the outside county. 

109. Q. Who summons the jurors for the Superior Court and upon what authority?

A. The Sheriff, upon a WRIT OF VENIRE. 

110. Q. What is meant by polling the jury?

A. Asking each juror if the verdict as returned is his or her individual verdict. 

111. Q. What is a crime?

A. A crime or public offense is an act committed or omitted in violation of a law forbidding or commanding it, and to which is annexed, upon conviction, either of the following punishments: 

1. Death; 

2. Imprisonment; 

3. Fine; 

4. Removal from office;

22 5. Disqualification to hold and enjoy any office of honor, trust or profit in this state (P. C.-15). The prohibited act is not necessarily a crime unless the statute provides a punishment. 

112. Q. Are there any common law crimes in California?

A. No. An act of omission or commission in California is not criminal unless prescribed by the Penal Code or other statute, ordinance or municipal, township or county regulations. 

113. Q. Who are the parties to a crime?

A. PRINCIPALS and ACCESSORIES. Principals are all per sons concerned in the commission of a crime, whether felony or misdemeanor, and whether they knowingly and with criminal intent directly commit the act or aid, abet, advise or encourage or compel its commission, whether present or not. (P. C.-31). An accessory before the fact is a principal. In 

California there are no accessories before the fact. Accessories after the fact are those who, after a felony has been committed, intentionally aid the principal to escape arrest, trial, conviction or punishment, having knowledge that he has committed or has been charged with such felony. 

114. Q. What is meant by corpus delicti, and must it be proved in every criminal prosecution?

A. In every prosecution for crime, it is necessary to establish the corpus delicti, i.e., the body or ELEMENTS OF THE 

CRIME. These are, broadly, the composite of all the facts necessary to constitute the particular crime. The corpus delicti is the proof that a crime has been committed. It must be distinguished from establishing the ultimate guilt of the defendant. The identity of the perpetrator is not a part of the corpus delicti. 

115. Q. Is intent a necessary element of every crime, and can you differentiate between specific intent and general intent?

A. In every crime or public offense there must exist a union or joint operation of action or criminal negligence and intent. (P. C.-20). There is a large class of crimes which do not require specific intent, and the defendant may have been entirely innocent of intentional wrong, and would not have done the act had he known it to be wrongful, yet he is guilty of the crime. All that is necessary is that he did the act intentionally. In other crimes there must be a specific intent, that is, a particular intent to accomplish a particular object

or purpose, and since this is an essential element of the crime, if the specific intent or particular state of mind is missing, there can be no crime. 

116. Q. Can you give an example of general intent?

A. A man believing himself to be legally divorced, though he actually is not, marries for the second time. His act of marrying was intentional, but his act in committing the crime of bigamy, of which he is now guilty, is not intentional. A person who knowingly acts is acting with intent. 

117. Q. Can you give an example of specific intent?

A. The State has designated a particular state of mind or intent as a necessary element in burglary, by specifying the entering "with intent to commit theft or a felony." If entrance is made for any other reason, the crime would not be burglary. 

118. Q. What is the meaning of transferred intent? 

A. Where the defendant shoots at "A" with intent to kill him, and by accident kills an innocent bystander, whom he had no intent to harm, he will be guilty of murder, and the intent will be deemed to have been transferred. 

119. Q. What is meant by an attempt to commit a crime?

A. An attempt consists of a specific intent to commit a particular crime, and a direct, voluntary act done toward but falling short of its commission. The act must go further than mere preparation; it must be such as would ordinarily result in the crime, except for the interruption. 

120. Q. Is it possible to actually attempt a crime and still not be guilty of attempted crime?

A. Yes; where the intended result is legally impossible because one of the elements of the corpus dlicti of the crime intended does not exist. 

121. Q. What is conspiracy?

A. Broadly, an agreement between two or more persons for the purpose of committing an unlawful act, and take any step, even though a legal act, toward its commission by either or both of the persons, they are guilty of the crime of conspiracy. 

Such step is known as the overt act. 

122. Q. Are all conspirators equally liable for the acts of each other?

A. Yes, provided such acts are done in pursuance of the object of the conspiracy.

HIT BY A TRAIN TWO KILLED 

Slogans may help to cut the death rate, but common sense could do a lot more if used by drivers. (Turn to page 184 and answer questions.)

25 

123. Q. "A", "B" and "C" plan the robbery of a bank. "C" alone commits the robbery, and unintentionally and because of nervousness, fires a gun that he did not know to be loaded, and kills a bank employee. In planning the robbery, no shooting had been contemplated by A, B, and C. For what crimes is each of them guilty?

A. Each is identically liable for murder, robbery and conspiracy to rob, because each of these crimes was in pursuance of the object of and the natural result of the conspiracy. 

124. Q. Is it a crime in Calfiornia to solicit another to commit a crime?

A. Yes. Under P. C.-653f it is a crime to solicit another to offer or accept a bribe, or to solicit the commission of certain of the major felonies. 

125. Q. Are any persons or classes of persons legally incapable of committing crimes?

A. Yes; infants under fourteen and idiots are incapable of com mitting crimes. See Section 26 Penal Code. Read the eight subdivisions. 

126. Q. Are married women always liable for their crime?

A. Yes, except when acting under the coercion of their husbands, except for felonies; and there can never be a crime of conspiracy between husband and wife alone. 

127. Q. Can corporations ever be held criminally liable for their acts? 

A. Yes, but the punishment must be restricted to fine, and not imprisonment. 

128. Q. If the victim of an offense has consented to the act, will this be a bar to criminal prosecution?

A. Consent is no defense, except in certain crimes where lack of consent is an essential element of the crime, as for instance, in rape, which is carnal knowledge of a woman WITHOUT HER CONSENT; but consent must be voluntary, and the victim must be legally capable of consenting. 

129. Q. What is Homicide? 

A. The killing of a human by a human being. (P. C.-187). 

130. Q. What kinds of homicide are there? 

A. 1. Murder. (P. C.-187). 

2. Manslaughter. (P. C.-192). 

3. Justifiable homicide. (P. C.-196, 198). 

4. Excusable homicide. (P. C.-195). 

5. Negligent homicide. (P. C.-500)..

131. Q. What is Murder? 

A.

Murder is the unlawful killing of a human being with MAL- 

ICE AFORETHOUGHT. 

132. Q. What are the degrees of murder? (P. C.-189).

A. First and second. First degree includes all murders perpetrated by poison, lying in wait, torture, or by any other kind of wilful, deliberate and premeditated killing, or which is committed in the perpetration of arson, rape, robbery, burglary or mayhem. 

133. Q. What is second degree murder?

A. Murder in the second degree includes all other kinds of murder; it does not require premeditation, but does require malice. 

134. Q. What is Manslaughter? 

A.

Manslaughter is the unlawful killing of a human being with out malice. It is VOLUNTARY MANSLAUGHTER when the killing is upon a sudden quarrel or heat of passion, and it is INVOLUNTARY MANSLAUGHTER when it results from the commission of an unlawful act not amounting to a felony; or in the commission of a lawful act which might produce death, in an unlawful manner, or without due caution and circumspection. 

135. Q. What is Arson? 

A. Arson is the wilful and malicious setting fire to or burning of any dwelling or building belonging or adjoining thereto whether owned by the guilty party or not. There are now no degrees of the crime. (P. C. 447-a.) 

136. Q. What burning is necessary to constitute the crime of arson?

A. It is not necessary that the building or property set on fire should have been destroyed. It is sufficient that the fire applied takes destructive effect upon any part of the substance of the building or the property. 

137. Q. What is rape? (P. C.-261).

A. An act of sexual intercourse accomplished with a female not the wife of the perpetrator, under either of the following circumstances: 

1. Where the female is under 18 years of age; 

2. Where she is incapable, through lunacy or other unsoundness of mind, whether temporary or permanent, of giving legal consent;

3. Where she resists, but her resistance is overcome by force or violence; 

4. Where she is prevented from resisting by threats of great and immediate bodily harm, accompanied by apparent power of execution, or by any intoxicating narcotic or substance; 

5. Where she is, at the time, unconscious of the act, and this is known to the accused; 

6. Where she submits under the belief that the person committing the act is her husband, and this belief is induced by an artificial pretense or concealment practiced by the accused with the intent to induce such belief. 

138. Q. What is Robbery?

A. Robbery is the felonious taking of the personal property, in the presence of another, from his person or in his presence, and against his will, accomplished by force or fear. (P.C.-211). 

139. Q. Define first degree robbery. (P. C.-211a).

A. First degree robbery is that which is perpetrated by torture or by a person being armed with a dangerous or deadly weap- All other kinds of robbery are of the second degree. 

The value of the property makes no difference. on. 

140. Q. What is Burglary? (P.C. 459).

A. Every person who enters any house, room, apartment, tenement, shop, warehouse, store, mill, barn, stable, outhouse or other building, tent, vessel, railroad car, mine or any underground portion thereof, and with intent to commit grand or petty theft or any felony, is guilty of burglary. 

141. Q. What is burglary in the first degree? (P.C.-460).

A. Every burglary of an inhabited dwelling or building, committed in the night time, and every burglary, whether in the day or night time, committed by a person armed with a deadly weapon, or who, while in the commission of such burglary, assaults any person, is burglary in the first degree. All other kinds of burglary are in the second degree. 

142. Q. What is meant by "night time" in the criminal laws? (P. C.-463). 

A. The period between sunset and sunrise.

888 

 143. Q. What is Criminal Assault? (P. C. 240, 245). 

A. An unlawful attempt coupled with the PRESENT ABILITY 

to commit a violent injury on the person of another. (Thus the mere pointing of a gun or shaking of a fist can constitute assault, even though no shot is fired or blow struck). 

144. Q. What is Battery? (P. Ç.-242).

A. Any wilful and unlawful use of force or violence upon the person of another. (Thus a simple assault is the threat or preparation, and battery is the act which carries out the threat). 

145. Q. What is Assault with a Deadly Weapon? (P. C.-245). 

A. Any attempt upon the person of another with a deadly weapon or instrument, or by any means or force likely to produce great bodily injury (thus assault with a deadly weapon can be committed with an automobile, or even with the fist). 

146. Q. Define Extortion. 

A. Extortion is the obtaining of property from another, with his consent induced by the wrongful use of force or fear, or under color of official right. It is somewhat similar to robbery; the distinction is that the extortion of property is ob tained WITH THE CONSENT OF THE VICTIM. 

147. Q. What three terms have been abolished from the Penal Code, and incorporated in the single word "theft"?

A. Larceny, embezzlement and obtaining money under false pretenses. The substantive elements of each of these three crimes remain the same, and it is necessary to prove these elements when prosecuting for them, though they are all now known as theft. 

148. Q. What is Theft (larceny)? (P. C.-484).

A. The felonious stealing, taking, carrying, leading or driving away of property of another, or the fraudulent appropriation of property intrusted to him, or the obtaining of property, labor or services by fraud, misrepresentation or false pretenses. 

149. Q. What are the degree of theft? (P. C.-487).

A. 1. Grand Theft: 

(a) When the money, labor or real or personal property taken is of a value exceeding $200.00; provided that when domestic fowls are taken of a value exceeding $50.00, the same shall constitute grand theft;

2. Petty Theft (P. C.-488), which includes theft in all other cases. 

150. Q. What is the punishment for grand theft?

A. Grand theft is a felony, and is punishable by imprisonment in the State prison for not less than one year and not more than ten years. (P. C.-489). 

151. Q. What is the punishment for petty theft? (P. C.-490).

A. Petty theft is a misdemeanor, and is punishable by fine not exceeding $500.00, or by imprisonment in the county jail not exceeding six months, or both. 

151a. Q. What is Mayhem? 

A. Every person who unlawfully or maliciously deprives another person of a member of his body, or disables, disfigures or renders it useless, or cuts or disables the tongue, or puts out an eye, or splits the nose, ear or lip, is guilty of mayhem. (P. C.-203). Mayhem is punishable by imprisonment in the state prison not exceeding 14 years. (P. C.-204). 

152. Q. What is false imprisonment?

A. This is the unlawful violation of the personal liberty of another. (P. C.-236). 

153. Q. What is Negligent Homicide? 

A. Negligent homicide is a crime which was added to the Vehicle 

Code in 1935. Where any person dies within one year of injuries caused by NEGLIGENT DRIVING, or the commis sion by the driver of an unlawful act not amounting to a felony, the driver is guilty of negligent homicide, a felony (with optional county jail imprisonment). (P. C.-500). 

154. Q. What is kidnapping?

A. Any person who fraudulently or forcibly abducts or steals another person is guilty of kidnapping. It is not necessary that the person abducted be taken to some other country, state or county, but merely to some other part of the same county. If strictly interpreted, this might mean even to another room in the same house. 

Kidnapping for extortion or robbery is broadly defined to include any carrying away or holding of a person for ransom

or the committing of extortion or robbery. Where the victim is so kidnapped and suffers "bodily harm," death or life imprisonment without possibility of parole is the penalty. In other cases, the penalty is life imprisonment with the possibility of parole. 

155. Q. Define Extortion. 

A. See Penal Code Section 518. 

156. Q. Define Criminal Libel. 

A. Criminal libel is a malicious defamation, expressed either in writing, printing or by signs or pictures, or the like, tending to blacken the memory of one who is dead or to impeach the honesty, integrity, virtue or reputation, or publish the actual or alleged defects of one who is alive, and thereby expose him to public hatred, contempt or ridicule. (P. C.-248). The element of malice must be present, or the libel will be merely civil libel, and truth is not a defense unless accompanied by good motives. 

157. Q. Define Slander. 

A. Slander is practically identical to libel, with the exception that it is a defamation orally uttered, and it makes no difference whether or not it be communicated through or by radio or mechanical or other means or device whatsoever, tending to blacken the memory of one who is dead, or to impeach the honesty, integrity, virtue or reputation, or to disclose the actual or alleged defects of one who is living, or of educational, literary, social or fraternal or benevolent corporation, association or organization, and thereby expose him or it to public hatred, contempt or ridicule. 

158. Q. Give the definition of a Riot. 

A. A riot is an unauthorized use of force or violence to disturb the public peace, or the threat to use force, coupled with the immediate power of execution, by two or more persons acting together. (P. C.-404). 

159. Q. What is the duty of a peace officer in case of a riot or impending riot? 

A. Where any number of persons, whether armed or not, are unlawfully or riotiously assembled, the Deputy Sheriff must go among the persons assembled or as near to them as possible, and command them, in the name of the people of the State, to immediately disperse. If the persons who are assem-

bled do not immediately disperse, such officer or officers must arrest them, and to that end may command the aid of all persons present or within the county. 

160. Q. What is meant by Lynching?

A. Lynching is defined as the taking, by means of a riot, of any person from the lawful custody of any peace officer. (P. C.- 405a). 

161. Q. Give a practical definition of Forgery. Is it a felony or a misdemeanor?

A. The Penal Code definition of forgery is exceedingly detailed, but in brief the crime consists of the false making, altering or counterfeiting or signing the name of another person (or a fictitious person) to, any document capable of affecting the rights or liabilities of any person, or an attempt to use any such altered or false document with the intent to defraud. 

Forgery is a felony, but P. C.-473 permits an alternative jail sentence. 

162. Q. What is meant by issuing checks to defraud?

A. This type of check is one issued willfully with intent to defraud, and is drawn upon a bank, person or firm for the payment of money by the maker who knows at the time that he has not sufficient funds or credit on deposit. 

P. C.-476a). This is a felony but may carry a county jail sentence. It must be observed that if a POST-DATED

CHECK is given, and the maker informes the payee of the fact that there are insufficient funds at the time, no crime has been committed. 

163. Q. Would a so-called "fictitious check" (not merely a check without funds) which bore a wholly fictitious name, come under the definition of forgeries, as stated above? 

A. Yes, since it involves a forgery of a fictitious name. 

164. Q. Is it a crime to give a post-dated check in payment for credit, goods or services if the check, upon being presented on date in question, is returned because of insufficient funds? 

A. Not if the person who receives it is informed that it was post-dated, for in this case it merely has the effect of a promissory note.

165. Q. As a matter of ACTUAL PRACTICE in this county, would you expect to be authorized to make an arrest for an insufficient check of from $5.00 to $45.00?

A. Yes, the crime is either check no funds, regardless of the amount, or petty theft. In either event an arrest should be made. 

166. Q. What is counterfeit money?

A. A spurious imitation of the legal, established currency or coin of the country, falsely forged to deceive and defraud. 

167. Q. What is mean by Perjury? 

A. To constitute perjury, there must be a false statement, wilfully made, of a fact material to the issue under oath before a competent tribunal, officer or person. (P. C.-118, 123). A person who willfully procures another to commit perjury is guilty of subornation of perjury, and is punishable in the same manner. (P. C.-127). 

168. Q. What is meant by the Compounding of Crimes? 

A. One is said to have compounded a crime who, having knowledge of the commission of the crime, takes money or other consideration upon an agreement to concel the crime or abstain from prosecution or withhold evidence. Compounding of crime may be either a felony or a misdemeanor, according to the circumstances. (P. C.-153). 

169. Q. What is Bribery?

A. A bribe is anything of value, including a promise, given with corrupt intent, to influence the action of any person in public or official capacity. (P. C.-7 (6)). The Code further provides that bribery includes the giving or even offering of bribes to executive officers, members of the legislature, jurors, board of supervisors, or witnesses, and the asking or receiving of bribes by such persons, or the agreeing to a bribe. 

170. Q. What is Adultery? 

A. The original rule on this crime was that each party must be married to another, and that they must live together in a state of cohabitation in adultery. The present rule, however, under C. C.-93 states that adultery is the voluntary sexual intercourse of a married person with a person other than offender's husband or wife, and P. C.269a states that the crime is a misdemeanor on the part of the married person. 

Thus, the unmarried party has committed no crime.

Accident Investigation Is a Real Science 

Many cases of criminal negligence have been passed by.

(See page 40) When investigating such an accident as pictured here a much different answer might be found by using present-day laboratory tests and the skid-speed chart.

171. Q. What is Abortion? 

A. The crime consists of administering to, or providing a pregnant woman with any instrument or other means with intent to procure her miscarriage, UNLESS THIS IS NECESSARY 

TO PRESERVE HER LIFE. (P. C.-274). A woman who so licits or submits to the attempt is also punishable under a separate Penal Code Section. (P. C.-275). 

172. Q. What is Bigamy? 

A. Every person having a husband or wife living who marries another person, is guilty, unless the other spouse was absent for five years and not known to be living or the previous marriage was void, annulled or dissolved. (P. C.-281, 282).

The honest belief of the invalidity of a previous marriage or of the death of the other spouse, except as above specified, will not act as a defense against bigamy. 

173. Q. What is Vagrancy? 

A. Persons without visible means of living, who have the ability to work and who do not seek or accept employment, beggars, lewd or disolute persons, common prostitutes and drunkards, drug addicts, etc., are vagrants, and are punishable by fine, or imprisonment, or both. Incomplete-see Code Section for further details. 

174. Q. What is a Habitual Criminal? 

A. One who has twice been convicted on separate occasions, in this state or elsewhere, and regardless of whether he had served a sentence or a probation after either conviction, of one or more of the following crimes, which include the major felonies: 1. Robbery; 2. Burglary; 3. Burglary with explosives; 4. Rape with force or violence; 5. Arson; 6. Murder, 7. Assault with intent to commit murder; 8. Grand theft; 

9. Bribery; 10. Perjury; 11. Subornation of perjury; 12. Train wrecking; 13. Receiving stolen goods; 14. Assault with a deadly weapon; 15. Extortion; 16. Kidnapping; 17. Mayhem; 

18. Escape from a State Prison; 19. Forgery; 20. Conspiracy to commit any one or more of the aforementioned felonies; or one who has THREE times been convicted of such FEL- 

ONY, and is thereafter convicted of a fourth felony. 

174a. Q. What is the penalty for habitual criminals? 

A. Imprisonment for life with no possibility of parole until twelve years have been served; upon the third conviction; imprisonment for life with NO possibility of parole, after three such prior convictions.

175. Q. What are "repeaters," as distinguished from habitual criminals? 

A. 1. Those convicted of petty theft with "prior", i. e., two separate convictions for petty theft, the penalty not to exceed five years in the State prison; 

2. Those convicted of felony with prior petty theft; penalty (a) not to exceed 10 years, if felony was one punishable by 5 years or less, (b) not less than 10 years if the felony was one punishable by more than 5 years; 

3. Petty theft with prior felony; penalty not to exceed 5 years. (Same penalty as p.t. with prior p.t.). 

4. Felony with prior felony; penalty, the maximum sentence provided for the second felony; thus if the second felony was forgery, which carries penalty of one to fourteen years, the penalty for forgery with a prior felony would be a definite term of 14 years. 

176. Q. What is the difference between (4) above, felony with prior felony, and habitual criminal? Why a maximum sentence of less than life?

A. Because felony with prior felony is penalty imposed upon a 

SECOND conviction, whereas the habitual criminal penalties are penalties imposed upon THIRD and FOURTH convictions. 

177. Q. What is Parole? 

A. To release a prisoner from prison after serving not less than his minimum term, and before the expiration of his sentence. 

Parole is determined by the Parole Board for county jail prisoners, by Board of Prison Terms and Paroles for State Penitentiary prisoners. 

178. Q. What is Probation? 

A.

The release of a person convicted of crime, before or after imposing sentence, BUT BEFORE AND WITHOUT THE SERVING OF ANY PART OF THE SENTENCE? 

179. Q. What is Bail?

A. Security given by a person charged with crime as a guarantee of his appearance in court. 

180. Q. In what form may bail be given? 

A. 1. Property bond; 

2. Bail bond furnished by a surety; 

3. Cash; 

4. U. S. Bonds, State of California Bonds.

181. Q. What is a Surety? 

A. One who bonds himself to forfeit the amount of bail specified in the event of defendant's non-appearance in court at the time agreed upon. Bail bonding companies or executors of property bonds are sureties. 

182. Q. Are all crimes bailable?

A. Yes, except that where the offense is one punishable by death, bail is not accepted if the presumption of guilt is great. 

183. Q. What is a Confession as distinguished from an Admission? 

A. A confession is an acknowledgement of guilt of the offense charged, while an admission is any statement which has a tendency to prove his guilt, but does not admit the ultimate fact of guilt of the offense charged. 

184. Q. What is the importance of this distinction?

A. Confessions, to be admissable in evidence, must be free and voluntary; whereas admissions are admissable whether free and voluntary or not. 

185. Q. As a peace officer, what precautions should you take in obtaining the dying declaration of the victim of a homicide? 

A. In order to assure the admissibility of such statement in evidence, it is necessary to question the victim in such a way as to bring out clearly in his answer the belief on his part that he is about to die. If this is done, the statement is then admissable, even if the victim recovers. If possible, such statement should be signed by several creditable witness. 

186. Q. What is direct evidence? What is circumstantial evidence?

A. Direct evidence is testimony of an eye witness to an occurrence; all other evidence is circumstantial. 

187. Q. What is the distinction between "court" and "judge”?

A. A judge is an individual who presides over a judicial tribunal. "The Court" is a term that is often used interchangeably with "the judge," and sometimes it becomes necessary to distinguish between the two. A court is a tribunal presided over by one or more judges for the exercise of such judicial power as has been conferred upon it by law. 

188. Q. There are certain courts in California established by constitution. Name these. (C. P. C.-33).

A. Senate, as court of impeachment; 

2. The Supreme Court; 

3. The District Court of Appeal; 

4. The Superior Court; 

5. The Municipal Courts. 

189. Q. What are the "Courts of Record"?

A. These are the Supreme Court, District Court of Appeal, Superior Court, Municipal Court, and also the Court of Impeachment. (C. P. C.-36). All other courts are courts not of record. Courts of record have SEALS. 

190. Q. What are the Inferior Courts? 

A. The inferior courts are: 

1. The Justices Courts; 

2. The Police Courts; 

3. The Recorder's Courts. 

These are courts of special and limited jurisdiction.

191. Q. What is the jurisdiction of the Supreme Court? 

A. It has original jurisdiction over certain writs, such as mandamus, certiorari, prohibition, habeas corpus, and others necessary to the exercise of the appellate jurisdiction. It passes, also on matters in law, equity and crime appealed to it from the Superior or District Courts of Appeal. It is the highest court in the State. It is the only court in the State hearing appeals in death penalty cases. 

192. Q. What is the jurisdiction of the District Courts of Appeal? 

A. The original jurisdiction of the District Courts of Appeal are equal with that of the Supreme Court, and are concurrent. 

It does not act on appeals in equity cases unless they are transferred to it from the Supreme Court. It acts on appeals in law cases in which the Supreme Court has original jurisdiction, except where appeal is directly to the Supreme Court, and in certain other cases. It acts on appeals from the Superior Court in matters of crime on questions of law alone in criminal cases prosecuted by indictment or information, except in the case where the judgment of death has been rendered. It acts on ALL matters transferred to it by the Supreme Court.

39 

193. Q. What is the jurisdiction of the Superior Court? 

A. The Superior Court acts on and tries all law and equity cases, except where special jurisdiction is given to Municipal, Justices or other inferior courts. It has no concurrent jurisdiction with any other court, and where a Municipal or Justices court has jurisdiction over a case, the Superior Court has none. The Superior Court has jurisdiction over all felons, and also over all misdemeanors not otherwise provided for. 

There is a special department of the Superior Court known as the Appellate Department, which hears appeals in cases arising from the Municipal, Justices and inferior courts. It also has jurisdiction of proceedings under the Juvenile Court, and in counties where there is no Municipal or Justices Court, it has jurisdiction over all misdemeanors. 

194. Q. What is the jurisdiction of the Municipal Courts? 

A. Municipal Courts may be established in any city where a population of over 40,000 people. They are courts of record, and have jurisdiction in cases at law where the amount, exclusive of interest, is not in excess of $2000.00. In all criminal cases they have exclusive jurisdiction over all misdemeanors committed within the city, except those in which the Juvenile Court has jurisdiction. They have concurrent jurisdiction with Justices Courts in all such cases where the offense is committed within the County but outside the city, except; 

1. Where the Juvenile Court has jurisdiction; 

2. Where, in cases inside a city, town or township, a Justices 

Court is given exclusive jurisdiction; 

3. Where Police, Recorder's or other city courts have exclusive jurisdiction. (P. C.-1462). 

195. Q. What is the jurisdiction of a Class A Justices Court? 

A...These courts have jurisdiction in cases at law, and in all matters where the value of the property, exclusive of interest, does not exced $1000.00, and they have jurisdiction in criminal matters in misdemeanors, exclusive of jurisdiction of Municipal Court established within the county, except cases within the jurisdiction of a Juvenile Court. (P. C.-1415). 

196. Q. What is the jurisdiction of a Class B. Justices Court? 

A. These courts are the same as the Class A Courts, except that the value is set at $300.00, and the crimes are misdemeanors punishable by fine not exceeding $1,000.00 or imprisonment not exceeding six months, or both.
