TEST NUMBER 10 PENAL CODE 

Only one of the following problems in this group is correct. Mark the correct one.

B intends to burglarize C's house. By mistake he enters D's house which is occupied solely by B. 

1. B's guilty intent will be transferred in law to make his entry of 

D's house an unlawful entry. 

X enters D's store to make a purchase. Just as X steps in the door he sees a watch lying on the counter and then decides to steal it. 

2. With the proof of intent X should be prosecuted for burglary. 

R is driving an automobile carefully. On a steep grade the brake suddenly gives way and the car races down the incline. At the bottom of the grade R sees a group of children playing to his right, and to the left is a man. To avoid striking the children, R steers the car to the left striking the man, killing him. The man proves to be E who is R's mortal enemy. R did not recognize him until after striking him, R said "I'm glad I killed E." 

3. This makes R guilty of manslaughter. L, intending to kill M, puts a loaded revolver in his overcoat pocket. 

O, for a joke substitutes a pop-gun. L on meeting M, pulls the pop-gun and aims it at M. 

4. L is guilty of assault. 2 A, a minor 9 years old, is charged with a felony crime. 

1 The State will have to establish beyond a reasonable doubt that he knew the wrongfulness of the act with which he is charged in order to hold A criminally responsible. 

2. Children under 14 cannot be held criminally responsible. 

3. Children under 14 can be held criminally responsible for misdemeanors but not felonies. 

4. Under the Common Law, A would be conclusively presumed incapable of committing crime, but not in California.

TEST NUMBER 14 PENAL CODE 

1 P, a police officer, detailed in guarding a fence surrounding a stadium where a pageant is in progress, is ordered by L, his superior officer, to use his revolver in repelling a group of men who are climbing over the fence. P does so and X, one of the men, is killed. 

1. Acting in obedience to orders of his superior will be a good defense for P. 

2. The fact that P acted in obedience to his superior's orders could not be considered in evidence for any purpose. 

3. P is probably guilty of manslaughter. 

4. Excusable homicide. 2 B went moonlight swimming in the nude and is arrested under an old ordinance that makes such an act a misdemeanor. B defends on the ground that the ordinance had not been enforced for fifty years and that custom and usage has sanctioned nude bathing at night. 

1. This is not a good defense here. 

2. Lack of intent here will be a good defense. 

3. Although B had no intent to violate the law, his intent to do the prohibited act is the only intent necessary to be established. The law, however, has been abandoned by long disuse and general custom and usage will be a good defense. 

4. General custom and usage is never a defense and cannot be considered for any purpose. 3 X, a boy 6 years old, pointed a gun at Y without provocation and fired. 

1. On account of X's age, no criminal proceedings may ever be held. 

2. On arriving at the age of 14, X may be tried in a criminal proceeding. 

3. Although unusual, under certain circumstances X may be held criminally liable. 

4. The law presumes X conclusively incapable of a guilty intent.

In order to elicit sympathy and thus to collect alms by begging, B solicits X to cut off B's hand; X does so. B dies from loss of blood. 

1. As B consented, X is not liable. 

2. This is involuntary manslaughter. 

3. This is murder. 

4. This is excusable homicide. 

Answers to Test No. 14 Question No. 1-The 3rd statement is correct. 

Question No. 2-The 1st statement is correct.

Question No. 3-The 3rd statement is correct. 

Question No. 4-The 3rd statement is correct.

TEST NUMBER 15 PENAL CODE 

1 W, desiring a divorce from her husband H, engaged the services of D, a detective, to get evidence. Instead D went to H and told H of 

W's plans. D suggested to H that they frame evidence against 

W. Not trusting D and fearing blackmail, H appeared to agree to 

D's suggestion. H secretely communicated with the District Attorney. H, then in furtherance of the "plan" with D, impersonating the voice of X, the supposed "boy friend" of W, telephoned W to make a date. D and H are arrested for conspiracy to prepare false evidence. 

1. A sufficient overt act was committed by H in furtherance of the consipracy. 

2. No sufficient overt act was committed and therefore the conspiracy charge must fail. 

3. No successful prosecution for conspiracy can be had. 

4. D and H can be convicted of conspiracy under this evidence. 2 A intending to rape F, throws her to the ground, tears her clothes and by physical force is overcoming her resistance, when seized by remorse, A voluntarily abandons his purpose. 

1. He having repented, A has not committed a crime. 

2. This constitutes an attempt. 

3. This is only an attempt to commit an attempt. 

4. A straight act of preparation in furtherance of a guilty intent is sufficient, so A is guilty of an attempt. A, B and C agree to steal an auto. A waits around the corner as a look-out; B tells C how to back the car down a driveway as C starts it. P, and O, officers, arrest all three at this point. 

1. This evidence would support a charge of conspiracy but not an attempt. 

2. This evidence would support a charge of an attempt but not conspiracy. 

3. B and C can be charged as principals, but not A. 

4. There are no accessories before the fact in California.

TEST NUMBER 20 PENAL CODE 

1. TF (460-269b) Testimony shows Mrs. M was a licentious, disolute woman. K, a married man, knowing B, who was M's husband, was out for the evening with friends at a beach resort, was found in M's bedroom in a position tending to show an improper intimacy. K admits unlawful entry to do what he was doing. The Desk Sergeant properly booked him first degree burglary. 

2. T F (532) R sent a merchandise order to Z, accompanied by one-half a $10 bill, promising to mail the other half of the bill on receipt of the goods R did not pick up. Book R for forgery. 

3. T F (26) H was a maker of counterfeit coins during the night time, while W, his wife who feared him, passed them out where she was employed as a waitress. The presumption of 

H's coercion of W will hold her innocent. 

4. T F (22) X, being intoxicated, stabbed Y. It's proper that the jury when deciding whether X acted under a bona-fide apprehension that his person was about to be attacked, should take into consideration the fact that the accused was intoxicated. 

5. T F (101) X rescued C, who had been convicted of a capital offense. Y attempted to free R, a rapist; Z sought to set B free (B was charged with abortion); Q tried to free N, charged with a misdemeanor. All defendants were found not guilty except X, and his punishment would be according to law if he received a fine and jail sentence. 

6. TF (270c) All children, no matter how indigent themselves, must support their indigent parents. 

7. TF (161) A child told his teacher: "My daddy was just convicted on a champerty charge." California has no such crime. 

8. TF (159) D was a young fellow charged with common barratry. 

Even if he is older than he appears, and is really an adult, three instances of unlawful acts must be shown to secure conviction.

TEST NUMBER 21 PENAL CODE 

1. T F (150) K was a high school boy going to the store with his mother. At a corner a policeman having trouble in arresting certain rowdies, called for assistance from the bystanders. The mother smiled and refused to assist the policeman. 

That's a misdemeanor. 

2. T F (859a) If the defendant is a minor, he is not allowed to be represented by counsel if he pleads guilty. 

3. TF (865) When the accused is a minor, he may be allowed to hear witnesses, and they may be cross-examined in his behalf. 

4. T F (647a) One who continuously loiters about a school building, annoying and molesting school children may be charged with vagrancy. 

5. T F (537) Anyone who defrauds hotel proprietors, inns, etc., is classed as a felon. 

6. T F (405a) This is a correct definition of LYNCHING: "The taking by means of a a riot any person from the lawful custody of any peace officer is a lynching." 

7. T F (273g) One indulging in immoral practices in the presence of a child is chargeable with a misdemeanor. 

8. T F (225) To engage in a pre-arranged combat is considered to be a duel. 

9. T F (203) In case one intentionally and with criminal intent should disable the tongue of a minor, it is mayhem. 

10. T F (190) It is a fact that justifiable and excusable homicide carry no punishment. 

11. T F (177a) B, aged 19, took opium to his father, a prisoner confined in city jail. This is a misdemeanor. 

12. T F (109a) C, a girl 18, wilfully assisted her mother who was an inmate of the city jail, to escape. C is guilty of a misdemeanor. 

13. T F (26) A married woman commits a misdemeanor under the coercion of her eldest son (a robust 20 year old boy). Such coercion excuses her from all blame. 

14. T F (22) K is a 17 year old high school boy, who while intoxicated commits a misdemeanor. Not his age but his drunkenness will excuse him.

TEST NUMBER 22 PENAL CODE 

1. TF No minor under the age of 16 years shall be employed, permitted or suffered to work in connection with any mercantile establishment, office, laundry, place of amusement, hotel or apartment house, under any circumstances. 

2. T F With certain exceptions no minor under the age of 18 years, shall be employed more than 8 hours in one day of 24 hours, or more than 48 hours in one week, or before the hour of 5 o'clock in the morning or after 10 o'clock in the evening. 

3. TF No girl under the age of 21 years and no boy under the age of 18 years may be employed as a messenger for any telegraph, telephone or messenger company, in the distribution or delivery of goods or messages in towns of more than 

15,000 inhabitants. 

4. T F The population of a city is a material fact to be considered in the enforcement of the laws pertaining to the employment of children. 

5. T F The Commissioner of the Bureau of Labor Statistics may give consent for a child between the ages of 15 and 18 years to perform in a theater after ten P. M. 

6. T F A farmer employs a minor 16 years of age during the school vacation and during such employment requires the boy to work 12 hours each day. This is a misdemeanor. 

7. T F The Commissioner of Labor Statistics may serve any process throughout the State, in labor cases coming within the provisions of the Child Labor Law but he cannot take or exert the powers of a peace officer. 

8. T F The Bureau of Labor Statistics has the power to issue permits for minors under the age of 18 years to attend part time school in order that such minors may accept employment. 

9. TF No minor under 16 years of age who would be required by law to attend school, shall be out of school and unemployed for more than ten consecutive days while the public schools are in session, but must enroll and attend school. 

10. TF No permit to work may be issued to a minor under the age of 16 years.

TEST NUMBER 23 PENAL CODE 

1. T F There are only three state institutions in which minors may be committed; namely, Ventura School for Girls, Preston

School of Industry and Whittier State School for Boys. 

2. 

T F The Coordinating Council is a case working agency. 

3. T F The father of a minor child is unable, by reason of physical infirmity, to provide the necessities of life to such child. The mother may be prosecuted if she fails to provide such necessities. 

4. T F The father of an illegitimate child is required by law to provide necessities of life for such child in the same manner and to the same extent as if the child were his legitimately but has no right to the custody or control of such child. 

5. TF No child under the age of 16 years may be transported in any vehicle to any place in company with adults charged with or convicted of crime. 

6. T F Commitment to a state school by the Juvenile Court, in effect suspends the operation of the statutes of limitation. 

7. T F A parent who sends his 19 year old son to a gambling house, knowing it to be such, is guilty of a misdemeanor. 

8. T F Parents of a minor child are separating and contemplating divorce. The child has been voluntarily left with the mother by the father. Later the father goes to the school which such child is attending and falsely representing himself as being sent by the mother, induces the school officials to release the child to him, with intent to spirit such child to another state for the purpose of forever concealing it from the mother. He has committed no crime. 

9. T F The rules of evidence apply in Juvenile hearings before 

Juvenile Court in same manner as in adult criminal cases in other courts. 

10. T F Every person who sells or deals in tobacco as a business and fails to keep posted in such place of business a copy of the law prohibiting the sale of tobacco to minors is guilty of a misdemeanor.

TEST NUMBER 24 PENAL CODE 

1. T F When a child has been declared a ward of the Juvenile 

Court, committed to an institution and makes a satisfactory record, the court may order the case dismissed. Such dismissal acts as a bar to prosecution arising out of the same set of facts as the original case. On the other hand, if such minor makes an unsatisfactory record it also lies within the province of the Juvenile Court to declare him unfit for further consideration by the Juvenile Court and remands him to be tried under the general laws. If convicted, the subject may be sent to a state penitentiary, all growing directly out of the original charge set forth in the petition filed before the minor was sent to the juvenile state institution. 

2. T F (1057) The panel is the list of prisoners to face the judge at that particular session. 

3. T F (841) P, a policeman in disguise, suspected D of crime because as P knew, she had no visible means of support, yet was living expensively. P asked D to procure a woman for him for immoral purposes. D accepted and two hours later procured and placed in P's custody a woman, W, who was so intoxicated as not to know or care for what purposes she was placed in P's custody. P paid D $50 for this. P never intended to, and did not in fact, use W for immoral purposes. 

This would be enough evidence to dismiss the officer. 

4. T F (931) Grand Jurors have the right to investigate property that might escheat. 

5. TF (1392) In proceedings against a corporation, the summons must be served at least 5 days before the day of appearance fixed therein. 

6. T F (1596) There is no rule that the provision of any section, in their entirety, shall be strictly pursued. 

7. T F (1562) The words "complainant" and "defendant" mean the same thing. 

8. T F (1538) W was an applicant for a search warrant. Later he asked the magistrate for a copy of the inventory. The magistrate is not duty bound to grant such a request.

TEST NUMBER 26 PENAL CODE 

1 M, a citizen of the United States, was the leader of an anarchistic group in Los Angeles. Y, a British subject, was the leader of a similar group in Canada, and Z, a Mexican citizen, was the leader of another similar group in Mexico. M plotted against the United 

States government, and planned on starting a war against the government in California. Help was needed to carry out M's plans so he sent for Y and Z with some of their followers, who came to Los Angeles and joined with M. Their first militant act against the government was a failure and M, Y and Z were arrested. 

1. M, Y and Z are guilty of treason against the government of the 

United States. 

2. On account of the nature of our treaties with our foreign neighbors, the guilt of Y would exceed that of Z. 

3. M is the only member of this group who is guilty of treason. 

4. According to our law, M is the only party guilty of a crime. Check 1 2 3 4 2 E had been living with M, though she was not married to M. After several years M lost interest in E and left her to marry L. E lay in wait for M and, when he appeared, shot at M with the intent to kill him. E missed her mark and shot and killed S, an innocent bystander, whose presence was unknown to E: 

1. E could not be held on a serious charge for killing S, as she knew nothing of the presence of S and had no intention of harming S in any way. 

2. As E shot at M with intent to kill him, she is guilty of murder in killing S with the shot intended for M. 

3. M could be held on a charge of bigamy for marrying L after having lived so long with E. 

4. L knew that M had been living with E, so L is guilty of bigamy in marrying M.

TEST NUMBER 27 PENAL CODE 

1 Only one of the following propositions is correct. Mark the correct one.

M, a Mexican, in his own language made a statement to officer O through an interpreter X. M first asked, "What can I expect from you if I talk?" and O replied through the interpreter X, "Not a thing."

M, however, made a full statement admitting he had committed a robbery. O testified at M's trial for the robbery concerning the conversation and as to what O said and what M said, through the interpreter. 

1. This evidence is not admissible.

O, an officer, found a still on premises occupied by A, B, C, and D. X, Y, and Z were also present. The officer said, "I'll help any of you get probation if you speak up." Y said, "Don't arrest X and Z. They don't know anything about it." 

2. Offer of reward will prevent the use of Y's statement. P, on arrest for murder, tried to escape, but was recaptured. P said, "I'm glad I was caught." Officer X said, "I'd hate to see you swing, I'll do all I can for you." P said, "If I can get life, I'll plead guilty." 

3. P's statement made before X spoke is admissible, but the rest is not admissible. O told Y that he had committed a burglary. B is on trial for that burglary. Y is called to the stand to testify that O had confessed to him that he had committed the crime and that B is innocent. 

4. If the confession of O was voluntary, this evidence is admissible, but not otherwise.

1. This evidence is enough to convict R or murder. 

2. Motive may not be proved. 

3. Motive is always an essential element to be proved. 

4. Further investigation might be advisable. Check 1 2 3 4

TEST NUMBER 28 PENAL CODE 

Only one of the following problems in this group is correct. Mark the correct one:

A law was passed in 1880 making petty larceny with a prior conviction of petty larceny a felony. In 1879 A had been convicted of petty larceny. In 1881 A was arrested for petty larceny, and the district attorney prosecuted A for a felony, based on the prior conviction of petty larceny in 1879. A contends the law was ex post facto in effect as to him. 

1. A is right. He can only be charged with petty larceny, a misdemeanor.

B was charged with burglary. B pleaded guilty and applied for probation in 1926. At that time the law provided that a court in granting probation had no power as one of the terms of probation to order a defendant into custody to serve a term in the county jail. In 1927 the legislature amended the law giving the court that power. After amendment went into effect, B came before the court on the hearing on the application for probation. The court granted B probation but imposed as a term of probation that B should serve the first year in the County Jail. B contends that the law was ex post facto as to him. 

2. B's contention is wrong. On the eve of a trial the District Attorney moved for a change of venue to another county on the grounds that because of preju- · dice a fair trial could not be held in the county where the crime was alleged to have been committed. C the defendant joined in the request. The court granted the motion and transferred the case for trial to another county. 

3. The court was wrong. The defendant should be tried in the county where the crime was committed. * * * D was tried and convicted of the crime of larceny. The crime was committed in 1924. In 1925, the legislature adopted indeterminate sentence law providing that the length of sentence should be determined by the State Board of Prison Directors and not by the court. The act was passed in May but did not become effective until August.

D was sentenced in July by the court to the penitentiary for 5 years. D contends that the court should have sentenced him for the period prescribed by law, and left the length of time to be determined by the State Board. 

4. D's contention is right.

Answers to Test No. 28 

The 2nd problem is correct.

How Positive Can Identification Be? 

(Make notes when interviewing witnesses) 

NEVER IDENTIFY UNLESS YOU ARE POSITIVE 

William West (above) and Will West (below) whose Bertillon measurements were found to be identical but whose fingerprints differed materially. The old saying, "Mike and Ike, they look alike" certainly holds true for William and Will West. 

These men are from different parts of the country and are not related in any way.

Eye witness testimony is not always best. Always make your own investigation in addition to information from eye witnesses.

TEST NUMBER 29 PENAL CODE 

A, B and C were leaders of a strike at the F. Co. factory. The strike was a failure and the officials of the F Co. refused to give jobs to 

A, B, and C and their followers. These strikers then planned an unlawful parade as a demonstration against the acts of F. Co. 

The parade formed in the business section of the city but A did not show up. Finally B and C led their followers through the city toward the F. Co. plant without waiting for the leadership of A. As they proceded, the members of the parading group became unruly and started raiding the smaller grocery stores and fruit stands as they passed these. As the parade entered the grounds of the F. Co. it was joined by A, who then took command of the parade: 

1. No crime here. 

2. A could be held responsible for the unlawful acts of his companions committed before he joined the parade. 

3. A could not be held responsible for the unlawful acts of his companions committed before he joined the parade. 

4. Participation in any unlawful parade constitutes a felony on the part of the participants. 

5. As the F. Co. refused to let the strikers return to work and by this action caused discontent that in turn was the reason for the unlawful parade, the F. Co. could be made co-defendants for the unlawful acts of the paraders. 2 Check 1 2 3 4 5 A sued B on a note in the court of Q, a justice of the peace. A was awarded a judgment of $205. Later, Q purchased this judgment from A for $185 though at the time of the suit Q did not know that A would be willing to sell, nor did A know that Q would be willing to buy: 

1. No crime here. 

2. Both Q and A are guilty of a felony. 

3. Only Q is guilty of a misdemeanor in this transaction. 

4. A has committed a misdemeanor in selling this judgment to C. 

5. A could sell his judgment to anyone willing to buy it.

TEST NUMBER 30 PENAL CODE 

B had been convicted of grand theft. He was being transported to the prison by C when D and E, friends of B attempted to block C in the corridor of the train and thus allow B to escape. C was able to get help and placed D and E under arrest: 

1. This case would have to be tried in the county from which B and C boarded the train. 

2. D and E could be sentenced to the state prison, for not less than one, nor more than 14 years. 

3. The crime of D and E amounts to an ordinary misdemeanor. 

4. Never eligible for parole. 

5. For such an offense as this, D and E are subject to imprisonment in the state prison for not less than six months or more than five years.

In order to cover up his misdeed he set fire to the railroad car and destroyed it: 

1. No crime here. 

2. The penalty for burning the railway car would be imprisonment for not less than one year nor more than three years. 

3. A conviction on the charge of burning the railway car would be a bar to prosecution on the charge of breaking and entering the same car. 

4. The burning of a railway car does not come within the statute on arson.

TEST NUMBER 31 TRAFFIC CODE NOTE: All of the following statements are to be marked according to the law as expressed in the California Vehicle Code. 

Circle T if true; F if false. 

1. T F According to the California Vehicle Code a bicycle is not considered a vehicle. 

2. T F Every vehicle of the trailer type having not more than one axle and two wheels so designed and used in conjunction with a motor vehicle that all of its weight is on its two wheels is called a "semi-trailer." 

3. T F Equipment specified as "special Mobile Equipment" is not self propelled. 

4. T F All special mobile equipment moved upon the public highway must have permanently attached thereto or painted in a conspicuous place, a sign in letters not less than two inches in height giving the name and address of the owner. 

5. T F A person who has leased a motor vehicle for a period exceeding ten successive days is considered as the owner. 

6. TF In order to class a district as a business district more than 

50 per cent of the property fronting on both sides collectively of said highway for a distance of 300 feet must be occupied by buildings in use for business. 

7. T F Every public highway under the jurisdiction of the State 

Department of Public Works shall be a through highway. 

8. T F Every public highway is presumed to be outside of a business or residential district unless it is signposted as required by the California Vehicle Code. 

9. 

T F Special mobile equipment must be registered. 

10. T F The State Motor Vehicle Division has authority to assign distinguishing motor numbers to the motor in any motor vehicle whenever the motor number thereon shall be obliterated. 

11 T F Motorcycles must display license plates, one in front and the other in the rear. 

12. T F The bottom of all license plates must be twenty-four inches from the ground.

Answers to Test No. 31 

Questions Nos. 1, 3, 8, 10 are true; all others are false.

TEST NUMBER 32 TRAFFIC CODE 

1. T F Number plates for the current year must be displayed on the vehicle 15 days after the expiration of the former license. 

2. TF Every transferee who takes possession of a motor vehicle must, within 10 days thereafter, forward both the certificate of ownership and the certificate of registration to the 

Motor Vehicle Division. 

3. TFA chattel mortgage on a motor vehicle is not valid, except as between the parties, until the mortgagee is registered as the legal owner. 

4. T F Any person who refuses or neglects to deliver a certificate of ownership to a transferee entitled thereto is guilty of a misdemeanor. 

5. T F It is not necessary to notify the Motor Vehicle Division when a motor vehicle is leased. 

6. T F In certain instances it is permissible for the police department to authorize the operation of a motor vehicle without requiring that such motor vehicle be registered. 

7. T F Temporary operation permits cannot be granted by the 

Motor Vehicle Division. 

8. TF In no case does a non-resident permit allow the operation of a motor vehicle for a period exceeding 6 months. 

9. T F When a number plate is lost, the owner may obtain a duplicate upon proper application, or a new registration under a new number if desired. 

10. T F Registration may be refused for unsafe motor vehicles. 

11. T F It is a felony to display a fictitious certificate of registration. 

12. T F It is a misdemeanor to represent as one's own any chauffeaur's license not issued to the person so displaying the same. 

13. T F It is a felony to erase from any operator's license certificate any endorsement thereon of any conviction made by order of court. 

14. T F When a property owner rents a private garage for the storage of an automobile he must notify the police department within 24 hours and give them complete information regarding the transaction.

Answers to Test No. 32 

Questions Nos. 3, 4, 5, 10, 12 are true; all others are false.

TEST NUMBER 33 PENAL CODE 

D is arrested charged with the murder of F, his father. The evidence is wholly circumstantial. At the trial, P, the prosecuting attorney, introduces a written diary kept by F in which F stated in an entry written ten minutes before he was shot that he had quarreled with D and D had threatened to kill him. 

1. The evidence is properly admitted as a dying declaration. 

X is arrested for the murder of Y his wife. Among X's effects are found letters written by X to P with whom he has carried on a secret love affair. 

2. The letters are admissible in evidence as the State must prove motive in order to convict X. 

B is charged with the murder of C. B claims self defense. The 

State offers evidence to show that B laid in wait for C, three nights previously, with a gun, believing that C was out with another girl. 

3. This evidence could not be introduced as it was too remote. 

R is charged with arson and burning insured property. The evidence is wholly circumstantial. The state offers to prove that two nights before the fire someone removed two truck loads of furniture from R's house and that R bought 100 gallons of gasoline. 

The odor of gasoline was detected by the firemen in the house. 

4. To show motive connecting R with the crime, the evidence is admissible. Check 1 2 3 4 2

TEST NUMBER 34 PENAL CODE 

R and S, citizens of California, have been having disputes for a long time. Finally they agreed to settle their differences by fighting a duel with pistols at twelve paces. They consulted their respective friends, A and B, regarding having these friends being their seconds at the duel. B knew something of the law and pointed out to R and S that fighting a duel in this state was a felony. All parties then decided to go to a nearby foreign country which had no law against duels and there go on with their plans. After R and 

S had both recovered from the wounds they had received in the duel they returned to their homes in this state: 

1. B gave information regarding the law with the intent that it should aid in evading the law so B is the only guilty party. 

2. A and B were only acting as seconds in this duel so would not be guilty of any crime punishable by this state upon their return but R and S could be punished upon their return in the same manner as they would have been in case such duel had been fought within this state. 

3. As R and S were outside of this state when they fought their duel, this state would have no jurisdiction. 

4. As all parties concerned left this state with intent to evade the local laws regarding the fighting of duels, they are punishable upon their return in the same manner as they would have been in case such duel had been fought within this state. TEST NUMBER 36 PENAL CODE 

1 Two trains, one a freight train and the other a passenger train, were timed so they passed each other each night at station X. At this point there was a switch from one track to the other for the transfer by train in case one train might be out of service. A conceived the idea of throwing this switch so as to wreck the freight and so block the track of the passenger. He felt that the passengers would leave their cars to view the wreck and he could then go through the cars and help himself to whatever of value the passengers might leave behind. B had a somewhat similar idea, though he intended to throw the switch so as to wreck the passenger train and then loot it, giving no thought to the freight train. Neither A nor B knew of the plans of the other. It so happened that each man threw the different switches on the same night. By each being thrown it gave a clear track from one main line to the other. The freight hit the switch and was going at such a speed that it was wrecked even though the track was clear. The passenger was late that night so was stopped and held at a station farther up the line until the freight could be cleared from the tracks: 

1. A is guilty of train wrecking and could be punished with death, but B's intent would make him guilty of only a felony punishable by not more than five years in prison. 

2. The death penalty could not under any circumstances be inflicted on A unless there was a loss of life due to the wreck. 

3. B threw the switch in such a way that it gave a clear track so he could not be held for any crime. 

4. A and B are equally guilty of train wrecking. 2 Check 1 2 3 4 A man who was running for election to a public office had a slight physical defect about which he was sensitive. FINGERPRINT PATTERNS 

The importance of fingerprints and footprints are proving their worth each day in our courts as well as in citizen identification all over the nation. The science of fingerprinting is ever growing.

Mutilation of fingerprints by sanding, acid solutions, or cutting, is only temporary, and grow back as before. 

Plain Whorl 

Plain Arch

Lateral Pocket Coop 

Central Pocket Coop 

Tented Arch

Twinned Loop 

Loop Loop Accidental

The presence or existence of whorls in fingerprint impressions is used as the basis for the determination of the chief or primary classification. Each whorl appearing in any or all of the ten fingers has a certain arbitrary or fixed value. The addition of the values represented by such whorls and the indication of the total value is known as the primary classification. Illustrations of the whorl types which are the same as patterns having the figured value are shown on chart as well as illustration of the other types.

R gives S a check for $100 in payment of an old account and S gives R $10 in cash. At the time, R knew he had only $6.00 in the bank. 

Q owed R $500 and had promised to put this money on deposit to R's credit that same day. The check was post dated and R had written the words "post dated" in the corner but said nothing to 

S about it. 

1. This evidence makes a clear case of "check insufficient funds.” 

2. The fact that the check was post dated does not necessarily save R. 

3. Q's promise to R is no defense and cannot be put in evidence for any purpose. 

4. A case cannot be made against R based on a post dated check given for a past consideration. 2 

X presented a letter to Y reading as follows: "This will introduce my friend X. Any favors you show him will be appreciated. (Signed

B.)" The letter was written by X without authority. Y because of the letter takes X to his home and gets him a guest card at his clubs. 

1. X is guilty of forgery. 

2. An intent to defraud will have to be established in order to make a case here on forgery. 

3. Even though X intended to defraud, X goes free of forgery. 

4. There is no case here as the evidence does not show that X defrauded either Y or B. 

A has been killed. Officer O finds X badly wounded. O tells X he is going to die and X says, "Oh Yeah!" O says, "You shot A. Why did you shoot him?" X replies, "Santa Claus shot A. A told

Santa Claus he liked his sleigh and reindeer, so Santa took him for a ride." X dies. Y, his pal, is charged with murder. 

1. This is hearsay on Y's trial. 

2. This is a dying declaration which is admissible because it is an exception to the hearsay rule. 

3. This is not a dying declaration because it is not shown X has abandoned all hope of recovery. It is admissible, however, as an admission. 

4. This is not a good dying declaration, but is admissible as an accusatory statement. Evidence of X's acts and conduct in the face of the accusation is admissible.

Answers to Test No. 46 Question No. 1-Check No. 2 Question No. 2-Check No. 3 Question No. 3-Check No. 1

TEST NUMBER 50 PENAL CODE 

1 J made complaint to the District Attorney against K who sold J $5,000 worth of oil company stock on K's statement that K was sure J would double his money within six months. After one year the stock is nearly worthless. K knew the company was a "wild cat" scheme.

TEST NUMBER 52 PENAL CODE 

1 A drove his automobile past a boulevard stop sign and hit B, seriously injuring him. A was arrested and tried for assault with intent to kill B as it was well known that A and B were enemies of long standing.' The jury brought in a verdict of reckless driving against 

A. A was released on bail until such time as the court should pronounce judgment. Judgment was rendered the following morning while A was not present, nor had he been notified to be present: 

1. The defendant is not required to be present when judgment is pronounced on a misdemeanor case. 

2. It is never necessary for the defendant to be present at the time the judge pronounces judgment. 

3. If E had filed a civil suit against A before the criminal action was started, this suit would be a bar to prosecution of A on a criminal charge. 2 Check 1 2 3 C was arrested for the murder of D. When the case came to trial C admitted killing D but pleaded "not guilty by reason of insanity." 

1. Defendants are not considered sane until so proven by the plaintiff. 

2. The burden of proof of insanity in this case would be on C. 

3. A plea of "not guilty by reason of insanity" would not in itself constitute an admission of the performance of the act. 

4. Upon a plea of "not guilty by reason of insanity" it is required by law that the question of guilt must be tried before the question of sanity. 3 Check 1 2 3 4 A picked the pocket of B and took a wallet containing $100.' In making his escape A dropped the wallet and its contents. C later found the wallet and appropriated the $100 to his own use, though the wallet contained B's business card and it would have been a simple matter to return the money and wallet to B: 

1. C is guilty of petty theft and A is guilty of grand theft. 

2. A is guilty of petty theft and C is guilty of grand theft. 

3. A and C are both guilty of grand theft. 

4. A and C are both guilty of petty theft. 

5. No crime here.

TEST NUMBER 53 PENAL CODE 

1 K was a deep sea fisherman out of San Diego. He was in the habit of fishing about 20 miles to the southwest of Catalina Island. K and 

H were enemies of long standing. F, a friend of H, advised H to go to K's fishing ground and there lie in wait for K to appear and when K appeared to kill him by shooting from a distance with a high powered rifle. H thought over the plan and finally decided the risk was too great, but he did go to the wharf in San Diego and put poison in the drinking water K carried in his boat. K went to his fishing ground and there he drank of the poisoned water and died from the poison in a very short time after taking the drink. 

1. The greatest offense of which E could be held guilty would be manslaughter. 

2. Killing by the use of poison cannot be murder in the first degree. 

3. Only H is guilty of any crime. 

4. Both H and E are guilty of murder in the first degree. 2 Check 1 2 3 4 A was on trial for murder. B, a friend of A, gave testimony tending to show A innocent. C, an enemy of B's, gave testimony contradictory to that given by B. D, A's attorney, had C arrested for perjury. 

This so angered C's friends that they in turn charged B with perjury. At B's trial, C was the only witness against B and the evidence submitted was not such as to warrant a conviction of B: 

1. No crime committed . 

2. B could not have been convicted unless perjury had been proven by the testimony of two witnesses, or of one witness and corroborating circumstances. 

3. The conviction or discharge of A could not be introduced as evidence in B's trial for perjury. 

4. D is undoubtedly guilty of common barratry. 

5. The acquittal of B on the charge of perjury would be conclusive proof of the guilt of C on the charge of perjury. Check 1 2 3 4 5

TEST NUMBER 54 PENAL CODE 

1 S, a 17-year-old girl entertainer, interviewed a theatre owner T, regarding a booking for her act in his theatre. T was very much attracted by the physical beauty of S and made improper advances to S. Feeling that T would give her a contract if she consented to his suggestions, S submitted herself to T for immoral purposes. Afterward T refused S a contract. This infuriated S who attacked T tearing off some of his clothes, and at the same time screaming to attract attention. When others arrived S accused T of an immoral attack and T was arrested: 

1. No crime here. 

2. The conduct of T led S to believe she was to get a contract, therefore T is guilty of seduction. 

3. It would be impossible to convict T as no witnesses were present at the time of the act. 

4. The testimony of S could not be accepted without corroboration from other witnesses present at the time. 

5. T is undoubtedly guilty of rape. 2 Check 1 2 3 4 5 D made a violent and sudden attack upon F which so frightened F that he jumped out of the window and was killed. 

1. This makes D guilty of murder. 

2. Proximate cause and reasonable apprehension of danger on 

F's part will make D criminally responsible for an unlawful homicide in some degree. 

3. Accident and misfortune will relieve D of criminal responsibility here-excusable homicide. 3 Check 1 2 3 H, a cripple threatened his wife W, a robust woman,with a knife and she rushed from the house. The next morning W was found frozen in the snow. 

1. W's leaving as the result of H's force and violence is not enough to make H liable... Something more must be shown.... 

2. The evidence is sufficient to convict H of murder. 

3. The evidence is sufficient to convict H of manslaughter. 

4. There the death of W is not the natural consequence of H's act. Therefore, H is not liable.

TEST NUMBER 55 PENAL CODE 

1 The house of S had been burglarized. S had reason to believe that his neighbor, K, was the guilty party, and that the stolen articles were concealed in K's house. S proceeded to swear out a search warrant for the property of K. This warrant was made out by

Officer D proceeded to serve the search warrant on K but K refused to allow D to enter his house. 2. M could be held responsible for the death of K as M gave D a search warrant that was not legal as to K's home. 

3. After D received the search warrant from M he could use whatever force necessary to enter the home of K. 

4. K had no legal right to resist a search of his property on this warrant.

TEST NUMBER 56 PENAL CODE 

T was driving his auto at night and in order to conserve his battery which was very weak, he turned off his lights. While driving without lights he struck and injured Z who was crossing the street at an intersection. At the trial T admitted a violent dislike for Z and also admitted several quarrels with Z: 

1. The intentional driving of a motor vehicle at night without lights is sufficient to supply the element of intent to constitute an assault in striking a pedestrian. 

2. T could be prosecuted on a felony charge. 

3. Z would have to go into civil court in order to prosecute T. 

4. In order to get a conviction for an assault all that Z would need to prove would be T's hatred for him. 2 Check 1 2 3 4 A was jealous of her husband, B. On June 1, 1938, A saw B letting 

C, the wife of D, out of his auto near the home of D. A and B quarrel about this incident to such an extent that A finally decided to kill B. On June 10, 1938, A shot B in the abdomen with intent to kill him. On June 15, 1939, B died; his death evidently being hurried by the wound received. 

1. Only a charge of second degree murder would stand against A. 

2. After the death of B the only charge that could be placed against A would be assault with a deadly weapon. 

3. A's intent to kill B would make her guilty of 1st degree murder. 

4. Even though B and C were innocent of any wrong doing in riding home together, A would be justified in killing B if B's actions would indicate a coolness toward A. Josephine Vorliw 812 Donney Ave. 

BE ON THE LOOKOUT FOR A WOMAN PASSING CHECKS IN THE ABOVE HANDWRITING METHOD OF OPERATION 

This woman usually enters a ladies ready to wear, jewelry or shoe store, about an hour before closing time, will take plenty of time selecting the articles she intends to purchase, and then tenders a check in the amount of $25.00, $30.00 or $35.00 as payment, stating that she is a registered nurse and has been taking care of the party who signed the check. On some checks passed have been written, in the lower left hand corner, "For Professional Services". Checks are numbered "66”.

SUSPECT 

Female, white American, 35 to 45 years, 5 ft. 3 to 5 in. tall, 160 to 170 lbs., brown hair, blue eyes, claims to be a Past Matron of the Eastern Star. 

This woman has been operating in California for the past 18 months, and will continue to do so until she is apprehended. 

In the event this woman should pick you as her next victim, notify your local police department at once. If she should leave before the arrival of the officers, have a clerk follow her to her car and secure her correct license number. 

We hold a felony warrant charging issuing bank checks with intent to defraud for the above woman. Arrest, hold and advise us at once, my expense.

TEST NUMBER 67 PENAL CODE 

R and S plan to kill T by having R drop a packing box off a roof on T's head. S entices T to a spot directly under the place on the roof where R is stationed with the box and raises his finger as a signal. O, an officer, arrests R at this point. 

1. R and S can be successfully prosecuted for a conspiracy but not for an attempt.

A and B agree to destroy Y's house by an explosive. Between them they construct an infernal machine. It is fully assembled in A's house when they are arrested. 

2. A and B probably will be convicted of attempt here. 

A and Y plan to burglarize Z's house. Armed with weapons and tools, they go to Z's house. They notice W, a night watchman, so to divert his suspicions they sit of the porch of Z's house and play a ukulele. 

3. If this isn't an attempt, what is?

A, intending to steal a steer belonging to B, rides to the range where the steer is grazing. A whirls his lasso when it catches on an overhanging branch of a tree. 

4. This does not constitute an attempt. 2 O, an officer in order to get evidence on J pretends to assist J in the burglary of R's house. This activity on O's part is known as: 

1. Inveiglement. 

2. Concealment. 

3. Entrapment. 

4. Enticement.

1. Murder. 2. Manslaughter. 3. Excusable homicide.

1 G became drunk and overturned a lamp which burned K's house. 

1. G is guilty of first degree murder. Voluntary intoxication is no defense. 

2. This may be only second degree murder on account of G's intoxicated condition. 

3. G is guilty of involuntary manslaughter only. 

4. Accident and misfortune will be an absolute defense for G. 2 Only one of the following propositions is correctly answered. Mark the correct one:

A, on Tuesday night attempts to enter X bank. He fails to get in but makes a diagram of doors and windows whereby he can later effect an entrance. On Wednesday he buys a kit of burglar tools. 

On Wednesday night he tells B and C of his plans and preparations, and they agree to assist A in burglary on the following night. P, an officer, overhears the agreement and arrests A, B and C as they are leaving their meeting place on Wednesday night. 

1. A conspiracy charge will lie against all three, as B and C adopted the acts of A.

A, B and C plan a robbery. C's son D, who knows of the plan, tells 

E, a detective. 

2. The statements of D and E will be evidence at the trial of A, B and C.

X, Y and Z plan to hold up a motor bus that night. Z fails to show up and X and Y alone stop the bus and rob the passengers. 

3. As Z withdrew he is not liable to prosecution for robbery.

U, V and W plan to burglarize an office building. On arriving they find the place guarded and abandon the plan for that night. 

On the way home O, an officer, stops them for questioning. W fires and kills O. 

4. U and V are equally guilty of the murder. 3 Check 1 2 3 4 One who is liable to prosecution for the identical offense charged against the defendant on trial is:

TEST NUMBER 69 PENAL CODE 

1 F, a prize fighter, is knocked out in a ring battle. When he gains consciousness, it is discovered he has developed scarlet fever and has a temperature of 106. He also has a fractured skull. F seizes a club and hits M over the head, without any provocation. 

1. Not being conscious of the act relieves F from liability. 

2. F must establish that he was insane to the extent that he did not know the wrongfulness of the act, in order to have a good defense. 

3. F's sole defense is based on misfortune and accident, there being no criminal intent. 

4. Ignorance of fact here disproving any criminal intent is F's defense. Check 1 2 3 4 2 Mrs. L was charged with robbery and secreting jewelry to defraud an insurance company. At the trial X testified that Y engaged him to perpetrate the robbery of four women including Mrs. L, who were at a bridge party, and further that Y had told X that Mrs. 

L was a party to the plan and was to pretend that she was robbed in order to collect the insurance on her jewelry which was to be secreted and later returned to her. On the trial of Mrs. L- 

1. This evidence connecting Mrs. L with the conspiracy is admissible. 

2. This evidence is not admissible. 

3. In a trial for any crime, evidence of a conspiracy is admissible, and here would be binding on Mrs. L as being the declaration of a co-conspirator. 

4. This testimony of X, an accomplice, will convict Mrs. L if corroborated.

Check 1 2 3 4 3 The order of a competent court or magistrate that the defendant be discharged from actual custody upon security being furnished is called: 

1. Discharge. 

2. Parole. 

3. Probation.

TEST NUMBER 71 PENAL CODE 

1 O, an officer, arrests X in Arizona for an offense committed in California. Learning that X will fight extradition, O, on the pretext of taking X to see his wife, gets him in an airplane and brings him to Los Angeles, where X is placed on trial. O is also indicted in 

Los Angeles. 

1. As X was illegally brought to California, he cannot be tried;

O's motive in bringing X to California is no defense in O's trial. 

2. Both O and X can be prosecuted in California. 

3. X can be prosecuted in California but not O. 

4. Neither X nor O can be tried in California.

Check 1 2 3 4 2 X in robbing V shoots and kills V and flees. X meets Y who is seated in an auto. He hides in the auto telling Y he had killed V. O, an officer, in search of X runs by. Y does not tell O of X's whereabouts. Y then takes X's gun and throws it in a vacant lot. J, a magistrate, lives next door to Y, and Y does not tell J what he knows. Both X and Y are later arrested. 

1. Y is not guilty of any crime. 

2. Both X and Y are guilty of murder, as Y aided and abetted. 

3. X is alone guilty of murder. Y is guilty of being an accessory after the fact. 

4. Under the common law, Y would be a principal in the second degree, but not in California.

Check 1 2 3 4 3 S, a switchman, has negligently set a switch in such a manner that an oncoming train will be wrecked. D intending to wreck the train, pulls the switch, thereby in reality saving the train. 

1. S's negligence will make him criminally liable for attempt but 

D is not liable. 

2. Both S and D are liable for an attempt to derail the train. 

3. S is probably not liable for attempt; D is liable for attempt. 

4. Neither S nor D is liable.

Check 1 2 3 4

Knowingly. Maliciously. Corruptly. "Every person who roams about from place to place without any lawful business" is guilty of: ​Being idle and dissolute.

Loitering. 

3 Vagrancy.

Trespass. 

A statement relating to the cause of injury made by an injured person under a sense of impending death, is called: 

A deposition. 

An admission.

Extradition is: 

An affidavit. 

A dying declaration. Making an arrest in another state. 

Moving a prisoner from one county to another county. 

Surrender of an accused person to the justice of another state or government.

Power to make an arrest outside the county. R gave his 14-year-old son, S, an old auto. S found he could not make the auto run so wrecked it and sold the junk to T, a local junk dealer. 

T has committed a crime in buying the junk from S. 

T was a licensed junk dealer so could buy junk wherever it was available.

As R gave the auto to S, both R and T are guilty of a misdemeanor.

The facts as stated would not constitute a crime. 

Answers to Multiple Choice Questions 

No. 1, Corruptly (4th); No. 2, Vagrancy, (3rd); No. 3, A dying declaration, (4th); No. 4, Surrender (3rd); No. 5, T has committed,

TEST NUMBER 72 PENAL CODE 

1 A intended to burglarize B's house. Y, a police officer, learns of the plan and pretends to A that he will assist him. B is told of the plan and leaves the front door of his house open and puts his valuables on a table in plain view. A, accompanied by Y, goes to B's house. A becomes frightened and runs away. Y enters B's house. 

1. Cannot be convicted of any crime here. 

2. The fact that B left the door open prevents A from being convicted of burglary. 

3. The entry by Y would constitute burglary and A is liable for the acts of his co-conspirator. Therefore, A can be convicted of burglary. 

4. An officer, for the purpose of obtaining evidence, may feign the role of an accomplice, and successfully prosecute the defendant, if the officer himself has not himself originated the scheme, and the defendant has himself committed all of the essential elements of a crime. Here A can be successfully prosecuted, but not for burglary. Check 1 2 3 4 2 Only one of the following propositions is correctly answered. Mark the correct one.

D is arrested charged with the murder of his father. The evidence is wholly circumstantial. At the trial, A the District Attorney, introduces a written diary kept by F in which F stated in an entry written three minutes before he was shot that he had quarreled with D who had threatened to kill him. 

1. The evidence is properly admitted as a dying declaration. 

X is arrested for the murder of Y, his wife. Among X's effects are found letters written by X to P with whom he has carried on a secret love affair. 

2. The letters are admissible in evidence as the State must prove motive.

F, a doctor, tells N, a nurse, to give P, a patient, certain dosages of medicine, he prescribes for P. N follows F's instructions. F made a mistake in the amount he ordered N to give P and the dose is fatal. 

3. F is guilty but not N.

B desiring to help C, who is starving, took a dollar out of D's cash register. 

4. Good motive here will be a defense for B. Check 1 2 3 4

TEST NUMBER 73 PENAL CODE 

B, a bandit, boarded a railroad train and attempted to hold up Y, and by threats caused Y to jump from the train while in motion and Y thereby was killed. The jury found B guilty of second degree murder. 

1. The court should modify this verdict to first degree murder. 

2. This is a good verdict. 

3. In the event of a second trial, B could not now be convicted of first degree murder.

Check 1 2 3 4 X, a bandit, derailed a train for the purpose of robbing the passengers. 

To protect himself from an attack by a posse, he placed E, the engineer on the cow catcher in front of him. S, a sheriff, in shooting at X killed E. 

1. If X had two necks he would hang twice. 

2. X will hang here for first degree murder. 

3. It was a shot fired lawfully by S that killed E; therefore X is not criminally responsible for E's death. 

4. Here X's act caused E's death. X may hang for E's death, but not otherwise.

Check 1 2 3 4 A and B in furtherance of a conspiracy, commit a burglary. W, the watchman, surprises them and opens fire. In the exchange of shots, W kills A and X, an innocent bystander. B is captured. 

1. B is criminally liable for burglary and the death of A and X. 

2. B is criminally liable for burglary and the death of A. 

3. B is criminally liable for burglary and the death of X. 

4. B is criminally liable for burglary only. Check 1 2 3 4 O, an officer, arrests P for misdemeanor. Knowing that small-pox has broken out in one of the tanks O puts P in this tank. P dies of small-pox.

TEST NUMBER 74 PENAL CODE 

1 In order to pay his grocery bill, A drew a check for $36, though at the time he knew he did not have sufficient funds in the bank and did not intend to place funds in the bank to cover the check: 

1. If A is convicted in the criminal court, the grocer could not bring action in the civil courts. 

2. This would be a plain case of grand theft. 

3. This case could be tried only in the civil court. 

4. It is within the jurisdiction of the court to sentence A on either a misdemeanor charge or on a felony charge. 

5. Petty theft. 2 Check 1 2 3 4 5 B placed a large quantity of goods in a bonded warehouse and received receipts for the same. B then altered the receipts to make it appear that the quantity of goods was much larger than actually present, and then used the receipts as collateral for a loan at his bank. 

1. No crime here. 

2. B intended to pay back the money borrowed so is not guilty of a crime although he has been indiscreet. 

3. B should be held on a charge of robbery. 

4. B is guilty of forgery. 

5. In this instance, B is guilty of embezzlement. 3 Check 1 2 3 4 5 A married his aunt, B, in Los Angeles. They then moved to Orange 

County to live. After a short time A deserted B and went to 

San Diego County where he established his residence. Sometime later A heard that a warrant had been issued in Los Angeles for his arrest on a charge of incest. A made an attempt to leave the state but was apprehended in Imperial County. 

1. B, having a legal residence in Orange County, could demand that A's trial be in that county. 

2. This case would come within the jurisdiction of any of the counties mentioned except San Diego County. 

3. As A and B made Orange County their home it would be necessary that this case be tried in that county. 

4. A could be prosecuted only in Los Angeles County on this charge. 

5. Federal case only.

TEST NUMBER 92 PENAL CODE 

1. T F The time to get evidence is when the case first breaks, or as soon as the crime has been discovered; the more time allowed to lapse between the discovery of the crime and the search for evidence, the greater the chance that some of the evidence will disappear or be unprocurable. 

2. T F The experienced detective ceases all further efforts at investigation if he gets a confession or substantial admissions from the prisoner. 

3. T F It is perfectly proper for the detective to consult his notebook to refresh his memory. 

4. T F The appearance of the auto tire marks on the road when going straight ahead, when turning, when sliding forward or when sliding sideways, the difference in tire marks of a car going forward from the marks by the same tires when the car goes backwards, are illustrations of things the detective may observe which may in the future prove of value in an investigation. 

5. T F The only time a confession is admissible in evidence is before independent proof of the corpus delicti. 

6. T F For the admission of a dying declaration it is not necessary that the person making the declaration to have believed, at the time of the declaration, that he was going to die. 

7. TF Arson may be committed to destroy incriminating records contained in the building. 

8. TF Insane people, little children and idiots are considered criminals, even though they commit lawless acts. 

9. T F Arson may be committed for the purpose of collecting insurance upon the property sought to be burned. 

10. T F Arson may be committed to cover and hide the traces of some other crime, such as an attempt to cover up a murder and destroy the body or at least its identity. 

Answers to Test No. 92 

Questions Nos. 1, 3, 4, 7, 9, 10, 11 are true; all others are false.

TEST NUMBER 93 PENAL CODE 

1. T F Whenever men are not engaged in any honest, gainful employment, an officer is justified in suspecting that they have no honest purpose, and while they may be honest, an investigation is worth while. 

2. T F In order for a dying statement to be admissible, it is necessary that the declarant shall die immediately after the declaration. 

3. TFA dying declaration that is in writing and signed may have greater weight with a jury. 

4. T F While the rule has no application to admissions, it is a settled rule of law that, before a confession can be received in evidence, it must be shown that it was made freely and voluntarily. 

5. T F It is not unlawful to possess a pipe or contrivance for smoking opium, if no opium or other narcotic is found with it. 

6. T F It is not unlawful to furnish cocaine or morphine to a person without a doctor's prescription, if it is given away and no charge made for it. 

7. T F Hearsay is the testimony of one person as to what some other person has stated in relating a past transaction or what the witness himself has related in the past to some other person. 

8. T F Seizure of narcotics cannot be made without an order from the State Board of Pharmacy. 

9. T F An admission is any act or statement of a person accused of crime which tends toward proving his guilt. 

10. T F If such act or statement mentioned under No. Nine, standing alone and by itself, without other essential facts, shows him to be guilty of the crime with which he is charged, the statement or act is a confession. 

11. T F To be admissible, dying declarations must be made by the deceased under a sense of impending death.

Answers to Test No. 93 

Questions Nos. 1, 3, 4, 7, 9, 10, 11 are true; all others are false.

TEST NUMBER 94 TRAFFIC CODE 

1. T F "Under a sense of impending death" the deceased must believe that his death will immediately or speedily follow his declaration in order to use same in court. 

2. T F To be admissible, dying declarations must be confined to the circumstances immediately attending the infliction of the fatal injury. 

3. T F "Under a sense of impending death" referred to in No. One, means that the declarant must, at the time of making the statement, believe that he will die as a result of his injuries; hope of recovery must be absent. 

4. T F The slang word "yegg" is used to designate a person suffering from the narcotic habit. 5. 6. 

TF In the language of the underworld, one who steals from the other by fraud or swindle is referred to as a "shover." 

T F "Touch him for his poke" and "steal his purse," are expressions somewhat synonymous. 

7. TF "Snowbird" is an underworld name often given to those who are addicted to smoking opium pipes. 

8. T F In criminal slang, the words, "stoolie," "squealer" and "informer," are often used interchangeably. 

9. T F The word "tail," is frequently used by detectives and investtigators and means "to follow." 

10. T F Nitroglycerine is sometimes referred to by safeblowers as "soup" and "grease." 

11. T F When a detective, in speaking of a criminal, says "There's a rap against him," he means that the court has issued an order restraining the police from arresting him on suspicion. 

12. T F "Snow" is a slang word applied to cocaine. 

13. T F Crooks have been known to speak of diamonds as "rocks."

Answers to Test No. 94 

Questions Nos. 2, 3, 6, 8, 9, 10, 12, 13 are true; all others are false.

TEST NUMBER 95 PENAL CODE 

1. T F (530) D, head of an automobile detective agency, devised a scheme to get business. D told the scheme to X and other thieves of his acquaintance. X, pursuant to the scheme, took Ps car in Inglewood, and abandoned it in an alley in 

Hawthorne. X then informed D of its location. D went to P and got P to hire him to recover the car. P gave D a diamond ring, to be returned if D did not locate the car; if 

D located the car, P was to get the ring back on payment of $50. D then took P to the place where the car was left. 

D gave X the ring. X took the ring to P, who paid X $50, and X returned the ring to P. X then paid D half of the $50 received from P. There's plenty of evidence here for an extortion charge. 

2. T F (447a) On April 7, 1937, D hired E to burn down D's house for the purpose of concealing evidence of illegal manufacture of liquor by D. At the time the house was, so far as D or E knew, uninhabited. Unknown to D or E, on April 8, 1937, a court legally ordered P, a policeman, "to occupy D's house and to live and sleep in the house" for the purpose of preventing anyone from destroying the evidence. P did so. 

On April 10, 1937, D went to another city to establish an alibi and E burned the house. P was burned to death while imprudently trying to put out the fire. P's carelessness reduces the crime to arson. 

3. T F (435) Suppose the City of Inglewood had an ordinance requiring all persons transporting explosives by truck should display a large placard with the word EXPLOSIVES. A was transporting a load of nitroglycerine in his truck through the city without having placed such a sign on his truck. Although in respects, A was driving in a careful manner, he was just about to run down P, an elderly lame man from Lennox, who was hobbling across the street, when negligently driven automobile of B collided headon with A's truck and diverted its course so that it did not strike P, as otherwise it would have done. The collision caused the nitroglycerine in A's truck to explode and the force of the explosion knocked P down, causing him to suffer severe injuries. P was taken to the Central Hospital where a newly appointed nurse (from Burbank) negligently dropped some hot onion soup in his eye. All we have from a police standpoint is a violation of a city ordinance.

Criminal Digest PART II

DIGEST 

of 

CRIMINAL LAWS 

and 

DECISIONS PART II EDITOR'S NOTE:-(C.W.F.) in the Digest means Charles W. 

Fricke, Judge of the Superior Court of Los Angeles County;

the outstanding authority on Criminal Law in America. 

Selected from the Records of the 

California Appellate Courts

Criminal Digest ACCOMPLICE-GENERAL 

"There being in the record other evidence which measures up to the requirement of Section 1111 of the Penal Code, tending to connect the defendant with the commission of the crime charged, the jury was entitled to consider the testimony of the accomplice the same as any other testimony and to give to it such weight as the jurors might conclude it was entitled to." 

People vs. Wynn, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 723. "An accomplice is not rendered incompetent as a witness by the mere fact that he has been promised immunity from prosecution or mitigation of his punishment." (Quoted from 22 C.J.S. page 1381, Sec. 805.) 

People vs. Ross, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 385.

ACCOMPLICE-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court instructed the jury that “the credibility of all witnesses is to be judged by the same standard as that of any other witness. The fact that the witness McKnight has entered a plea of guilty to one count of the indictment *** standing alone should not discredit these witnesses or their testimony, unless, in addition thereto, there is something about their testimony or their manner of testifying or their interest in the case, or their bias or prejudice against one or any or all of the defendants which casts suspicion upon it in your minds."

The court says: "The testimony of an accomplice is not to be judged by the same standard as the testimony of any other witness but such evidence is to be acted upon with great caution and is subject to grave suspicion." (Note: In support of this the court cites a number of cases. People vs. Coffee, 161 Cal. 433, 438 discusses the discredit of a person testifying as an accomplice and the practice of common law judges to instruct juries to view such testimony with care, caution and suspicion but included in the instruction was the statement that if the accomplice were corroborated in any material matter by independent evidence the jury might put faith in all the accomplice had said. The case does not hold that accomplice testimony is not to be weighed by the same standard as other testimony. Neither can it be said that, because testimony is such that it should be viewed with caution, it is to be weighed by standards other than is applicable to other testimony for, if such were the rule, the testimony of the victim of a sexual crime, testimony as to oral declarations, etc. would have to be covered by a special instruction as to the standards by which they should be judged. In 

People vs. Haack, 86 Cal. App. 390, 400, the court instructed the jury that if the testimony of the accomplice "carries conviction, and the jury are convinced of its truth, they should give to it the same effect as would be allowed to a witness who is in no respect implicated in the offense." Held

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

on appeal not a correct statement of law since the testimony of an accomplice "cannot be given the same effect as may be given the testimony of one who is not implicated in the offense." The court, be it noted, was discussing the "effect" of testimony not the standard by which it should be judged. People vs. Swoape, 75 Cal. App. 404, 414 is not in point either for there the error consisted in the court's refusal of an instruction on the law of accomplices as to what corroborative evidence is and what corroboration the law required. 

The citation does not cover the standards to be used in weighing testimony. 

People vs. Braun, 31 Cal. App. (2d) 593, 603 is not in point; it is a case in which as a matter of law there was no corroboration of the accomplice such as required by the law and the comment as to weighing the testimony of the accomplice appearing on page 603 is merely that his testimony should be "viewed in the light of the reward extended him in so far as the motives arising therefrom might prompt him in the giving of his testimony” a rule applicable to all witnesses. People vs. Walther, 27 Cal. App. (2d) 583, 590, the only other California case cited in the case at bar, does not discuss the question as to whether the general standards of judging the credibility apply to accomplices the same as to other witnesses but is upon an entirely different point. (C.W.F.) 

People vs. Dail, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 906, 911.

Where there is no conflict in the evidence, it is proper for the court to instruct that certain witnesses, who had freely testified to criminal participation in the offense charged, were accomplices. People vs. Black, 45 Cal. 

App. (2d) 87. 

ACCOMPLICE CORROBORATION OF 

Rule and language of People vs. Negra, 208 Cal. 64 that the corroboration "may be slight and of itself entitled to but little consideration" need not corroborate the precise facts testified to by the accomplice and is sufficient if it tends to connect defendant with the offense" quoted and followed. 

While defendant in a 288a case was out on bail the other participant, the accomplice, was in jail on another charge. Defendant wrote several notes to accomplice while in jail reminding him of their friendship, asking him not to testify against him and promising him various things when he was released from jail and sent him a total of $50 at five dollars a week. Held sufficient corroboration. 

People vs. Andrew, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

Evidence of accomplice in a bribery case held corroborated. 

People vs. Suter, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 444.

Corroboration of one accomplice by another is not such corroboration as the law requires. 

People vs. Shaw, 17 Cal. (2d) 820, 842.

Where defendant was charged with violating sections 288 and 286 of the Penal Code the court, with reference to the testimony of the 9 year old victim, says: "While in cases of this type corroboration admittedly is not necessary," etc. (Note: This is not safe to follow since in cases under Section 286 a nine year old victim could be an accomplice. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Karpinsky, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 545.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

3 Circumstances held sufficient to connect defendants with a bookmaking place. 

People vs. Irvine, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 205.

One Ross testified that he and defendants conspired to steal from the secretary of a union certain moneys and a cash book, the secretary being in on the deal, and that the plan was carried out but the amount of money fell far short of what the secretary was expected to have in his possession. The evidence showed that defendants knew Ross had a criminal record, had knowledge of and informed Ross of the approximate amount of dues collected and told him about the exact amount the secretary actually received on the day in question and of the secretary's custom to take the money and cash book home with him at night (facts, which the opinion states, Ross had no other way of knowing). One of appellants admitted they were suspicious that Ross was the man who committed the theft, yet failed to disclose his identity to the officers. Defendants arranged to restore Ross to standing in the union knowing his criminal record. Corroboration held sufficient. (Note: Personally it does not appear to me that the alleged corroboration tended to connect defendant with the crime. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Ross, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 385.

Rule stated. "The relationship of the parties and their acts and conduct are competent evidence to be considered." 

People vs. Ross, supra. "The statements and admissions of defendant, made in connection with other testimony, may afford corroboratory proof sufficient to sustain a conviction." Admissions as to one of the perpetrators of the robbery putting the stolen property in his, defendant's, automobile and placing such property in a garage he had rented and a faked purchase of some of the property, engineered by defendant held sufficient corroboration. 

People vs. Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 411. 

An accomplice Gallina testified that he, one Spencer and appellant went to the Times Theatre, went out the balcony door leaving it open at the end of the show and about an hour and a half later reentered through the balcony exit, broke into a lobby show case and stole six radios which he and Spencer tried to sell to a radio shop next day and were there arrested. 

The manager of the theatre testified to seeing appellant in the theatre during the show. One Carson testified that about a week before the burglary, appellant Gallina and Spencer came to his house and appellant "mentioned there was some radios at the Times Theatre *** and it would be easy to get the radios and we could possibly get $10 apiece for them;" that they all four talked but no definite arrangements were made to get the radios, that they had not decided "whether I was to go, I mean we had not decided when to go" and that he had not agreed to go with the three and had not taken any part in the burglary. It also appeared that appellant and his accomplices were school-day associates. Defendant put in no defense. 

Held insufficient corroboration of the accomplices. 

People vs. Sawaya, 46 Cal. App. (2) 466.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"In order to convict upon the testimony of an accomplice, it is necessary only to connect the accused with the commission of the crime by proof other than that of the accessories. If, by her extrajudicial declaration, or by her testimony or by her stipulation in open court, her participation in the crimes was established, the cited section (P.C. 1111) will not shield appellant from the wrath of the law if from such proof the jury believed that she uttered the checks intending to defraud, as indeed they did.” "Corroborating evidence in such cases is sufficient if it tends to connect the accused with the crimes charged, even though it were slight if it stood alone." 

People vs. White, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 90.

The corroborative evidence is insufficient if it "takes direction and tends to connect the appellant with the offenses charged only when interpreted by and when read in conjunction with the testimony of the admitted accomplice." (Quoted from People vs. Shaw, 17 Cal. (2d) 778.) Court also quotes People vs. Morton, 139 Cal. 719 as to the test of considering only the evidence not given by the accomplice to see whether there be any tending to connect defendant with the crime. 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306.

In discussing the question of corroboration of accomplice testimony the Court says, "Flight is a factor tending to connect an accused with commission of an offense." (Note: The case does not hold that flight alone would be sufficient corroboration. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306.

The testimony of an eyewitness is sufficient corroboration. 

People vs. Poor, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 241.

It was established by witnesses not accomplices that one Jacoby was an active participant in a robbery in which furs were taken. Jacoby testified that appellant was the one who planned the robbery and took charge of the booty. Part of the loot was found in a room rented by appellant before the robbery, he had previously received stolen furs from Jacoby and, according to his own statement, knew a few days before the robbery that it was going to be committed. Held sufficient corroboration. 

People vs. Miller, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 470.

ACCOMPLICE CORROBORATION- INSTRUCTIONS 

Instruction identical with rule in People vs. Yeager approved. 

People vs. Andrew, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

Appellant claimed error because the trial court did not, of its own motion, give an instruction as to the necessity of corroboration of an accomplice. "There was no occasion for the giving of this instruction, for there was an

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

5 abundance of evidence to sustain the conviction without the testimony of the witness Black." 

People vs. Marvich, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 858. to same effect

People vs. Knight, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 887. 

In instructing the jury the court omitted the clause of Section 1111 of the Penal Code, "and the corroboration is not sufficient if it merely shows the commission of the offense or the circumstances thereof." The court mentions that in People vs. Cowan, 38 Cal. App (2d) 231 such an omission was held to be error but not prejudicial since the jury was otherwise sufficiently instructed as to the rule. Error here, similarly held not prejudicial. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

ACCOMPLICE-FEIGNED 

The police officer who contacted defendant and purchased narcotics from him is not an accomplice of the defendant. (The opinion puts this conclusion on the basis of feigned accomplice but the better and more applicable rule is that, in a case of selling narcotics, the buyer can not be an accomplice as he is not liable to prosecution for the offense charged. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 690

ACCOMPLICE-WHO IS-INSTRUCTIONS 

Where the jury is fully instructed on the law of accomplice and the question whether or not a particular witness was or was not an accomplice is one as to which different conclusions might be drawn from the evidence, it was proper for the court to refuse to instruct the jury that such witness was, as a matter of law, an accomplice. People vs. Wilson, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 993. 

ACCOMPLICE-WHO IS NOT 

A person to whom defendants suggested that he act as a receiver of bets on horse races but who replied that he was not interested, although he had in the past taken such bets, was not an accomplice of defendants charged and convicted of conspiracy to violate subdivision 1 of Section 337a, and of vio lating subdivisions 1 and 2 of said section of the Penal Code. 

People vs. Irvine, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 205.

The minor victim of a violation of Section 288 of the Penal Code is not an accomplice even though he knew it was not right to permit the acts. 

People vs. Slaughter, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 724. 

following People vs. Hulbart, 55 Cal. App. 112.
