The female induced to become an inmate is not an accomplice of the person charged with pandering. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Defendant induced complainant to play cards for money with a person actually acting jointly with defendant upon defendant's assurance that he would signal to complainant what cards his opponent held and how to bet. 

After winning for a while complainant lost a large sum of money. Held that complainant was not an accomplice as he could not be prosecuted for the theft of his own money. 

People vs. Solano, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

Under a charge of selling narcotics, the buyer is not an accomplice. 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 690. "It is clear that a victim who consents to the perpetration of sodomy or fellatio upon himself solely because of the infliction of physical violence or an account of threats of great bodily harm, is not an accomplice to the crimes. 

The consent to participate in a crime, which constitutes one an accessory thereto, must be knowingly, willingly and voluntarily given, with full appreciation of the nature of the act." 

People vs. Battolina, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 685. 

"The vendor of stolen property is not an accomplice of his vendee." 

People vs. Burness, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 234.

The victim of a murder wherein death was caused by an illegal abortion is not an accomplice even though, had she not died and had the prosecution been for abortion, the law requires that she be corroborated. The latter rule does not apply where the charge is murder.

People vs. Wilson, 25 A.C. 337. 

People vs. Clapp, 24 Cal. (2d) 835. "The only evidence that the defendant committed the crime of incest upon his thirteen-year old daughter Barbara came from her lips, but no corroboration is required in such a case." Citing People vs. Hobday, 131 Cal. 

App. 626; People vs. Hall, 25 Cal. App. (2d) 336, 339. (Note: In the Hobday case the victim was the 14 year old daughter and, without citation of authority or reason given, the court merely says that this has been the law for many years. In the Hall case the victim was an eighteen year old daughter. The court states that her uncorroborated testimony was sufficient to sustain the verdict and cites People vs. Gomez, 118 Cal. 326 and People vs. Rabbit, 64 Cal. App. 264. The charge in the Gomez case was not incest but assault with intent to commit rape and no element of blood relationship appears but it does appear that the prosecutrix was "fully" corroborated. The Gomez case is obviously not in point. The charge in People vs. Rabbit was statutory rape a crime which can only be committed by a male. In the last two cited cases the victim is of course not an accomplice. 

Where incest is with the consent of both parties they are each guilty. (People vs. Stratton, 141 Cal. 604; People vs. Smith, 23 Cal. App. 382; People vs. Adinolfi, 106 Cal. App. 261) but where the female is under 18 years of age and incapable of consent she is not an accomplice. (People vs. Stoll, 84 

Cal. App. 99; People vs. Kemp, 139 Cal. App. 48; People vs. Lee, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 199; People vs. Lachuk, 5 Cal. App. (2d) 729.)

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

In a prosecution of a husband for placing his wife in a house of prostitution two persons who conducted such house and with whom defendant had discussed the employment but who did not know of the relation of husband and wife are not accomplices. 

People vs. Stein, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 470.

ACCUSATORY STATEMENTS 

Evidence was received of an accusatory statement made by an accomplice in defendant's presence to which defendant made prompt denial. Held that defendant was not prejudiced by the error since the accomplice testified at the trial to the same facts covered by the accusatory statement. (Note: Court does not consider that the accusatory statement and denial may have been admissible as in affect a false statement tending to show a consciousness of guilt. C.W.F.)

People vs. Dorenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 411. 

Where the failure to deny an accusation is due not to the defendant's hesitancy but to the interruptions by his interrogator or other person present which chop off his response before it is completed "no inference of acquiescence on the defendant's part is justified" and the evidence is inadmissible. 

People vs. Lee, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 199. 

In the written transcript of the accusation and what was said on that occasion the stenographer, following one of the accusing questions inserted "(no reply)". Held that it was error to read this since that was hearsay. "If the defendant made no reply someone present should have so testified, but no one did." 

People vs. Lee, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 199. 

In a prosecution for abortion evidence was received of the statement of a woman that defendant had committed an abortion upon her, and when asked if she had anything to add to the statement defendant replied in the negative. "It is true that the statement constituted an accusation of an offense other than the one with which appellant was charged; but the offense of which he was thus accused was one of which evidence could have been offered to show appellant's intent in committing the acts charged against him." Held admissible.

People vs. Marineau, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 1035. 

ADMISSIONS 

Guns shown defendant in Portland were admitted by him to be his property. The guns described by the witnesses to the hold-up correspond to the guns in Portland which were not produced at the trial being held by the Portland officers. Held that the declaration of the defendant as to the ownership of the guns was admissible. 

People vs. Best, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 100.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

A statement by defendant on the day of his arrest in which he denied his guilt but which also included admissions of fact against himself is admissible.

People vs. Mandell, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 806. 

AGE 

A minor becomes fourteen years of age on the day preceding his fourteenth birthday. 

People vs. Dudley, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 187.

AMENDMENT OF INDICTMENT 

After both sides had rested the court granted a motion of the district attorney to amend the indictment by inserting after the words "offer and give a bribe or bribes" the words "to-wit, money" and prior to the names of the councilmen to whom it was alleged bribes were offered the words "any five of the following persons, to-wit". Appellant contends that he was entitled to be rearraigned upon the indictment as amended and to time to plead thereto.

Held that Section 1008 of the Penal Code "authorizes the type and kind of amendments permitted in the instant case" and that since defendant had already pleaded not guilty to the charge and the amendments did not change the offense but merely showed more particularity as to the offense there was no error when the court denied appellant a continuance for plea or demurrer. 

People vs. Suter, 43 Cal. App. 444.

The counts of an indictment charging kidnapping for the purpose of robbery were amended by adding the statement that the victims had suffered bodily harm. Held that this did not "change the offense charged" but merely added facts which bore upon the question of penalty, that it was not a necessary allegation and defendant was not prejudiced. (Note: The court might more directly have stated that, even for the purpose of penalty the allegation was not necessary. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Haley, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 618. An indictment charging two or more crimes may be amended by adding the allegation that they were connected in their commission. 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306.

APPEALS-GENERAL 

Where defendant was convicted of both statutory rape and forcible rape, both charges being based upon but one sexual act and constituting but one offense, judgments under both convictions were modified by order of the Supreme Court to constitute but one judgment. 

People vs. Craig, 17 Cal. (2d) 487.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

9 Where matters omitted from the reporter's transcript could in no way injure the presentation of the appeal on its merits the appellate court will not send back the transcript for correction. (Note: Matter omitted was part of appellant's argument to the trial court on a question of law.) 

People vs. Marr, 44 Cal. App. 760, 935.

Upon appeal after a second trial the appellate court cannot consider any evidence given at the first trial. 

People vs. Campanella, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 697. 

There can be no appeal from a judgment where defendant was placed on probation without any judgment or sentence having been pronounced. 

People vs. Duncan, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 642.

Where, in response to the request of appellant that the arguments of the district attorney be included in the transcript on appeal, the inclusion in such transcript of those portions of such argument to which objection was made "was a sufficient compliance with the order" made by the trial judge. 

People vs. Stone, 199 Cal. 610, 624.

Where the clerk's transcript on appeal shows that a motion for a new trial was made and denied the court on appeal must presume that the record speaks the truth. 

People vs. Palmer, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 719.

On an appeal from the order denying a motion for a new trial, an attack upon either an indictment or information cannot be made. Matters which could be covered by demurrer or motion to set aside, or motion in arrest of judgment, can be raised only on appeal where the appeal is from the judgment. 

People vs. Duncan, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 184.

When a judgment is appealable and the legal point in question can be and is properly raised on an appeal from the judgment the appeal must be taken from the judgment and not from a subsequent order refusing to vacate the judgment. 

People vs. Montgomery, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 444, 777.

Defendant, after conviction, was sentenced to state prison and execution of the sentence suspended and placed on probation. Thereafter probation was revoked and the sentence ordered in full force and effect. Defendant appealed from this order putting the judgment in force but did not attack the order revoking probation, the sole argument being as to the validity of the judgment entered some sixteen months previous. Appeal dismissed. 

People vs. Morago, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 1009.

Where appellant's brief contains no adequate citation to the transcript and fails to point out wherein the evidence is not sufficient the court is "justified in refusing to search the record for the purposes of an opinion." 

People vs. Quitoriano, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 816.

CRIMINAL DIGEST APPEALS-APPEALABLE-WHAT IS 

An order granting probation or suspending sentence without following the provisions and requirements of section 1203 of the Penal Code is an error of procedure which is reviewable on an appeal from the judgment. 

People vs. Lopez, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 854. 

"Not being void on its face, any errors or uncertainties could have been reached by appropriate action before judgment or presented on appeal from the judgment, and are, therefore, not reviewable on an appeal from an order denying a motion to vacate the judgment." 

People vs. Marr, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 760, 935.

APPEALS-APPEALABLE, WHAT IS NOT 

"An appeal does not lie from the sentence." (The points so involved are covered by appeal from the judgment.) 

People vs. Andrew, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 126. 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 690.

The Appellate Division of the Superior Court, Los Angeles County, having before it a case in which, instead of appealing from the sentence and the judgment as in some cases, decided by the District Court of Appeal in which the point could be considered under the appeal from the judgment, there was an appeal from the sentence but no appeal from the judgment and the error of the trial court produced, in the light of the law as declared by the court, an injustice, reviews the decisions holding there is no appeal from the sentence and notes the distinction therein contained between the terms "sentence" and "judgment." The court then says: "An appeal lies from a 'sentence' in a criminal case because it lies from the final judgment of conviction; the two terms are synonymous." (Lengthy and comprehensive and quite conclusive citation and quotation of authorities.)

People vs. Lopez, 43 Cal. App. 854. 

No appeal lies from an order denying a motion in arrest of judgment. 

People vs. Dallas, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 596.

On October 18 the court denied defendant's motion for leave to withdraw his plea of guilty entered in Municipal Court and have a preliminary examination and imposed sentence. On October 29, 1940 defendant presented motions for a writ of coram nobis, to withdraw the plea of guilty and to set aside the judgment and sentence. The motions were denied. Opinion states appellant appealed that appellant took an immediate appeal from the orders of October 18th and also appealed from the orders of October 29th. The ap plications of October 29th were but repetitious of the applications of October 18th. 

While the law permits an appeal from an order after judgment affecting the substantial rights of a defendant, "the rule is well settled that an order made after judgment is not appealable where the motion merely asks the court to repeat or overrule the former ruling on the same facts." 

People vs. Vivian, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 163.

An order placing defendant on probation is not an appealable order. 

People vs. Kauffman, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 393. 

People vs. Crowe, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666. 

Neither is an order denying probation appealable. 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 690.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

11 

APPEALABLE-WHAT IS NOT 

"An order is made after judgment not appealable where the motion or application merely asks the court to repeat or overrule the former ruling on the same facts." "To allow an appeal from an order denying a motion to set aside or modify the final judgment where, as in the instant case such appeal is based upon errors and rulings which might properly be considered and which were as a matter of fact considered upon the appeal from the judgment, would in fact amount in criminal appeals to removing entirely the time limitation mentioned." (viz. the time for appeal from a judgment.) 

People vs. Palmer, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 567, 579.

An order denying a motion to set aside a plea of guilty is not appealable. 

People vs. Price, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 716.

In addition to finding defendant not guilty by reason of insanity the court found that defendant was still insane and directed that he be confined in the state hospital for the insane. Held that there was no judgment from which defendant could appeal, that the order of commitment was not appealable and that if defendant desired to attack the commitment or detention in the hospital the remedy was by habeas corpus. 

People vs. Scarborough, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 210.

APPEALS-DIMINUTION OF RECORD 

"Nowhere do we find authority to enlarge or expand the record on appeal except as that process might take place when the record is corrected by the addition of matters which should come up with the record. If there is any error in the record on appeal the application to correct it should be made in the trial court. That court has the power to alter the record so as to make it speak the truth and for the purpose of correcting any error or defect in the transcript the appellate court may make its order for diminution of the record. The appellate court may also require the production of a certified copy of any paper or record which was before the trial court and make it a part of the record on appeal if it will assist in a determination of the appeal on its merits." Held, however, that affidavits prepared after the appeal from the judgment was taken and which deal with a subject that could have been included on the motion for a new trial could not be added to the record by the trial or appellate court. 

People vs. Buck, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 558.

A motion for diminution of the record will be granted to include therein a transcript of the preliminary hearing where counsel in his supporting affidavit states that it is necessary to properly present the questions involved. 

People vs. Duncan, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 642.

APPEAL-DISMISSAL 

Where defendant is placed on probation before and without any judgment a motion to dismiss an appeal from the judgment must be granted. 

People vs. Duncan, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 642.

CRIMINAL DIGEST APPEALS-SUFFICIENCY OF RECORD 

Where the record does not show the entry of a judgment (probably because the appeal was taken before judgment and while an application for probation was pending) the Appellate Court cannot review the denials of motions to set aside, and in arrest of judgment. 

People vs. Wilde, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 482.

The court will not consider error in the refusal or giving of instructions unless all instructions on the subject are set forth in appellant's brief. 

People vs. Flint, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 203.

ARREST 

When one Lepter returned to his car, which he had left parked on the street, he found defendant behind the wheel with the right door open and the vent of the cowl pried open. Defendant was in the act of searching the glove compartment. Lepter requested defendant to leave and attempted to hold him when defendant struck him and ran about a block with Lepter yelling and trying to hold him. During the scuffle both fell down and as a result of the fall Lepter suffered a broken ankle. 

"When a person temporarily deprives the owner of a vehicle of temporary possession even without intent to steal he is guilty of a felony.. temporary possession may be for a day and it may be for less than a minute. 

For some time at least appellant then and there occupied complainant's car without the latter's consent. (Here the court points out that appellant had also violated section 504 of the Vehicle Code.) "Finding that appellant had already injured the car and was in the act of committing both a felony and a misdemeanor it was the duty as well as the right of complainant to arrest appellant. A private person making such arrest is not required to advise the offender of his intention to arrest him when the latter is actually engaged in the commission of, or in attempting to commit an offense, or where he is pursued immediately after its commission or after an escape. 

(Penal Code, Sec. 841.)" 

.... "On finding himself at bay it was appellant's duty then and there to stand and wait either to be escorted by Lepter to a magistrate (Penal Code, 849) or until the arrival of the police." 

People vs. Score, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 495.

An officer observed two men hunting and shooting burros by looking at them through a telescope and went over and arrested them for hunting without a license. Held that even though he did not ascertain that they had no licenses until he came up to the men, the offense was committed in his presence. 

Roynon vs. Battin, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 970.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

13 

ARSON-GENERAL 

Penal Code, Section 447a, defining arson held constitutional. The court considers the point that under this section a person may be guilty of arson though he is the owner of an unoccupied building. Equal protection of the laws, legality of the classification of the object of arson and reasonableness considered. 

People vs. George, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 568.

Evidence held sufficient to establish the offense and defendant's guilt. 

People vs. Arnold, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 11. 

ASSAULT BY FORCE LIKELY TO PRODUCE 

• • • • 

GREAT BODILY INJURY 

"To constitute the crime of assult within the reasoning of Section 245 of the Penal Code it is not required that an intent to severely injure the victim by the use of force be proved, nor is it required that the injuries inflicted be of a serious nature. The word "violent" as used on Section 240 of the Penal Code defining an assault has been held not to be synonymous with 'bodily harm' but to include any wrongful act committed by reasons of physical force against the person of another 

What force is likely to produce great bodily injury is a question of fact to be determined by the jury." 

People vs. Bumbaugh, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 791. 

ASSAULT WITH INTENT TO COMMIT MURDER 

Where defendant earlier in the day had told his wife that he was going to kill her and that she had better be praying and later came to the front door and called to his wife to open it as his key would not work and upon her refusal fired a shot through the door and then poked the gun through the hole in the glass and, pointing the gun in her direction, fired another shot, the evidence was sufficient to establish the necessary specific intent to kill. 

People vs. Fitzgerald, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 518

ASSAULT WITH INTENT TO COMMIT RAPE 

Prosecutrix called at defendant's dance studio and rehearsed a few dance numbers with him and was asked to return the next day. This she did and defendant took her to another building where he arranged for a room while she waited in the hall. The room procured contained no furniture but a piano. They here did some dancing. Defendant remarked that she was not doing well at team dancing and suggested she do some solo work dancing partly in the nude. She objected to the idea. Defendant pulled her dress to her waist and put one hand on her breast and one hand on her privates,

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

pulled her toward him and kissed her, forced her to a bench where he kept kissing her and putting his hand on her breast and spoke of loving her. 

After a considerable time she left. 

"Whatever the extent and however rough the fondling of the woman, if her pursuer without fear of interruption voluntarily abandons his endeavor to ravish her sexual organs, then the force he employed was not an assault with intent to commit rape. The persuasions, caresses and embraces of the seducer are not evidences of a felonious intent". (Since defendant though expressing a desire for sexual intercourse did not evidence a desire to attain that result by means which would constitute rape the crime charged was not proven. C.W.F.) Order denying new trial reversed and defendant ordered discharged. (No explanation why, in the face of evidence of assault only, the conviction was not reduced to one of simple assault, C.W.F.) 

People vs. Mullen, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 297

ATTEMPT 

Note: In discussing a question of instructions, finally resolved against appellant, the court, without citation of authority, states that it is the law that "if a person voluntarily abandons his proposed plan of crime before actual commission of the crime he cannot be held guilty of an attempt". 

This is obviously not the law. After the acts have gone to the extent of an attempt to commit a crime the perpetrator is guilty of an attempt even though he thereafter voluntarily abandons his purpose and does not complete the crime. (People vs. Johnson, 131 Cal. 511, People vs. Stewart, 97 Cal. 238 the court in the last case stating "It is no less a crime though the aggressor should abandon his intentions before the consummation of the act, by reason of the pains of a stricken conscience alone". The court evidently confuses abandonment before the attempt has been made with a case in which the attempt has been consummated.) 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1

ATTEMPT TO COMMIT EXTORTION-PLEADING 

An indictment charged that defendant did wilfully and feloniously "and by wrongful use of force and fear, attempt to obtain . . . . . $2000 . . . . from one Mrs. E. P. Wood, as follows, to-wit: (here follows a lengthy allegation that defendant, an agent of a labor union, threatened to cause labor trouble among the employees of Mrs. Wood and prevent her serving her customers unless she paid him $2,000, that he had no intention of calling a strike or acting for said employees or any labor union). Indictment held sufficient. 

People vs. Bolanos, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 433

BIGAMY 

Appellant contended that since he was presumed innocent at the time of his third marriage then the presumption of continued life running to the first wife is dispelled. The court says that as this theory the presumption of innocence running to the second marriage would dispel the same presumption, the second marriage would be valid and that wife, having appeared at the trial, was sufficient proof that he had a wife living at the time of the third marriage. 

People vs. Burke, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 316

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

15 

Defendant was charged with having bigamously married Josephine

Roney in November 1938 in Arizona, he then being the lawful husband of a woman, Selma Lamarr, whom he had previously married, and with having cohabited in Los Angeles County with said bigamous wife. At the trial, by the testimony of defendant and one Wally Meyer Lamarr disclosed that the defendant had been married to the latter prior to the two marriages pleaded. 

Defendant contended that he could not be convicted under the allegations of the information since the lawful marriage was that to Wally Meyer Lamarr. 

The court considers People vs. Spitzer, 57 Cal. App. 593 where a similar contention was made but calls attention that there the alleged third and prior marriage was in fact not established and says, "There is a fundamental difference between the proposition that proof of a valid previously contracted and existing marriage is absolutely essential as a basis for guilt of bigamy on subsequently contracting another marriage and the theory that proof of the invalidity of the first marriage charged in the information, through establishing a prior existing marriage requires acquittal. 

A misnomer in the information, of the spouse, marriage to whom established the status of the defendant, is not necessarily. fatal to the judgment." 

..... "It is not to be implied. . . . that the state could allege as a lawful marriage a defendant's marriage to B and a subsequent bigamous marriage to C and then upon proof by the defendant or otherwise that he had in past previously married A and that the marriage to B took place during the existence of the marriage to A thereupon claim a conviction on the charge as laid on the theory that the B marriage was the bigamous one. On that theory the defendant would be entitled to an acquittal on the ground of variance." (and, as pointed out, the C marriage might still be valid if contracted after the marriage to A terminated.) (Here follows an able discussion of authorities here and elsewhere. C.W.F.) Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Lamarr, 20 Cal. (2d) 705.

BURGLARY 

Appellant was employed as a salesman by Sears, Roebuck & Co. and devised a scheme whereby he procured other persons to enter the store and obtain goods from him for less than the regular price and appropriated the money received to his own use. As an additional inducement he permitted such purchasers to receive other merchandise without charge. Defendant and one of these "purchasers" were charged with burglary, the case dismissed as to the purchaser Coman so he might be used as a prosecution witness and defendant was convicted. "When Coman entered the store he did so with intent to aid and abet appellant in the crime of theft. This entry, therefore, was with the intent required to be shown in order to establish the crime of burglary. Since appellant and Coman were both principals in the nefarious scheme the felonious entry of Coman was imputed to appellant." 

People vs. Sparks, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 748

Proof that certain personal property was in a cabin, that the door had been knocked off by drilling the lock and the personal property was missing held sufficient to show burglary. 

People vs. Blain, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 844

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Where defendants were neither the owners nor entitled to the possession of the premises in question "they were not entitled to make an issue as to the ownership or possession" of the premises.

People vs. La Marr, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 24 BURGLARY-PLEADING 

Defendant was charged with feloniously entering "a cabin belonging to one Walter Bidstrup" with intent to commit larceny. It appeared that Bidstrup owned several cabins and appellant contended that the information was defective in failing to designate which cabin was entered. Information held sufficient. 

People vs. Blain, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 844

BURGLARY WITH EXPLOSIVES 

Evidence showing defendants entered a safe by the use of an oxyacetylene torch held sufficient to sustain a conviction. 

People vs. Wilson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 218

CERTIORARI, WRIT OF 

"Since an appeal to the Superior Court may be taken from a judgment of conviction in a justices' court, the writ of certiorari may not be used as a means of challenging the judgment of the lower court." "Since no further appeal lies from the judgment of the Superior Court (on such appeal C.W.F.) . . . . the writ of certiorari is assailable as a means of challenging the jurisdiction of that court." 

Portnoy vs. Superior Court,, 20 Cal. (2d) 375.

CHILD STEALING-GENERAL 

A seventeen year old girl was walking along a little used road on her way to school. Defendant came along in an automobile and jumped out, grabbed her and ordered her to get into the car. In the struggle the girl succeeded in getting away. Held that the intent must be determined from the circumstances. "The fact that the girl was able to escape has no bearing upon the inference of an intent to detain and conceal which may reasonably be drawn from the evidence." Conviction affirmed. 

People vs. Palmer, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 697 The word "conceal" does not necessarily mean that the concealed individual or hidden object may not be located or found by reasonable means of discovery." Writing to the mother of the children that he intended to keep the children, without disclosing where they were does not necessarily refute the intention of concealing them from the mother. 

People vs. McGinnis, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 1054

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

17 

CHILD STEALING-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court refused an instruction that even though defendant intended to conceal the child from its parents they could not convict unless such intent to conceal was accompanied by an intent to detain him. The court did give an instruction properly defining the offense. Held that since the jury was fully and fairly informed of the elements of the offense, there was no error.

People vs. Casagranda, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 818 CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE-GENERAL 

"Since the decision in People vs. Newland, 15 Cal. (2d) 678, the rule applicable in cases of circumstantial evidence has been definitely established. 

If the circumstances shown in evidence reasonably justify the verdict of the jury, the opinion that those circumstances might also reasonably be reconciled with the innocence of defendant will not warrant interference by a reviewing court. (People vs. Miller, 41 Cal. App. (2d) 252)."

People vs. Arnold, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 11 (See further discussion under NEW TRIAL-INSUFFICIENCY OF EVIDENCE.) CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE-INSTRUTIONS 

"The instructions in question were properly refused on the ground given therefor by the trial court-that the evidence in this case is basically direct and positive and not circumstantial."

People vs. Hannon, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 484 CONFIDENTIAL INSTRUCTIONS 

While on the witness stand a police officer stated that he had made a police report of a conversation had by him with the complaining witness and that he had such report in his possession. The witness did not, however, use the report while on the stand. "The report was confidential and not admissable in evidence" and the court did not err in refusing to permit the defense to examine the report.

People vs. Santora, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 707 CONFESSIONS-GENERAL 

The signed written confession of a defendant is admissible without the testimony of the shorthand reporter who took and transcribed the statement. 

People vs. Young, 17 Cal. App. (2d) 484 

Where the testimony contradicting defendant's testimony, that his alleged confession was secured through the use of violence, is in places contradictory and uncertain and defendant's testimony named as participants three officers who did not deny the use of force, the circumstances strongly indicating that force was used and the only other evidence implicating defendant was the testimony of a witness whose testimony implicating two others was held by the court to be so unreliable that said two were found, not guilty by the court sitting without a jury, conviction reversed. 

People vs. Williams, 20 Cal. (2d) 273

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

(Appellant was found guilty and sentenced to the death penalty. C.W.F.) "Hoyt's challenge that his statement or confession was improperly admitted in evidence because initially he was not apprised of his right to counsel or his right to remain silent if he desired and that his statement could be used against him, lacks merit." 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306

At the preliminary examination the magistrate refused to permit defendant to put on evidence that the confession was not free and voluntary but in the Superior Court defendant was permitted to do so. Conviction affirmed. 

People vs. Neal, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 455 "In the absence of a showing of abuse of discretion upon the part of the trial judge we cannot say that his ruling was error. It was for the trial judge to determine whether the confession was voluntary. In ruling upon the admissibility of statements purporting to constitute the confession of an accused, the court is governed largely by the circumstances surrounding the purported confession. The fact that it may have been made to an arresting officer or that the accused had been interrogated many hours does not render his statements involuntary." 

People vs. McEvers, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 548

Undoubtedly portions of the statements or confessions were admissible even if it be conceded that they were involuntary and were obtained by duress, or hope of reward or immunity from punishment for the reason that the defendants themselves directed the officers to the places where the stolen articles had been concealed by them and to persons to whom they had sold other portions of the stolen property. Physical facts and circumstances corroborated their confessions in this respect and the rule is that where physical facts and circumstances so corroborate confessions of guilt the reason of the rule which would otherwise exclude involuntary confessions to this extent ceases to exist. 

People vs. Costello, 194 Cal. 595, 601

CONFESSIONS-FOUNDATION 

Defendant charged with lewd acts upon several children made a confession. One of the officers had stated to defendant that he would bring as many as thirty children to bear witness against defendant. Defendant argued that this constituted a threat and that he was induced thereby to confess to save the children from embarrassment. Held that this was "not the sort of threat or intimidation that would have rendered the confession involuntary had it been acted upon." "It must be apparent that a confession dictated by the hopes and motives of the accused and inspired wholly by his own mental processes and emotions is voluntary in a legal sense. It is not the pressure of the circumstances surrounding an accused person, but pressure exerted by others tending to influence his actions that is to be weighed in determining whether his confession of guilt has been made voluntarily or under the compulsion of external influences." 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

19 1 

CONFESSIONS-INSTRUCTIONS 

"If the state of the evidence is such that it admits of no rational conclusion other than that the confession was freely and voluntarily made, there is no necessity for instructions as to the governing rules of law with relation to the receipt and consideration of confessions, and that the failure of the court to give such instructions of its own motion is not error.” (Quoted from People vs. Dye, 119 Cal. App. 262, and followed.) 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

CONSCIOUSNESS OF GUILT-GENERAL 

After the death caused by an abortion performed by defendant the latter stated to one John Rosa that she would give $50 or $75 to help out with the funeral. Rosa left his hat at defendant's residence and on the following Monday returned and reclaimed his hat from a woman who was accustomed to answer the door for defendant and in the hat was an envelope containing $50 in currency. Evidence held admissible. 

People vs. Wilson, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 434

CONSCIOUSNESS OF GUILT-FALSEHOOD 

Where defendant, in his statement to the deputy sheriff, stated that on one occasion he had found deceased in bed with his wife evidence was admissible to show that this statement was false.

People vs. Amaya, 44 Cal. App. (2) 656. 

CONSCIOUSNESS OF GUILT-FLIGHT 

Evidence of flight of the accused iş admissible not as a part of the res gestae but as indication of a guilty mind. 

People vs. Santora, 51 Cal. App. (2) 707 CONSCIOUSNESS OF GUILT-FLIGHT- INSTRUCTIONS 

The court gave an instruction in the language of Section 1127c of the Penal Code. Held that this was a "correct statement of law" and "it was unnecessary to give any other instruction upon the doctrine of flight." 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2) 265, 270. (Held also that earlier cases are superseded by the code section.) 

In giving the instruction Section 1127c Penal Code was not strictly followed, the court saying "The flight of a person after the commission of a crime... omitting the "immediately" before the word "after." Held not prejudicial. 

People vs. Soldavina, 45 Cal. App. (2) 460. 

CONSPIRACY-GENERAL 

It is not necessary that there be direct evidence of the conspiracy nor need the defendant, to be liable for the acts of the conspiracy by reason of being a co-conspirator, be present at the time the acts are committed. 

People vs. McCarthy, 18, Cal. App. 551.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Appellants were charged and convicted of conspiracy to violate the California Small Loan Act in making loans in violation of that Act and in a separate count with engaging in the small loan business without a license. 

Appellants contended that since the violations of the Act would only be misdemeanors that the Superior Court had no jurisdiction as the case fell within subdivision 5 of Section 182 of the Penal Code covering offenses against public morals to pervert and obstruct justice and the due administration of the law, and the Small Loan Act not being part of the Penal Code the conspiracy to violate it would only be a misdemeanor of which the Superior Court would not have jurisdiction. 

Held that unless the offense constituting the object of the conspiracy is punishable under the provisions of the Penal Code the jurisdiction is controlled by the second penal paragraph of Section 182 and that violations of the Small Loan Act are provided for in that Act and not in the Penal Code; that if the object of the conspiracy is a misdemeanor and denounced by the Penal Code the conspiracy is punishable as a misdemeanor but if not the conspiracy is punishable as a felony under the second penal paragraph of 

Section 182. 

People vs. Vanderpool, 45 Cal. App. (2) 685, 20 Cal. (2) 782.

Defendants may be charged and convicted of both the conspiracy to commit a crime (robbery here) and the crime (robbery) constituting the consummated object of the conspiracy. 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2) 306.

Where two persons are jointly charged with robbery conspiracy is not necessarily involved and the charge not involving the elements of the crime of conspiracy the acquittal of one is not an acquittal of the other. 

People vs. Wilson, 54 Cal. App. (2) 520.

The agreement is the gist of the crime of conspiracy and this situation is not changed by the statute requiring the the performance of an overt act. 

Purchasing stamps, to mail poison in pursuance of a conspiracy to commit murder, though a lawful act would be sufficient as an overt act. 

People vs. Corica, 55 Cal. App. (2) 165. 

"It is not necessary that the purpose of the conspiracy be fully accomplished nor that each conspirator perform some overt act. It is sufficient if one conspirator commit an overt act in carrying out the purpose of the conspiracy thus making all conspirators liable to punishment." 

People vs. Corica, 55 Cal. App. (2) 165. 

CONSPIRACY-ACTS AND DECLARATIONS OF CO-CONSPIRATORS 

The common design of a conspiracy "may extend in point of time beyond the actual commission of the act constituting the crime for which the accused is being tried, such as for the purpose of concealing the crime, securing the proceeds thereof, sharing or dividing the proceeds of the crime, or bribing or influencing witnesses, and that consequently, evidence is admissible to prove acts committed after as well as before the perpetration of the crime for which the accused is on trial. Of course, it must reasonably appear that such acts were committed in furtherance of the common design of the conspiracy . . . 

People vs. Suter, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 444.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

21 Upon a trial for conspiracy evidence of conversations and acts of the conspirators are not inadmissible solely because they occurred more than three years prior to the return of the indictment. 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 87. 

"It is true that some of the acts of the appellants relied upon as proof that they were implicated in the alleged conspiracy occurred after the money and property had been stolen. That evidence was, however, competent for the reason that the conspiracy in the present case included not only the stealing of the money and the cash book from Mr. Brennan, but also a division of the money between the conspirators and the payment of a specified sum of about $200 to Lyle Ross for perpetrating the crime." 

People vs. Ross, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 385.

Defendant told Magusin and Ardolino that he had had trouble with "his girl" and that if they would go to her house and ask for her furs and jewelry that he had bought for her she would deliver them because she would be scared. Defendant drove to the house of Mrs. Knechtel, who in fact was not and had not been his "girl," pointed to the house and told Ardolino and Magusin, who had followed in another car of defendant, to go in. This they did, entering with guns, and robbed the woman of clothing, furs and jewelry which defendant later placed in a private garage. "The jury could reasonably draw the conclusion that defendant conspired with Ardolino and Magusin to burglarize and rob Mrs. Knechtel. . . . . 

It was within the province of the jury to determine from all the evidence whether the crimes charged were the natural and probable consequence of the unlawful design of the conspirators. As a conspirator defendant was liable for all the natural and probable consequences incident to the commission of the act which he had advised." 

People vs. Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 411. 

CONSPIRACY-EVIDENCE 

While it is the general rule that evidence of the acts and declarations of co-conspirators should follow proof of the existence of the conspiracy such rule is not absolute and where, at the conclusion of the trial, there is ample evidence of the conspiracy no prejudice is created by varying the general rule as to order of proof. 

People vs. Suter, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 444. "Participation in a conspiracy, however, can be shown by circumstantial evidence." 

People vs. Gonzales, 20 Cal. (2d) 165.

CONSPIRACY-INSTRUCTIONS 

Instructions on conspiracy are proper, even though the crime of conspiracy is not charged, if there is evidence that defendants committed the crime charged in pursuance of a conspiracy. 

People vs. Fluvnerfelt, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

An instruction to the effect that a conspiracy need not be the result of an express agreement but may result from the actions of the defendants in mutually carrying out a common purpose to achieve an unlawful end "correctly stated the law." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

22 An instruction that to convict of a conspiracy to commit two named crimes the jury must acquit if the jury find that defendant conspired to commit only one of the crimes is properly refused. In such event defendant would still be guilty of conspiracy. 

People vs. Marvin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 180.

CONSPIRACY-PLEADING 

In charging a conspiracy to commit certain named crimes, such crimes need not be set forth with particularity as to times, places and persons. 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 87.

An indictment charged that in carrying out the conspiracy "certain" of the defendants, without naming or identifying them, would engage in certain described unlawful activities and defendants conspired that "certain" of their number would engage in such activities. Held sufficient and that it was not necessary to name or identify the "certain" defendants nor to state what particular part each conspirator was to play in carrying out the conspiracy. 

People vs. Corica, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 165. 

Under our simplified forms of criminal pleading it was unnecessary to allege just what particular part each conspirator was to play in carrying out the conspiracy.

People vs. Corica, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 165. 

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW-EQUAL PROTECTION OF THE LAW 

While a law unjustly discriminating against one class of persons is unconstitutional, it is not a defense to a charge that other persons have violated the same law and have not been prosecuted. "The remedy for the unequal enforcement of the law in such instances does not lie in the exoneration of the guilty at the expense of society." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

A law, not discriminatory on its face, is not unconstitutional because in its application it is not applied equally in all cases, as where under 644 of the Penal Code all cases are not adjudicated alike. 

People vs. Keilly, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 1012. 

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW-LOCAL LAW ON SAME SUBJECT AS STATE LAW 

Riverside County passed an ordinance against gambling, pool selling and bookmaking. "The rule is well settled that a county or city has the power under section 11 of article XI of the Constitution to enact police regulations and ordinances promoting considerations of public welfare, public morals, public health and public safety, provides that such regulations are not in conflict with the general laws. The only way the legislature can inhibit local legislative bodies from enacting rules and police regulation is by the state itself occuping the same legislative field so completely that legislation on the same subject by local legislative bodies will necessarily be inconsistent with the state act. . . There may be different regulations without a conflict.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

23 ...

Where the legislature has assumed to regulate a given course of conduct by prohibitory enactments, a county or municipality with subordinate power to act in the matter may make such new and additional regulations in aid and furtherances of the purposes of the general law as may seem fit and appropriate to the necessities of the particular locality and which are not in themselves unreasonable." "While the ordinance may include, among the acts denounced, some of the acts punishable by state law, the fact that it includes, by way of description, games prohibited by statute, as well as those not so prohibited, does not affect the validity of the entire ordinance. But all games so prohibited by statute must be excluded from the operation of the ordinance." 

Portney vs. Superior Court, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 833.

CONTINUANCE-GENERAL 

(See also: Dismissal for Failure to Try in Time)

Where nine defendants were jointly charged held proper to continue the case beyond the sixty day period so that two of the defendants, then on trial in the federal court, could be tried with the other seven. 

Ferenz vs. Superior Court, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 797.

CONTINUANCE-ABSENCE OF WITNESS 

No attempt was made to subpoena the witnesses for appellant, whose principal defense was an alibi until shortly before the trial and it was then discovered that several such witnesses could not be located. Denial of continuance held not an abuse of discretion. 

People vs. Jones, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 845.

CONTINUANCE FOR PREPARATION 

At the time case was set for trial defendant was represented by attorney Sanford Sill. On the day of the trial defendant appeared in court with attorney Charles M. Kilpatrick but his counsel Sill was absent. A continuance was requested because his new counsel had just been brought into the case. 

The court phoned attorney Sill who appeared and moved that attorney Kilpatrick be associated with him in the trial. The motion was granted. 

Motion for continuance was again made upon the ground that neither counsel was prepared. The motion was denied and the case went to trial. "We have examined the reporters transcript in this case and find that both counsel appeared to be familiar with the case and with considerable ability cross-examined witnesses." Held no error. 

People vs. Shaw, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 768.

CONTINUANCE-MOTION FOR NEW TRIAL 

"An appellate court will not consider alleged errors of the trial court in refusing to grant a continuance of a hearing of a motion for a new trial, unless the motion for a continunace is supported by affidavits which would warrant the court in granting the motion." 

People vs. McClain, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 445.

CRIMINAL DIGEST CONVERSATION, PROOF FOR PART OF 

Evidence was received as to what appellant was heard, by a bystander, to say as his end of a telephone conversation. The appellate court first concludes that the portion of the conversation heard was "intelligible and pertinent to the case.' Neither party to the telephone conversation testified. 

Held that the evidence was admissible. (The court says this case is distinguishable from People vs. Rabalete, 28 Cal. App. (2d) 480, as in that case nothing was presented which establishes the pertinency of the portions of the conversation heard to the charges on trial, keeping a bookmaking establishment and receiving a bet on a horse race. (337a P. C., subds. 2 and 3). In that case defendant was heard to say "242 to show "and was seen to receive what appeared to be a dollar bill and to make a notation on a piece of paper and there was also evidence that on that day a horse numbered 242 was racing at the New Orleans Fair Grounds as shown by a racing form found on the counter of defendant's bookstore where the incident occurred. It seems to the writer that the language in the Robalete case was "intelligible and pertinent to the case when taken in connection with all the other facts and circumstances in evidence." The Rabalete case, as well as the case at bar, was decided by the same court, opinion by the same judge, but in neither case is People vs. Daniels, 105 Cal. 262, 265, cited, though that case in laying down the rule uses the identical phrase "intelligible and pertinent to the case." People vs. Rabalete, therefore, must be considered as recognizing the rule in the Daniels case as does also the case at bar, and the Rabalete case must be accounted for on the theory that the words there heard by the witness were considered by the appellate court to be not "intelligible and pertinent to the case." C. W. F.) 

People vs. Suter, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 444. "There was no error in the admission of part of a telephone conversation heard by a witness. The part heard by the witness appears intelligible and the witness was properly allowed to testify to the part he heard, namely that part carried on by appellant." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

CORAM NOBIS 

Application for the writ cannot be made before judgment. 

People vs. Vivian, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 163.

CORPORATE SECURITIES ACT 

Appellant urged a reversal of the judgment on the ground that there was no proof that his codefendant, the actual seller of the securities which appellant persuaded complainant to buy, did not possess a permit from the Corporation Commissioner. The evidence established the fact that it was through appellant that the securities were sold and that he aided his codefendant in their sale and was not the agent of the purchaser. The only evidence as to knowledge was appellant's testimony that he did not know his codefendant did not have a permit to sell. "But this does not excuse him for committing the acts charged against him which in plain and positive language are denounced and forbidden by the statute the burden rested upon appellant to ascertain at his peril whether his acts were in violation of the statute."

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

25 25 

People vs. Flumerfelt, 35 Cal. App. (2d) 495, distinguished as there it was held that defendant was entitled to show that in selling the stock she relied upon information from her attorney that a permit had been issued and that she acted in good faith in relying on such information while in the case at bar there was no showing that defendant had received information that a permit had been issued. 

People vs. Stowell, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 580. "The inclusion in the statutes of the words 'certificate of interest or participation,' in addition to the certificates of interest which are otherwise specifically mentioned, indicates an intention to include some other certificates of interest as securities."

People vs. Marr, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 38. 

Flumerfelt and Ardelle, as copartners, were interested in a machine for the recovery of gold in mine dumps or tailings. A corporation was planned to use the machine and in consideration of the transfer of the machine to the corporation appellants King and Cartwright agreed to secure funds to acquire a dump. All parties were to receive “promotion stock." The sale of the stock to the public was arranged so that in return for his money the investor received King's personal note and a promise of two shares of stock for every dollar invested. At the same time the investor signed a document, retained by the corporation, in which he declared himself about to become associated with others for the purpose of forming American Metals Recovery Corporation (the name of the corporation) and agreeing to advance the designated money to help finance its organization and to take its corporate note for the amount when the corporation was completed. Fantastic statements of returns as high as two hundred per cent a month were made to prospective investors together with various false representations as to ownership of mine dump properties and past results in gold extraction. No permit was ever obtained to sell stock. Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Flumerfelt, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 1. 

"The jury was justified in inferring that the corporation was simply a means used by Allen and Dibert to assist them in selling this worthless stock to the public. There are many cases holding that under such circumstances a sale of the stock in this state without a permit violates the Cor porate Securities Act." 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735. "The element of selling or issuing a security plays no part in the statutory definition of a security." 

People vs. Marvin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 180. "A deed to mineral rights may or may not be a security. The determination of its true character requires an inquiry which goes beyond the mere name of the instrument or the nature of the interest conveyed. In deciding whether a given instrument is a security the courts have invariably looked through mere form to substance. (Many cases cited)." Held there was evidence that while the documents were "conveyances of definite interests in real property, they were intended by both seller and buyer to transfer rights to participate in earnings or profits in the nature of landowner's oil royalties that might inure to the benefit of numerous landowner lessors under community leases and securities." 

Moore vs. Stella, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 766.

CRIMINAL DIGEST CORPORATE SECURITIES ACT-INSTRUCTIONS 

Where the facts warrant it is proper for the court to instruct the jury that the documents in question constituted securities as a matter of law. 

People vs. Marvin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 180.

The court instructed the jury that good faith or advice of counsel affords no immunity to one accused of a violation of the Corporate Securities Act. Held that while this might be improper in a case where there was evidence that counsel had told defendant that a permit had been issued or where personal stock is sold on advice of counsel the instruction was not improper in the case at bar where it appeared that defendants sold securities after they knew that the permit to sell the same had expired. 

People vs. Marvin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 180. 

CORPORATE SECURITIES ACT-PLEADING 

Indictment attacked on motion to set aside Judgment on ground that court had no jurisdiction because indictment failed to state a public offense. 

Pleading in the language of the statute held sufficient. 

People vs. Marr, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

CORPUS DELICTI 

Upon a trial for murder it appeared that the body of deceased was recovered by dragging the Sacramento river, was clad only in bathing trunks and was found about 850 feet from where deceased's clothing was found on the bank. No mark was found on the body except a scratch on the head. An autopsy surgeon testified that in his opinion deceased came to his death by drowning, that he was partially or more probably totally unconscious when his body entered the water, a conclusion based upon the condition of the heart and lungs and the finding of traces of chloral hydrate in the stomach. Held sufficient to establish the corpus delicti. "To prove a prima facie case of corpus delicti, all that was required was to show a reasonable probability that a criminal act of another had been the direct cause of the death of Sherrard." 

People vs. Ives, 17 Cal. (2d) 493.

Slight or prima facie proof of the corpus delicti is sufficient to permit the introduction in evidence of a confession or admission. 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802; Roche, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 590.

Circumstances held sufficient to prove corpus delicti of a murderdeath resulting from a blow on the face. 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802. "It is not necessary that such proof (i. e. of the corpus delicti C. W. F.) be made before the extra-judicial statements of the appellant were heard, or that such evidence be taken in any particular order." 

People vs. Housman, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 619.

Statement and declarations of the defendant can not be used toward proof of the corpus delicti.

People vs. Davis, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 331. 

The corpus delicti need not be proved beyond a reasonable doubt; prima facie proof is sufficient. 

People vs. Davis, supra.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

27 The corpus delicti can be established by the testimony of an accomplice and corroboration is not required. (Note: It is only as to defendant being a guilty party that there must be corroboration. C. W. F. 

People vs. White, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 90.

The corpus delicti must be proved by evidence outside of the extrajudicial admissions of the defendant but slight or prima facie proof of the corpus delicti is sufficient to render such declarations admissible. 

People vs. Seymour, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 341.

CORPUS DELICTI-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court instructed the jury that: "Proof of the corpus delicti means proof of the essential elements constituting the crime charged and of the existence of criminal agency or means as the cause of them. And it may be proven by circumstances shown in evidence, or by inference drawn from facts proven. "The evidence of the corpus delicti need not be of conclusive character, and need not necessarily connect the party informed against with the commission of the crime, in order to justify the admission and consideration of the confession of such party so charged with the crime. "It is not necessary that the corpus delicti should be proved beyond a reasonable doubt before proof may be made of an extra-judicial confession. 

Prima facie evidence of the corpus delicti is sufficient; and it is for the court to say whether there is sufficient evidence of the corpus delicti to go to the jury, precisely as in the case of any other material fact.” 

Held confusing, susceptible of a construction that prima facie evidence alone was required to establish the elements of the offense, that the court had determined that the proof of the crime independent of the confession was sufficient and that the jury must abide by that decision and prejudicial The court says, "The rule of the law stated in the instruction is exclusively one for the guidance of the court during the trial.” error. 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

An instruction that admissions are not sufficient to convict unless there be other proof that the defendant committed the offense charged is properly refused as not the law, since the corpus delicti does not include identity of perpetrator, nor is it the law that the corpus delicti and defendant's guilt must be established beyond a reasonable doubt before the admissions of a defendant can be considered. 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

COUNSEL 

"The matter of granting appellant Wilson permission to dismiss his counsel and defend in person was likewise one addressed to the sound discretion of the court." (Note: The court here, after several requests by defendant and his counsel, permitted counsel to withdraw and defendant to act as his own counsel. The case therefore is not authority that the court could deny such a request though circumstances in a particular case might warrant such a denial. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Wilson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 218.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"To hold that a defendant charged with crime has an absolute right to counsel of his own selection, with unlimited right to insist upon continuances of his trial would be subversive of the prompt administration and execution of the laws upon which depends largely their effectiveness. (Above remark appropos of defendant on the day of trial desiring to change counsel and also secure a continuance upon the ground that counsel coming into the case was not prepared to go to trial.) 

People vs. Shaw, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 768.

Where defendant was informed by the court 10 days before his trial that his attorney was in jail and that he had the right to stay with him or to secure other counsel. On the day of trial defendant appeared with his attorney and sought to substitute another attorney but the court refused to permit this unless new counsel were prepared to go ahead with the trial. 

The original attorney then said he was prepared to proceed. The case was continued for four days and the motion to substitute counsel was renewed though proposed new counsel stated he was not prepared. Finally defendant pleaded guilty and at no time thereafter moved for leave to withdraw that plea.

The court refers to Section 1050 of the Penal Code and holds the court acted properly. 

People vs. Whinnery, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 899.

CROSS-EXAMINATION-GENERAL 

In cross-examination of a witness for a defendant on trial for selling marihuana the prosecutor asked him whether he had had any trouble with the police officers in any way in connection with narcotics. Held "clearly incompetent," the court basing its decision on the proposition that impeachment is limited to prior felony conviction. (Note: The opinion does not disclose what the direct examination disclosed. Depending on that the question may have been proper as bearing upon the bias of the witness against the prosecution by reason of prior conflicts with the law particularly since the question was as to "the" police officers, evidently meaning the officers in the case. Also, the questioning may have been leading up to the point that the witness may have been an accomplice of defendant. Unfortunately the opinion doesn't state what evidence the witness gave. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Duvernay, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 823.

CROSS-EXAMINATION-BIAS, ETC. 

On cross-examination of a codefendant testifying for the prosecution questions whether he knew that he wouldn't be prosecuted and whether he expected probation are proper. Error in sustaining objections held not prejudicial. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1. "It is an unquestioned rule of evidence that when any witness admits bias and prejudice on cross-examination, on redirect the reasons for such prejudice cannot be gone into, at least where such reasons involve other alleged offenses outside the issues."

People vs. Zemansky, 20 Cal. (2d) 56.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

209 

CROSS-EXAMINATION OF CHARACTER WITNESS 

Character witnesses may be cross-examined as to their knowledge of reports of specific acts or charges inconsistent with the good character of the defendant as testified to by such witnesses. 

After a witness had testified to defendant's good reputation for decency and morality he was asked if he knew that certain immoral pictures had been found in defendant's room and was then shown certain pictures and then asked whether he had not heard that defendant took a young boy to his room and showed him the pictures. Held that, assuming it was improper to show the pictures to the witness, any prejudice was removed by the court's admonition to the jury that defendant was on trial only for the offenses charged and that mere questions are not evidence. (The question was not answered). 

People vs. Haley, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 618.

In a prosecution for robbery wherein defendant produced witnesses who testified to his good character for honesty it was error to permit their being asked on cross-examination whether their opinion would be the same if they knew that he had been arrested for assault with a deadly weapon and also for carrying concealed weapons since the traits of character of the latter offenses do not include that of honesty and are not inconsistent with a good reputation for honesty. Error held, however, not prejudicial. 

People vs. Hernandez, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 132.

CROSS-EXAMINATION OF DEFENDANT 

Defendant was charged with the theft of two automobiles. There was evidence tending to prove that defendant had purchased a wrecked car and had used parts of the stolen cars to repair the wrecked car. Held that merely because defendant denied the theft of the two cars did not preclude his being asked on cross-examination as to the number and kind of cars he had fixed up for sale. 

People vs. Smith, 43 Cal. App. (2) 110.

Defendant may be asked on cross-examination as to his prior convictions of felony and the nature thereof. 

People vs. Vivian, 50 Cal. App. (2) 662. 

CUSTOM AND USAGE 

"A usage is operative upon parties to a transaction where and only where they are manifest an assent to each other that it shall be operative, or where one of the parties knows or has reason to know that the other party intends to be governed by the usage, or where the usage exists in such transactions and is generally known by persons under similar circumstances." 

People vs. Seymour, 54 Cal. App. (2) 341.

DATE OF OFFENSE-GENERAL 

"The general rule, with respect to proof of the time when an offense is committed, is that there is no fatal variance from the allegation that it was committed on a particular date, to show that it was actually committed on or about or near that date, unless the variance results in misleading the defendant so as to prevent him from making his defense to the charge or to deprive him of the benefits of a plea of former jeopardy in the event of another trial for the same offense." 

People vs. Tracy, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 564.

CRIMINAL DIGEST DATE OF OFFENSE-INSTRUCTIONS 

In all but one of the court's instructions the court referred to the offense as charged to have been committed "on or about the 20th of April, 1940." 

The court, however, gave one instruction which stated, in concluding, "if you convict the defendant you must convict because you find that he committed the act charged upon the 20th day of April, 1940." The proof showed that the offense was committed about 4 A. M., April 21, 1940. Held that the erroneous instruction did not tend to mislead the jurors and there was no ground for a reversal. 

People vs. Andrew, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

An instruction that it was immaterial on what day the offense was committed, without specifically advising the jurors that they must all agree upon the same incidents as the basis for their verdict, is reversible error where the evidence tends to establish several offenses, on separate dates, all of the same character. 

People vs. McMillan, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 821. 

DECLARATIONS OF DEFENDANT (See also: ACCUSATORY STATEMENTS, ADMISSIONS, CONFES- SIONS, RES GESTATE.) DISMISSAL-GENERAL 

A dismissal during the trial of a count of rape does not operate as an acquittal of a count of violation of section 288, even if the offenses were connected in their commission. 

People vs. Kearney, 20 Cal. (2d) 461. 

(See also: SEPARATE OFFENSES; VERDICTS-INCONSISTENT.) 

A dismissal of a count charging receiving stolen property does not affect a conviction of robbery and burglary based on the same set of facts. 

People vs. Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 411. 

DISMISSAL-FAILURE TO TRY IN TIME 

Where all the continuances, beyond those caused by appellant's escape and absence from the jurisdiction were made either at his request or with his consent he cannot complain of the delay in bringing the case to trial. 

People vs. Santora, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 707.

The seven petitioners were on May 5, 1942, jointly indicted with Robert Noble and Ellis O. Jones. The trial was first set by consent for July 20th, and reset by consent for July 6th on which date the prosecution moved to continue the trial to July 21st, upon the ground that Noble and Jones were then on trial in the Federal Court and their attendance could not be procured. The court continued the case to July 21st, over opposition and on 

July 21st the prosecution moved for a continuance to July 28th upon the ground that the federal trial had not been completed. The case was continued to July 28th. Subsequently petitioners moved for a dismissal under 

Section 1382 of the Penal Code and, that being denied, sought relief by mandamus. The court holds that there was good cause for continuance beyond the 60 day period.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

31 "It does not necessarily follow that a defendant who has not been brought to trial within sixty days from the finding of the indictment or the filing of the information shall be entitled to a dismissal of the charge for lack of diligence in the prosecution of the case." 

Ferenz vs. Sup. Ct., 53 Cal. App. (2d) 797.

DRAWING CHECK WITHOUT FUNDS 

"Section 476a provides for separate offenses of 'drawing' or 'uttering' or delivering a check upon a bank where the maker has insufficient funds with which to pay it with the intent to defraud another person.” 

Where defendant uttered or passed in Sacramento a check drawn upon a bank in Merced, the actual passing being not by defendant but by a coconspirator, the jurisdiction was properly laid in Sacramento County. 

People vs. Descant, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 343.

Defendant cashed checks on a bank in which he had overdrawn his account ten weeks previous. The manager of the branch bank on which the checks were drawn testified that he knew defendant, that he knew the latter had no credit arrangement with the bank and as to the state of the account. 

Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Dunkin, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 120.

DYING DECLARATIONS 

Defendant was charged with murder in causing death through an illegal abortion. Deceased was told by her attending physician that she was going to die and that he wished her to make a statement to a member of the District Attorney's office. Another doctor also told her she was going to die. A priest told her she was in danger of death and that she should prepare by receiving the last rites of the Church and she had the rites administered. Deceased made a statement to the effect that defendant had caused her condition. Held that while deceased did not declare that she expected to die and was without hope of recovery, the court properly admitted the dying declaration in evidence. 

People vs. Wilson, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 553.

In such case the victim is not an accomplice and the law does not require that the dying declaration be corroborated even though in a prosecution for abortion the victim must be corroborated. 

People vs. Wilson, supra.

ELECTION OFFENSES 

A motion requiring the district attorney to elect upon which act he will rely is made prematurely if made before an opening statement by the district attorney and before the introduction of any evidence as there is nothing before the court upon which the motion could be predicated. 

People vs. Fremont, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 341.

The prosecution is not obliged to elect to designate the exact date upon which it relies in proof of the commission of the offense. 

People vs. Tracy, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 460.

CRIMINAL DIGEST ENTRAPMENT 

The mere fact that, after being introduced to defendant, a police officer gave a defendant money with which to procure marihuana which defendant then did, the transaction being obviously that of a willing seller, does not make a case of entrapment. 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App (2d) 690.

ESCAPE Petitioner, while an inmate of the County Jail upon conviction of a misdemeanor and while on the county road gang, escaped therefrom and upon conviction of escape was sentenced to state prison. The court recites the splitting of former section 107 of the Penal Code into two parts one of which retained the old number and the other, covering escapes of prisoners, being given the section number 4532.

Petitioner claimed that that section did not apply to misdemeanor prisoners. The court calls attention to the fact that as to escape of prisoners the section does not differ materially except as to punishment from section 107 as construed in In re Haines, 195 Cal. 605, to include misdemeanor prisoners, that the Legislature is presumed to have known of the decisions when it enacted section 4532 in practically the language of 107. Held that the law includes the escape of misdemeanor prisoners. 

In re Holcomb, 21 Cal. (2d) 108. 

In re Haynes, 21 Cal. (2d) 114. 

In re Petrie, 22 Cal. (2d) 115. 

People vs. Smillie, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 447.

EXHIBITS 

Where guns are used in the commission of an offense, evidence that the guns produced at the trial are similar to those used in the perpetration of the crime, as well as the guns themselves, is admissible in evidence. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 265.

At the time four guns, found in defendant's apartment at the time of his arrest, were received in evidence the district attorney directly stated that it was not contended that these four guns were used in the shooting out of which the prosecution arose, and that "they are certainly different guns." Held that, though it be conceded that such evidence was inadmissible, defendant was not prejudiced and there was no miscarriage of justice. (Note: As this was a gang killing and evidence of the possession of four guns by defendant might well have affected the jury, the case goes pretty far. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 270.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

33 Where an exhibit (as here part of the stolen property) is not produced in court it is proper to receive testimony describing such property including a price mark stamped thereon. Such evidence is admissible to identify the property but not for the purpose of fixing value. 

People vs. Latham, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 35.

There is no objection to a witness describing an exhibit subsequently received in evidence or describing two guns which, if present in court would have been admissible as exhibits. 

People vs. Best, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 100.

Where the money stolen in the robbery consisted mainly of small change taken from the cash register, a bag of coins found in appellant's car at the time of his arrest shortly after the robbery held admissible even though the coins could not be identified as having been taken in the robbery. 

People vs. Harsch, 44 Cal. App. 572. 

Any physical object related to the fact in dispute may be exhibited to the jury or its existence, character and nature proved by witnesses and the admission of such evidence "must be regulated by the sound discretion of the court."

Code of Civil Procedure, Sec. 1954. 

EXPERIMENTS 

Where there was evidence that members of the conspiracy had secured metallic potassium to be used to start fires in pursuance of the objects of the conspiracy an experiment, conducted in the court room, to demonstrate how that metal coming in contact with water would burst into violent flame was admissible. 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 87.

The trial court refused to permit defendant to walk as an example of his natural style for the benefit of a prosecution witness then under crossexamination. Held that this was a matter within the judge's discretion and that there was no error. 

People vs. Mead, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 91. 

A demonstration or test of fingerprint expert, with fingerprints of jurymen, held proper. 

Moon vs. State, 22 Ariz. 418; 16 A.L.R. 362.

EXPERT TESTIMONY 

While a police officer familiar with bookmaking may testify as to the custom or manner in which bookmakers operate and the paraphernalia used, this does not permit such witness to testify as to the meaning of letters and numbers written upon a slip of paper by defendant as such testimony would be mere surmise and the expression of an opinion upon a matter not the subject of expert testimony.

People vs. Davis, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 331. 

Where the qualifications of a police officer as an expert on bookmaking paraphernalia was stipulated testimony that the markings were such as are customarily put on scratch sheets and betting markers by bookmakers is proper.

People vs. Cohen, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 506. 

People vs. Kelley, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 150.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Where a police officer has become familiar with the papers, books and paraphernalia ordinarily used by bookmakers "he is qualified to testify as to the custom or manner in which bookmakers operated and it was proper to permit the officer to explain the nature and character of the papers taken from appellant and his place of business and to state his opinion with reference to the significance attaching to the letters and figures which appeared thereon." 

People vs. Allen, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 74. 

Where a doctor had testified that an examination of the prosecutrix had disclosed that her sexual organs had been penetrated but did not disclose whether it had been by a male organ or some object used in masturbation, it was improper to permit the doctor to testify that in his opinion the condition was due to sexual intercourse and not masturbation since such opinion was not that of an expert but was based upon his belief of the story of the prosecutrix. 

People vs. Lee, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 199.

While testimony of a police officer expert as to the manner in which bookmakers keep their records may not legally be introduced in the form of an opinion by the witness as to what certain papers were found and what certain marks thereon indicated, held not ground for a reversal where the other evidence amply warranted a conviction. One justice dissents on the strength of People vs. Davis, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 331, 334. 

People vs. Pruitt, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 316.

EXPERT TESTIMONY-INSTRUCTIONS 

Where the court has given the instruction in the language of section 1127b of the Penal Code this is sufficient and it is not error to refuse to give further instructions on the subject. 

People vs. Walsh, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 164.

FAILURE TO PROVIDE
