Defendant, divorced from his wife who was also guardian of the minor child in question, earned an average of $50 a month. Held that it was not necessary to prove that defendant had actually abandoned or deserted his child; that defendant could not claim lawful excuse because of inability to pay; that defendant's offer to pay made after he had violated the statute was not a defense. 

People vs. Norton, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 139.

Defendant earned good wages as a painter except when he failed to obtain or retain his employment by reason of the excessive use of intoxicating liquor, averaging over $160 per month for at least three months of the six months covered by the information. Defendant turned over to his wife not to exceed $50 a month, a sum insufficient to provide rent, utilities and necessaries. Conviction of failure to provide for two minor children sustained.

People vs. Dorius, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 364.

35 

FORGERY 

Where defendant had forged a check signing it with the fictitious name "Warren F. Clark, M. D.," the court properly excluded evidence that defendant had made arrangements with the bank to open an account under the name of W. F. Clark, also not defendant's true name.

People vs. Kauffman, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 393. 

Changing the name of the grantee in a deed and using the deed to obtain money held forgery. 

People vs. Hall, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 386.

FORMER JEOPARDY-GENERAL 

No evidence was introduced to sustain the plea of former jeopardy. 

Held that, therefore, there was no occasion for the court, trying the case without a jury, to give judgment on the issue tendered by the plea and the finding of the defendant to be guilty was an implied finding against the plea. 

In re Swanson, 46 Cal. App. 814.

FORMER JEOPARDY-FORMER ACQUITTAL OR CONVICTION 

Conviction of battery may bar a subsequent prosecution for attempt to commit rape. See this case under Separate Offenses. 

People vs. Mendoza, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 707.

GAMBLING 

Defendant was found in a single apartment, partly dressed. On the table were two telephones, betting markers, scratch sheets for the day on which the arrest was made and for a day a week earlier. While the officers were there they twice answered the telephones and the parties at the other end of the line made requests to place a bet on a certain horse. Defendant testified he had been granted the use of the apartment by "Brownie" but that he had no connection with any betting. Defendant had occupied the apartment for two weeks.

Held that it is not the actual making of bets but the occupancy of a place with equipment for recording bets which constitutes a violation of subdivision 2 of section 337a of the Penal Code. 

Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Kobakoff, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 170.

Evidence clearly sufficient under subdivision 2, section 337a Penal Code. 

People vs. Joffe, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 233.

An officer familiar with the methods, practices and procedure of bookmakers cannot testify as to the meaning of notations written by defendant on a slip of paper as that would be mere surmise and not expert opinion since what the letters and numerals meant was known only to the person who made them. In the light of this rule held the corpus delicti on a violation of section 337a, subdivision 2, is held not established. (See also: EXPERT 

TESTIMONY.) People vs. Davis, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 331.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"Examining the state laws relating to lotteries and gambling we find none which penalize the possession of a lottery device as such 

The bare possession or control of slot machines is not penalized by section 330a. ... In short, there is no statute in California that has been called to our attention that we have been able to find that vests in the court authority to declare a forfeiture in a case such as we are considering." (Slot machines in defendant's possession for storage or repair.) 

Chapman vs. Aggeler, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 848.

An officer qualified as an expert may testify that the markings on scratch sheets and betting markers are such as are customarily used in bookmaking. 

People vs. Cohen, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 459.

The use to which the premises were put may be established by the condition of the premises, documents found, telephone calls by persons attempting to make bets and the attempt of defendant to conceal evidence. 

People vs. Cohen, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 459.

Defendant Whalen leased certain premises, the lease limiting his occupancy to the use of the premises "as a brokerage room for the placing of bets on horse races." After the place opened for business a police officer in civilian clothes went in and told defendant Wittmayer that he desired to place a certain bet on a horse race. The officer was given a scratch sheet and signed a card as "principal" which authorized Wittmayer to act as his agent in procuring for him a mutuel ticket within the Bay Meadows race track, to return the ticket if the horse failed to win or collect the winnings less a commission, and received a receipt from Wittmayer. 

Appellants claimed error because the court did not instruct the jury that they could not be convicted of violating section 337a of the Penal Code if they acted as agents and personally placed bets at the track through the lawful pari-mutuel machines, basing this claim upon the 1935 amendment to section 3 of the Horse Racing Act (now chapter 4, sections 1900 to 19663 of division 8 of the Business and Professions Code). Held that the Act as amended in 1935 did not repeal section 337a and that the prohibition of bookmaking is still in force and (following People vs. Walker, 11 Cal. (2d) 

464) that 'the actions of a commercial solicitor of bets, operating outside of the track, receiving bets from others and never placing them himself in the manner authorized by the Act, do not come within the protection of the 1935 amendment and such actions are punishable under the provisions of the Penal Code, "but that the case at bar is distinguishable as the issue here is "whether an agent who solicits wagers on a commercial basis outside the enclosure of a race track and thereafter personally places the bets in a parimutuel machine at the track is protected by the exception set out in the 1935 amendment so that he is not guilty of a violation of section 337a of the Penal Code." Held that such a case was not protected by the 1935 amendment and that defendants were guilty of violating section 337a. 

People vs. Haughey, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 506. 

Evidence of finding blank A. B. C. betting markers in defendant's apartment, telephone wires found cut and when reconnected some 50 telephone calls came in, listened to by one of the officers, each from a patron stating a bet he wished to place on a horse race being run that day held sufficient to sustain a conviction for violating sub. 2, section 337a of the Penal Code. 

People vs. Kelley, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 150.

Conviction of keeping a place for bookmaking sustained. 

People vs. Shapiro, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 60.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

37 Defendant was charged, under section 318 of the Penal Code with through invitation and device prevailing upon one Walton to visit a place "kept for the purpose of gambling." The place in question was a "social club" which maintained a building for the purpose of enabling its members to play poker for money. The court says that the games conducted undoubtedly constituted gambling not unlawful under any statute or ordinance. 

Defendant, secretary of the club, wrote a letter to Walton soliciting his membership in the club, as the result of which Walton paid the club a visit. 

Held that the section was violated whether the gambling was lawful or unlawful and that the fact that the club operated under a license from the City of Gardena did not relieve defendant of guilt under section 318. 

People vs. Philbin, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 859.

Evidence that offer observed defendant writing on a large sheet of paper with a magazine and "scratch sheets" before him and when he noticed officer hid the sheets in the magazine and went to the rear of the cafe, that the white sheet was a betting marker and corresponded to the scratch sheet for the races that day held sufficient to convict defendant of registering a bet.

People vs. Buckendorff, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 595. 

Conviction under sub. 3, section 337a P. C., sustained. 

People vs. Abraham, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 701. On a charge of keeping a place for recording bets on horse races (sub. 

2, sec. 337a P. C.): "It is not a necessary element of the offense . . . . that a bet be recorded" and "it is not a requirement that it be established that there was a race or purported race." "It is the keeping or occupancy of a room or place with paper, papers or paraphernalia for the purpose of recording bets on horse races, among other kinds of contests, that is denounced by subdivision 2 of said section 337a."

Proof of the purpose for which the place is kept need not be by direct but may be by circumstantial evidence. 

People vs. Allen, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 74.

Evidence held sufficient to sustain a conviction under sub. 2 section 

337a Penal Code. 

People vs. Vertlieb, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 848.

Evidence held sufficient to sustain a conviction under sub. 2, section 

337a Penal Code, keeping a place for the purpose of receiving bets. 

People vs. Pruitt, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 317.

GRAND JURY 

"A grand jury's function is to return an indictment against a person only when the evidence presented to it indicates that he has committed a public offense. . . . “A grand jury which indicts a person when no evidence has been presented to connect him with the coinmission of the crime charged excedes the authority conferred upon it by the Constitution and laws of the State of California, and encroaches upon the right of a person to be free from prosecution for crime unless there is some rational ground for assuming the possibility that he is guilty. Such an indictment is void and confers no

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

jurisdiction upon a court to try a person for the offense charged. It has long been settled in most jurisdictions that an indictment is invalid if it is unsupported by any evidence before the grand jury. If there is some evidence to support the indictment, the courts will not inquire into its sufficiency but the lack of any evidence conclusively establishes that the grand jury has exceeded its authority in returning an indictment." (The court here mentions In Re Kennedy, 144 Cal. 634, in which the court, where an indictment was based on the uncorroborated testimony of an accomplice, declined to interfere. As to Brobeck vs. Superior Court, 182 

Cal. 289, where the court held that prohibition would not lie where the indictment was not supported by any evidence before the grand jury the court in the case at bar calls attention to the fact that now, under 925 of the Penal Code, a transcript of the evidence before the grand jury must be given to the defendant and the unimpeachability of an indictment under the common law, because of absolute secrecy and the fact that the court had no access to the evidence, no longer exists.) Held prohibition will lie. 

Greenberg vs. Superior Court, 19 Cal. (2d) 319.

HABEAS CORPUS-GENERAL 

A petition for habeas corpus based upon the same grounds as a previous petition presented to the same court will be denied. 

In re Miller, 17 Cal. (2d) 774.

Where defendant was not present at his trial for a misdemeanor, he not having knowledge of the trial and not having waived his presence, he will as against the conviction be released on habeas corpus from imprisonment under the conviction. 

In re Tipton, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 374.

HABEAS CORPUS-COMPLAINT, SUFFICIENCY 

A misdemeanor complaint charging larceny of property "from the immediate possession of the lawful owner thereof," is good as against an attack on habeas corpus. 

In re Clark, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 683.

HABEAS CORPUS-CONSTITUTIONALITY OF STATUTES AND ORDINANCES 

"While a few courts require that all available remedies by appeal be exhausted before habeas corpus can be invoked to test constitutionality, most jurisdictions, including California, do not make the requirement mandatory and even permit the issue of constitutionality to be raised by habeas corpus before trial. Since the granting of a writ of habeas corpus, however, results in the release of the petitioner, while reversal on appeal may result merely in a new trial with the exclusion of those charges found based on unconstitutional enactments or the inclusion of that procedure found constitutionally guaranteed, the court may in its discretion refuse to grant the writ if the remedy by appeal is not exhausted."

The ordinance must be judged on its face to determine whether it is unconstitutional. 

In re Bell, 19 Cal. (2d) 488.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

39 "When a petitioner has been convicted of violating a statute that is entirely unconsitutional, the court will ordinarily issue a writ of habeas corpus releasing him from custody. The present ordinance, however, contains a valid provision prohibiting acts of violence. The complaint charges pétitioners with picketing by acts of violence and therefore charges a public offense for such acts are not consistent with lawful action. 

As defendants were charged with violating any one "and/or" any other provision of the ordinance and were in effect charged with violating all of the provisions and it could not be determined from the face of the record which particular provisions they were convicted of and, one of the provisions being valid, "The ambiguity of the judgment in the present case would thus clearly warrant a reversal of the conviction on appeal or other direct attack. 

A habeas corpus proceeding, however, is in the nature of a collateral attack, and a judgment that is collaterally attacked carries with it a presumption of regularity. . . . The presumption, however, is not conclusive, but places upon petitioners the burden of proving that their convictions were based not upon the constitutional but upon the unconstitutional provisions of the ordinance."

Held that in such case petitioners were not confined to the face of the record and that the actual evidence at the trial could be considered by the court and that even additional evidence might be submitted to establish what the verdict was and whether it violated constitutional guarantees. Held also that petitioners had failed to sustain the burden of proving that they were not convicted of violating the valid provision of the ordinance. 

In re Bell, 19 Cal. (2d) 488

Constitutionality of an ordinance upon which is based a conviction in justice's court, affirmed on appeal by superior court, may be tested on habeas corpus. 

Portnoy vs. Superior Court, 20 Cal. (2d) 375. 

HABEAS CORPUS-FORMER JEOPARDY 

Where there is absolutely no showing that any evidence was introduced at the trial to sustain the plea of former jeopardy and the petition is based upon the failure of the court, trying the case without a jury, which found defendant guilty, to give a judgment or make a finding on the plea of former jeopardy held that the appellate court was without power to issue the writ. 

In re Swanson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 814.

HABITUAL CRIMINALS 

... "In order to invoke the operation of the habitual criminal statute the prosecution in addition to alleging and proving the fact of the prior conviction must allege and prove that the accused has served a term of imprisonment in a state prison or federal penitentiary for the particular felony and that the failure to allege or prove such servitude is fatal to the adjudication, unless it appears.from the record in some manner that the accused has admitted having served such term of imprisonment or that the court in its judgment has so found." "The law does not require allegation or proof of imprisonment for the full term; service of part time is sufficient to bring the case within the purview of the statute."

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

People vs. Murray, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 209.

Where defendant is on parole from state prison at the time of committing another offense such prior conviction and service of part of the term of imprisonment is sufficient conviction and service of a term to bring it within the habitual criminal law. It is not necessary that defendant shall have served the entire term in prison. 

People vs. Keilly, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 1012.

Evidence is not admissible, to establish that Section 644 of the Penal Code permits discrimination, that in its application the law is not uniformly administered. The discrimination affecting constitutionality is discrimination which appears on the face of a statute. 

People vs. Keilly, supra. 

HABITUAL CRIMINAL-PLEADING 

An allegation that defendant following a prior conviction "was received at San Quentin prison" is not sufficient to charge that he served a term of imprisonment in that institution. 

People vs. Murray, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 209.

HEARSAY 

"Statements made by others are no less hearsay when made in the presence of a defendant." (Note: In other words, if evidence is inadmissible under the hearsay rule when the statements are made in the absence of the defendant, they are equally admissible if made in the presence of the defendant. Of course there are exceptions such as the accusatory statement, etc. C.W.F.) 

People vs. White, 44 Cal. App. 183.

IDENTIFICATION AND IDENTITY-PERSONS 

The manager and one of the clerks of the store testified that appellant was one of the robbers. At the preliminary examination the manager gave testimony showing he had some doubt as to the identification of defendant and the clerk had admitted the possibility that he might be mistaken. Held that the question was settled by the jury whose duty it was to weigh the evidence. 

People vs. Addington, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 591. "Nor need the identification of the defendant be positive." 

People vs. White, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 183. "Defendant refers to the fact that the lower part of the robber's face was covered with a cloth. This, however, does not nullify their testimony to the effect that they recognized him as the perpetrator of the crime." 

People vs. Knight, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 887. 

Even though the victim of a theft from the person could not identify defendant such identification can be fully established by another witness. 

People vs. Corsalini, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 704.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

41 Witnesses testified that the robber who was masked, the upper part of his face covered with a cloth, was "similar in appearance" to defendant Schunke and that his "height, slope of his forehead and his general body build" were the main things that resembled defendant. Defendant's left hand was deformed. The witness testified that the masked man kept his left hand in his pocket and used his elbow in going through a doorway. On the day after the robbery, Schunke paid $200 on an automobile. Evidence held sufficient to identify Schunke as one of the robbers. 

People vs. Schunke, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 542.

In considering the evidence as to the identity of defendant as the perpetrator of a kidnaping in Count I, the appellate court takes into consideration that the evidence under another count for kidnaping a different girl showed a similar method, were committed in the same vicinity and "within a comparatively brief period of time". (One offense was committed 

Nov. 10, 1940, and the other July 2, 1941.) 

People vs. Hunter, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 243.

Identification of defendant, by his two victims, as the person who committed the robbery held sufficient to sustain the verdict and that discrepancies in the description of the two bandits and their clothing was a matter for the trial jury. 

People vs. Stein, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 289.

IDENTIFICATION AND IDENTITY-PROPERTY 

A pistol, stated by the victim of the robbery as looking like in general size, color and appearance and resembling the gun held by one of the robbers is sufficiently identified to be admissible in evidence. 

People vs. L'Hommedieu, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 27. 

Flashlights and goggles found in the car occupied by defendants the night after the robbery and identified as similar to those used in the robbery are admissible. "With regard to the latter it is not necessary that such objects be positively identified. It is sufficient if they are recognized as similar or bear a sufficient resemblance to remove the elements of mere speculation or surmise." 

People vs. White, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 183.

IMMUNITY 

Petitioner, a deputy sheriff, had appeared before the grand jury which was conducting an investigation concerning the crime of bribery and had given testimony. Thereafter the grand jury indicted petitioner for bribery. 

He thereupon brought these proceedings to restrain the superior court from trying him on said charge contending that since part of his testimony and evidence before the grand jury related to gaming he had immunity by reason of Section 334 of the Penal Code, which provides that no person is disqualified from testifying "concerning the offense of gaming on the ground that such testimony may criminate him, but no prosecution can afterwards be had against him for any offense concerning which he testified."

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"Insofar as any evidence concerning gaming was given by the petitioner before the grand jury, it was confined to proof that illegal gaming existed within his district to his knowledge. As we have seen, this evidence was offered not for the purpose of prosecuting the person guilty of violating the gaming laws, but as a foundation upon which to predicate the charge of bribery for which crime petitioner was subsequently indicted. No protection is afforded a witness testifying in an investigation when the subject of the investigation is bribery. It is confined solely to those witnesses who testify in a case where the defendant is charged with gaming or in an investigation of the offense of gaming. Petitioners testimony was not given in either of such proceedings. He is therefore not protected from prosecution based upon the indictment charging him with the crime of forgery." 

Rittenhouse vs. Sup. Ct., 20 Cal. (2d) 71.

IMPEACHMENT-GENERAL 

A witness who was with defendant at the time of the fatal accident testified that she herself was "cold sober." Held that it was permissible "for the purposes of impeachment" to prove on rebuttal that shortly after the accident she reacted positively to the Rhomberg test, was unable to walk a straight line without swaying, that her eyes did not react properly to light, her speech was thick and her breath alcoholic. 

People vs. Crowe, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666.

IMPEACHMENT-CONTRADICTORY STATEMENTS-GENERAL 

Where the witness admits making the prior contradictory statement there is no reason for proof that the witness made such statement and it is not error to exclude such further proof. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42, Cal. App. (2d) 270.

Where the witness had testified that defendant had been "cold sober" it was proper to show on cross-examination that the witness had on previous occasions made contradictory statements as to the amount of beer defendant had consumed. 

People vs. Crowe, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666.

General rule stated and applied. 

People vs. Brazil, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 754. 

IMPEACHMENT-CONTRADICTORY STATEMENTS-EXPLANATION OF 

Held that defendant was not entitled to explain inconsistent statements made by him to the police officers where he had denied having made such statements. (Obviously a witness cannot explain something which he testifies he did not say. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Briggs, 20 Cal. (2d) 42.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

43 

IMPEACHMENT-CONTRADICTORY STATEMENTS—IMMATERIAL MATTERS 

Upon a trial for murder in which the evidence showed defendant had a co-principal the court refused to allow a witness to be impeached by proof that, before the grand jury, he had testified that he was unable to describe or identify the second man. "One of the issues in the present case was whether defendant participated in the murder and it was entirely immaterial for the purpose of this trial, assuming that the evidence disclosed that he did, who his partner in crime might be." (Note: The court recognizes and cites authority for the rule that a witness may not be impeached upon "irrelevant and collateral matter." The case should be used as a precedent with great caution as, under a different set of facts, the rule might be otherwise. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2) 270. 

IMPEACHMENT-CONVICTION OF FELONY 

Defendant may, on cross-examination, be asked as to his prior felony convictions. "Such facts could properly be shown by cross-examination or the record of the judgments.” 

People vs. Vivian, 50 Cal. App. (2) 533.

IMPEACHMENT-IMPROPER 

In a prosecution for rape evidence that prosecutrix had had sexual intercourse with other men was not admissible as showing a want of credibility in her testimony.

Statutory: People vs. Vivian, 50 Cal. App. (2) 533. 

Forcible: People vs. Murphy, 53 Cal. App. 474.

In a prosecution of defendant for statutory rape upon his stepdaughter the prosecution introduced evidence tending to show that defendant was probably not married to a former wife, that three children were illegitimate and that defendant had frequently been charged with failure to support them. Held prejudicial error.

People vs. Zemavasky, 20 Cal. (2) 56. 

"A witness cannot be impeached on an immaterial or collateral matter." 

People vs. Burness, 53 Cal. App. (2) 234.

IMPEACHMENT-REBUTTAL OF 

In a prosecution under Section 288 of the Penal Code an attempt was made by the defense to create an inference that the prosecutrix, defendant's daughter, had given her testimony in revenge for alleged acts of unkindness on his part. The court, over objection, thereafter permitted a deputy sheriff to testify to the conversation she had had with the prosecutrix at the commencement of her investigatioin. "It is a well known rule that, under ordinary circumstances, prior extrajudicial statements of a witness consistent with his testimony on the stand

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

will not be admitted for the purpose of bolstering such testimony. But the exception to the rule is that such evidence is proper to rebut inferences created by the cross-examination of the witness and to rehabilitate his evidence following attempts to impeach him by endeavoring to show that his testimony is actuated by base or ulterior motives, or that his testimony in court is of recent fabrication." 

People vs. Nobles, 44 Cal. App (2) 422.

INDICTMENT-GENERAL 

A writ of prohibition will lie to prevent the trial of a defendant under an indictment where there was no evidence before the grand jury even remotely supporting the charges. See this case quoted under Grand Jury. 

Greenberg vs. Sup. Ct., 19 Cal. (2) 319.

INDICTMENT AND INFORMATION-GENERAL 

"Section 952 of the Penal Code was passed to simplify pleading in criminal cases and to remove many technicalities formerly required in them. 

The various provisions of the section are permissive and directory and not mandatory. They do not direct what must be alleged in a pleading but indicate rather broadly the form a pleading may follow." ... 

People vs. Price, 46 Cal. App. (2) 59.

It is not necessary that the information or indictment specifically state whether the offense charged is a felony or a misdemeanor. 

People vs. Brown 52 Cal. App. (2) 428.

In a charge of receiving stolen property the property was described as "five wheels and tires". Held that if defendant needed more particularity of averment the remedy was by demurrer and not having demurred he is deemed to have waived the defect. (Note: The court overlooked the fact that the details appeared in the transcript of the preliminary examination furnished to defendant. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Burness, 53 Cal. App. (2) 234. 

People vs. Beesly, 119 Cal. App. 82, commenting on fact that accused is entitled to a copy of the transcript of the testimony forming the basis of the charge and that a defendant is entitled by the pleading simply to notice of the offense with which he is charged and not as to any particular circumstances, these being furnished by the transcript, cited and followed. 

People vs. Corica, 55 Cal. App. (2) 165. 

INDICTMENT AND INFORMATION-PLEADING IN CONJUNCTIVE 

Where the statute enumerates a number of different ways in which it may be violated such ways may all be set forth in the conjunctive and such pleading charges but one offense. 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2) 87.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

45 While criticising the practice of charging violation of the various provisions of an ordinance by setting forth such provisions united by "and/or” the court says that defendants "were in effect charged with violating all the provisions of . . . . the ordinance or any one provision." (Note, however, that the case was before the court on habeas corpus and not appeal. C.W.F.) 

In re Bell, 19 Cal. (2) 488. 

INDICTMENTS AND INFORMATIONS— CONSOLIDATION OF See also: INDICTMENT AND INFORMATION-JOINDER OF OFFENSES. 

"Section 954 of the Penal Code vests in the trial court the discretion of ordering consolidated for the purpose of trial two or more indictments where the offenses, as in this case, are of the same class." (Note: The syllabus indicates that the consolidation held proper by the court was that of "charges against two different defendants involving bank robberies of which two were committed by both and a third person on one occasion in one bank and the other was committed by one defendant with three others in a different bank. The opinion does not tell the nature of the defendants and charges in the indictments consolidated. If this case holds that a consolidation may be ordered where the parties defendant in the consolidated indictments are not the same, it is in conflict with prior decisions. C.W.F.)

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 265. 

By stipulation of counsel, the court consolidated two informations, one charging one defendant and the other charging two defendants. Held appellant could not complain as he had waived the privilege of a separate trial. 

People vs. Marvich, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 858.

Defendants Perona and Carson were held to answer and later defendant Thomas was held to answer under the same complaint. An information was filed under each commitment, and later, the trial court consolidated the two informations for trial. Conceding that Section 954 of the Penal Code does not authorize the consolidation of separate informations against different defendants even though the same offense is charged, "it has been definitely held in more recent cases that an error committed in this regard is not jurisdictional; that it is one of procedure, and that before it becomes a ground of reversal, it must appear that the consolidation has resulted in prejudice to some substantial right of the accused. (People vs. Aguinaldo, 3 Cal. App. (2d), 254; People vs. Shepherd, 14 Cal. App. (2d) 513." Trial court sustained. 

People vs. Thomas, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 128.

After the consolidation, an amended consolidated information was filed, and the court stated that it was not necessary to arraign defendants thereon and without objection the cause went to trial. Held that the trial was not illegal because of the failure to arraign defendants, that the consolidation was fully effective without the amended information which added nothing of substance, and that rearraignment would have been merely an idle act. 

People vs. Thomas, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 128.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Defendant was charged in each of several informations with robbery, kidnapping for the purpose of robbery and assault by force and violence upon one of the robbery victims. Held that the consolidation of the informations was proper. (Note: The court seems, however, to feel that the rule is regulated by the question as to whether the court abused its discretion in ordering the consolidation. Since under Section 954 of the Penal Code an indictment or information may charge two or more different offenses, if of the same class of offenses or connected in their commission, it would seem the principle of our law that joinder is permitted by statute and is not governed by judicial discretion, and that the permission given by Section 954 to consolidate separate indictments and informations is purely procedural and not a grant of discretionary power to regulate some right of the accused. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Duane, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 835; 21 Cal. (2d) 65.

Section 954 of the Penal Code authorizes the trial court to consolidate different informations charging defendant with offenses of the same class. (Here robbery and attempted robbery.) 

People vs. McClain, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 445.

INDICTMENT AND INFORMATION— PLEADING DATE OF OFFENSE 

The precise date or time of the offense need not be pleaded. 

People vs. Fremont. 47 Cal. App. (2d) 341.

INDICTMENT AND INFORMATION- JOINDER OF OFFENSES 

Where either by consolidation or original joinder, a count against several defendants is tried jointly with a count against a single defendant, the unconnected defendant or defendants may be prejudiced thereby. See: People vs. Duane, 21 Cal. (2d), 65, and cases cited. Subject of joinder and consolidation discussed.

INFAMOUS CRIME AGAINST NATURE-PLEADING 

The indictment charged that the crime was committed by having "carnal knowledge" of the body of the prosecutrix. "The term 'carnal knowledge' has been held to be synonymous with the phrase of sexual intercourse. (People vs. Carroll, 1 Cal. App. 2; People v. Allison, 25 Cal. App. 746; People vs. Hopwood, 130 Cal. App. 168.)"

People vs. Battalina, 52 Cal. App. (2) 685. 

A charge that defendant did feloniously have carnal knowledge of the body of the victim in violation of Section 286 of the Penal Code held sufficient. "On account of the degrading nature of the crime of sodomy it is uniformly held that it is not necessary to describe the offense with the same particularity which is required in other crimes." (Note: Here follows a discussion disclosing that the court is obviously not familiar with modern pleading. (C.W.F.) 

People vs. Battalina supra.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

47 While "carnal knowledge" includes sexual intercourse "that is not all that the term carnal knowledge includes. It is our opinion that sodomy may not be committed without experiencing carnal knowledge of the victim." 

Also held that, according to dictionary definition of "carnal" the term "covers carnal knowledge of the body of another person by either natural or unnatural copulation."

People vs. Battalina supra. 

INSANITY DEFENSE 

Defense counsel stated to the court that he desired to prove that in the past appellant suffered from a "mental ailment" which sometimes resulted in a loss of consciousness, that such ailment "may be and may not be insanity, that it is only akin to it" etc. The court says that the proferred testimony clearly "related to the alleged diseased mental condition of the defendant" and that whether the ailment was such that defendant suffered from insanity or "partial insanity" or to "an alleged abnormal mental condition" such evidence was not admissibile on the trial of the guilt issue." 

Note: This case seems to go too far. Section 1026 merely says that upon the trial of the issue raised by the plea of "not guilty" defendant shall be conclusively presumed to have been sane at the time of the offense but this obviously can not mean that the defendant shall be conclusively presumed to be free of a subnormal mental condition. Section 1026 is limited to the defense of insanity and cannot be stretched to include a mental condition other than insanity. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Brown, 43 Cal. App. (2) 430.

After finding defendant guilty of assault with a deadly weapon the jury disagreed upon the trial of the insanity defense and another jury was sworn to try the sole issue of not guilty by reason of insanity and returned a verdict finding defendant sane. Held "No substantial right of the defendant can therefore be prejudiced by this action of the trial court.' (Cases reviewed). "The jury having disagreed in the instant case on the issue presented by the second plea, it was as if there had been no trial on that issue." 

People vs. Messerly, 46 Cal. App. (2) 718.

Defendant requested that, in addition to the two alienists appointed by the court, another expert be appointed. Held there was no abuse of discretion in denying the request. 

People vs. Messerly, 46 Cal. App. (2) 718.

Defendant committed the homicide in July, 1937, and in October of that year, on an issue of present sanity, was found insane and sent to a hospital for the insane and declared sane and returned for trial in 1941. The same jury tried the issues under the plea of not guilty and the insanity plea. 

Held that, while this and other evidence was in conflict with the theory of sanity, there was ample evidence which warranted the jury's verdict that defendant was sane at the time of the murder. 

People vs. Coleman, 50 Cal. App. (2) 592.

Defendant introduced evidence that in 1937 he was placed in an insane asylum from which he escaped and some witnesses testified to acts tending to show he had been irrational at times, but three alienists testified that he was sane. Held this supported the court's finding that defendant was sane. 

People vs. Williams, 20 Cal. (2) 273.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Defendant, convicted of first degree murder carrying the death penalty contended that the jury should have had before it on the trial of the issue of not guilty the testimony on the insanity issue, it being defendant's theory that in following the procedure required by Section 1026 of the Penal Code the punishment should not be fixed until the jury had heard all of the testimony including that relating to defendant's sanity. 

The court reviews earlier decisions and particularly the dissenting opinions and affirms the judgment carrying the death penalty without any dissent. (Justice Houser, who did not participate, would no doubt have dissented had he been on the case. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Coleman, 20 Cal. (2) 399. 

Defendant interposed the sole plea of not guilty by reason of insanity and the court, sitting without a jury, found the defendant "not guilty by reason of insanity and further finds that the defendant is insane at the present time." Held that this established that defendant had not "fully recovered his sanity" and that he was properly committed to a state hospital for the insane as provided for in Section 1026 of the Penal Code. (Note: 

The statute provides that upon such an acquittal the court shall commit the defendant to a state hospital "unless it shall appear to the court that the defendant has fully recovered his sanity." Hence, unless the court makes such finding of sanity the court "shall" direct the confinement in such hospital. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Scarborough, 52 Cal. App. (2) 210.

INSANITY DEFENSE-INSTRUCTIONS 

Though no defense of insanity was interposed the court instructed the jury that the defendant should be conclusively presumed to be sane on the dates of the alleged offenses. There was, however, the testimony of a doctor to the effect that defendant had a mental illness but was not insane and a defense witness had volunteered the statement that she did not believe that anyone would have committed the (sex perversion) acts charged if he had been sane. Held that this was not objectionable as suggesting that defendant was relying on the insanity defense. Note: Instruction was eminently proper to meet the evidence referred to. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2) 49.

INSTRUCTIONS-GENERAL 

An instruction defining the offense charged which fails to include an essential element of the offense charged is error and, since it tends to convict a defendant on less than the corpus delicti is ground for reversal. 

People vs. Greco, 47 Cal. App. (2) 628.

After modifying some of defendant's instructions the court gave them and allowed all the instructions given to go to the jury room wtih the jury. 

Held no error. "If any of the jurors noticed the stricken portion they could only conclude that the court did not agree with appellant's counsel or the principle which they undertook to state and since in our opinion the court was right... no harm was done."

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2) 620.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

49 

INSTRUCTIONS-ABSTRACT 

Instructions not supported by facts proved are properly refused. 

People vs. Marvin, 48 Cal. App. (2) 180. 

People vs. Caldwell, 55 Cal. App. (2) 272.

There is no error in including in the instructions on impeachment the statement that a witness may be impeached by proof that he has been convicted of a felony even though there was no evidence of any witness having been so convicted. "That instruction was harmless."

People vs. Descant, 51 Cal. App. (2) 343. 

1 

INSTRUCTIONS-CAUTIONARY 

Defendant, in a statutory rape case, assigned error in the failure of the court to give a cautionary instruction in effect warning the jury of the danger of conviction on the testimony of the prosecutrix not corroborated. No such instruction was requested. The court points out that in People vs. Benson, 6 Cal. 221, where the failure to give a cautionary instruction at defendant's request was held prejudicial error, the unsupported testimony of the prosecutrix was so improbable of itself as to warrant the belief that the verdict was the result of prejudice or popular excitement; that in People vs. Lucas, 16 Cal. (2d) 178, 181, while the court said that defendant should have the benefit of a cautionary instruction the conviction was affirmed. Held here that defendant was not prejudiced by reason of the absence of a cautionary instruction. (Note: The court still overlooks that under Section 1127 P. C. counsel may not request instruction on fact. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Strangler, 18 Cal. (2d) 688.

The court in a case involving felonious assault, rape and 288a refused an instruction that "the evidence in a case of this kind is to be weighed by the utmost care, without bias or prejudice; since in this class of cases the accused is almost defenseless and ample opportunity is afford for the free play of malice and private vengeance." Held not error "where, as in the case before us, the story of the prosecuting witness is corroborated by other testimony." 

People vs. Bumbaugh, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 791.

An instruction "You are instructed to view with great care and caution the testimony of a child of immature years" held properly refused since a child of 12½ years old cannot be said to be of immature years. 

People vs. Putnam, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 666.

The court refused certain cautionary instructions upon a trial for violation of section 288 of the Penal Code and statutory rape committed upon two girls 9 and 14 years old "in which the jury would have been admonished of the dangers with which this class of prosecutions is fraught, of the ample opportunity for the free play of malice and private vengeance and of the fact that such cases are difficult to defend. In its opinion the court states that it is in accord with People vs. Benson, 6 Cal. 221, 223 but that in that case the testimony of the prosecutrix was inherently improbable. "However, where, as here, the testimony of the prosecuting witness is amply corroborated by direct and circumstantial evidence, it is not prejudicial error to refuse such cautionary instructions." The court adds that where there is not corroboration of the prosecuting witness, the cautionary instruction is not only proper but wholesome in the interests of justice.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

(Note: The court, though it was covered by the respondent's brief, pays no attention to the fact that under Section 1127 Penal Code, counsel may not request an instruction on a matter of fact, which a cautionary instruction is, and that the court may or may not in its discretion give a factual instruction. C. W. F. 

People vs. Roberts, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 558.

Appellant was convicted of committing a violation of Section 288 upon a 12 year old boy. The court refused to give a cautionary instruction as to the boy's testimony. The Supreme Court evidently felt a doubt as to the boy's testimony and the evidence, for, at the close of the opinion, it says: "In view of the circumscribed extent of the acts alleged and the inconsistencies in the witness' testimony, it is doubtful whether the same verdict would have been rendered had the cautionary instruction been given." Case reversed on the ground that, "While the trial court did not err in refusing the particular instruction requested, it does not follow that it was justified in failing to give an appropriate instruction." (The instruction refused was: "You are hereby instructed to view with great care and caution the testimony of a child of immature years. The refusal of this the court says was not error.)

The court does say however: "The defendant is entitled to have the jurors informed that the charges made against him are 'easily made and difficult to disprove' and that 'the testimony of the prosecuting witness should be examined with caution.' A simple admonition to view the testimony of the complaining witness with caution would be inadequate and misleading for it would carry the impression that for undisclosed reasons the trial judge distrusts the testimony of the particular witness." "There is no general rule that the testimony of children admittedly competent as witnesses is less trustworthy than the testimony of older persons; hence there is ordinarily no occasion for a cautionary instruction on that basis."

The court then declares the rule that it is the duty of the court of its own motion to charge the jury upon the law relating to the case. (This is obvious dicta since the instruction under discussion is not on a matter of law but on fact. C. W. F.) The court also says: "The requirement of cautionary instructions in cases like the present one is prompted by the same considerations that necessitate instructions with respect to corroborative evidence. (Again the court overlooks the difference between an instruction on fact and an instruction declaring the law which in certain cases makes corroboration necessary. C. W. F.) 

In fine the court adds that refusal of the cautionary instruction is not error when guilt is clear but is error where a different verdict would not have been improbable had such instruction been given. Conviction reversed. 

People vs. Putnam, 20 Cal. (2d) 927. (Note: The court again fails to recognize the law that, while the trial court must instruct on the law of the case, counsel may not request an instruction on a matter of fact, (Penal Code, Section 1127) and that while the court may, under the Constitution and statute, instruct on matters of fact, there is no law that the court shall or must do so, such instruction being wholly discretionary. The statement that such a charge is easy to make

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

51 is no more true in this case than in any other case in which a warrant may issue or an indictment returned upon the testimony of one witness. Neither does there seem to be any reason for stating that a sex charge is difficult to disprove. What is there which makes such a case more difficult to disprove than any other case in which there was no third person present? Is an alibi or physical inability more difficult to prove in such a case? We must not overlook either that the difficulty of disproof referred to in the early English cases, whence the language originated, was based largely upon the law which at that time prevented a witness from testifying. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Putnam, 20 Cal. (2d) 927.

People vs. Putnam, 20 Cal. (2d) 927, cited and approved with the statement, "Whether the refusal to give a cautionary instruction is prejudicial error depends upon the circumstances of each case and that in this case the failure to give the instruction was prejudicial." 

People vs. Williams, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 790.

INSTRUCTIONS-FACTS, ASSUMING 

An instruction that "if you are satisfied. . . . that the defendant Montgomery or any other conspirator in furtherance of the conspiracy" etc., does not assume the fact that defendant was a conspirator. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

INSTRUCTIONS-FACT, COMMENT ON 

Cautionary instruction on the defense of alibi held proper. See this case under ALIBI. 

People vs. Costello, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 517.

INSTRUCTIONS-FAILURE TO REQUEST 

Where not requested there is no error in failing to instruct on the law of confessions where there is no rational conclusion but that the confession was voluntary. 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84. 

(This case also under CONFESSIONS.) 

Where but one crime was committed but it was charged in three counts as grand theft, extortion and robbery the court was not bound, in the absence of a request, to instruct the jury that they could convict on only one count. 

People vs. Sigel, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 325.

INSTRUCTIONS-COVERED BY INSTRUCTIONS GIVEN 

It is not error for the trial judge to refuse an instruction where the rule therein stated is covered by the instructions given to the jury. 

People vs. Giovanni, 45 Cal. App. (2) 229.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

INSTRUCTIONS-INCONSISTENT OR CONFLICTING 

"When instructions are inconsistent or conflicting, such inconsistencies or conflicts constitute reversible error, if the substantial rights of the defendant have been affected. 

People vs. Kearney, 48 Cal. App. (2) 672.

INTENT 

The intent with which a defendant took the automobile of another is matter for the jury to determine from the circumstances and facts of the case.

People vs. Jeffries, 47 Cal. App. (2) 801. 

In a case where the girl victim was raped and then murdered, evidence that some months previous to the homicide defendant made the statement that that he would like to or intended to give deceased "the works," indicating something of a sexual nature, is admissible, if not as threats, as tending to show defendant's intent or state of mind toward deceased and the remoteness of such statement in time affects its weight and not its admissibility.

People vs. Mandell, 48 Cal. App. (2) 806. 

INTENT-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court defined the offense set out in section 288 of the Penal Code in the language of the statute and also defined the word "wilfully" in the language of subdivision 1, Section 7 of the Penal Code. Held that the latter instruction "in effect told the jury that, in order to constitute a violation of section 288, it was not required to find that the defendant had any specific intent." (Note: Since it was proper to define "wilfully," that word being used in the information, the latter instruction was proper. It would seem that, where the court correctly defined the offense in the first instruction and stated the necessary specific intent that there was no conflict in the instruction. C. W. F.)

The court also adds that the instruction that it was immaterial on what day or night the offense was committed provided the jury believed it was committed within three years prior to the filing of the information accented the error due to the conflict in the two other instructions. (I fail to follow this contention as the statute of limitations instruction is eminently proper in any case except where there has been a failure to elect which of several offenses proved the prosecution relies on and such failure has not been corrected by appropriate instruction. Note also that Moore, J. filed a lengthy and vigorous dissent. C. W. F.) Judge Moore in his dissent points out that the very fact that defendant performed the act wilfully was of itself proof of intent 

People vs. Kearney, 48 A. C. A. 672. 

BUT the Supreme Court, on hearing held the charge neither misleading nor improper and that the instructions must be read together and, when so read, fully and fairly advised the jury of the necessity of proof of both intention and wilfulness. 

People vs. Kearney, 20 Cal. (2d) 435.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

533 Held not error in a prosecution for child stealing to instruct the jury that one is presumed to intend to do that which he actually does and that defendant must have intended the natural and probable results of his own conduct. (Case not good authority, however, as the instruction was qualified by other instructions as act and specific intent; a case of single instance. 

C. W. F.) 

People vs. McGinnis, 95 Cal. App. (2d) 1054.

INTOXICATING LIQUOR-GENERAL Section 56.4 of the Alcoholic Beverage Control Act making it a misde meanor for a woman who was neither the on-sale licensee or the wife of such a licensee to mix alcoholic beverages at an on-sale place of business held valid. 

People vs. Jemnez, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 739.

INTOXICATION-INSTRUCTION 

Where the court instructed the jury in the language of Section 22 of the Penal Code it was not error to refuse an instruction that if the jury believed that defendant was so under the influence of intoxicants that he could not form a felonious intent he should be acquitted. 

People vs. Casagranda, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 818.

JUDGE DISQUALIFICATION OF 

Just before the hour set for pronouncing sentence defense counsel, because of his conduct in discussing the matter of sentence, was ordered out of the judge's chambers. Held insufficient to show bias or prejudice on the part of the judge. 

People vs. Murray, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 535.

JUDGMENT AND SENTENCE-GENERAL See also: HABITUAL CRIMINAL, SECOND OFFENSE. 

Defendant, charged with and convicted of both statutory rape and forcible rape based upon but one sexual act was sentenced to state prison on both counts. Held that these judgments, but one offense having been committed, should be modified by consolidating them into a single judgment. 

So ordered by appellate court. 

People vs. Craig, 17 Cal. (2d) 487.

Where there is a valid verdict convicting defendant of a failure to provide for a minor child, a judgment entered upon such verdict which does not specifically recite that such failure was wilful and without lawful excuse is not subject to attack. Defendant's remedy, if he desires such result, is to secure a correction of the record. 

People vs. Dorius, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 259.

CRIMINAL DIGEST JUDGMENT AND SENTENCE CONCURRENT OR CONSECUTIVE 

Upon an appeal the conviction was affirmed but the degree of the crime reduced to second degree murder and the cause remanded for modification of the original judgment in accordance with the decision on appeal. 

In repronouncing judgment and sentence the court added the provision that such sentence run consecutively with the felony sentence defendant was then serving under a previous conviction. Held that the court had no power to add this provision since the judgment and sentence after appeal was not a new one but only a modification of the original judgment and sentence and that, since more than sixty days had elapsed since the original judgment and sentence, the court was, in view of the provisions of 669 of the Penal Code, without power to order the sentences to run consecutively. 

People vs. Cowan, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 155. An order that two sentences for receiving stolen property should run consecutively held not an abuse of discretion in view of the fact that defendant had suffered five prior convictions of felony. 

People vs. Murray, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 535.

JUDGMENT AND SENTENCE-DEATH PENALTY 

In fixing and imposing a sentence of death after a plea of guilty to the charge of murder, the court stated that defendant had already killed two men, a statement which was not correct. It appeared however that in sentencing defendant to death rather than life imprisonment the judge did so not because of any previous crime but because in his opinion the judge felt that the evidence called for the death penalty rather than a life sentence. 

Judgment affirmed. 

People vs. Lininger, 17 Cal. (2d) 894.

JUDGMENT AND SENTENCE-PRIOR CONVICTION 

(See also: Habitual Criminal Second Offense)

Where a defendant has admitted a charged prior felony conviction it is sufficient if the judge in pronouncing judgment sentences defendant for the instant offense of which he has been convicted and in such case, the clerk in entering the judgment may under Section 1207 of the Penal Code state the fact of prior conviction. 

People vs. Shack, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 16. 

JUDICIAL NOTICE 

Where there was evidence that the crime was committed in Laguna Beach the court could properly take judicial notice of its location and could have properly instructed the jury that Laguna Beach was in Orange County. 

People vs. Andrew, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

55 

JURISDICTION-GENERAL 

Even a trial court has jurisdiction to determine the constitutionality of a statute or ordinance and it is not deprived of jurisdiction by virtue of the fact that it may decide the question erroneously. 

Held that certiorari would not lie to review the affirmance on appeal by the Superior Court of a conviction in justices' court but that the constitutionality of the statute upon which the conviction was based could be raised, after decision on such appeal, by habeas corpus. 

Portnoy vs. Sup. Ct., 20 Cal. (2d) 375.

JURISDICTION-ABSENT PRINCIPAL 

Defendant drew a check without funds on a bank in Merced, payable to one Bartlett, a co-conspirator in the scheme to pass worthless checks, who passed the check in Sacramento. Held that Sacramento County had jurisdiction the passing of the check having occurred there and, under Section 792 of the Penal Code jurisdiction of a prosecution against a principal not present is in the same county as it would be if he were present aiding and abetting. 

People vs. Descant, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 343.

JURISDICTION OF INFERIOR COURTS 

The police court of the City of Stockton does not have jurisdiction of the offense of contributing to the delinquency of a minor notwithstanding the charter provision that such court "shall have exclusive jurisdiction in all misdemeanors ..." committed within such city, such offense being one of which the Superior Court has trial jurisdiction. 

In re Guzman, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 359.

Complaint was filed in the Municipal Court of Los Angeles on charges of battery committed in Whittier Township. Thereafter, in mandamus proceedings, it was established that the Justice's Court of Whittier Township was of Class A. Defendant, after such determination was tried and sentenced in the Municipal Court. Held that upon the decision in the mandamus proceeding the Municipal Court was divested of jurisdiction and its proceedings thereafter were void. 

In re Cavitt, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 698.

Defendant contended that the Jusitce's Court of San Rafael Township was a Class A Justice's Court and hence it and not the Superior Court had jurisdiction of a prosecution under 270 of the Penal Code for failure to pro-. vide for a minor child. On January 31, 1941, at the time the information was filed San Rafael had a population of less than 30,000 and the Justice's Court was therefore a Class B Court (which would not have jurisdiction over said charge.) The results of the 1940 census were not announced until February 

18, 1941. Such results showed that on April 1, 1940, the population of the 

Township was 30,111. No determination of the census was ever made by mandamus proceedings. The court holds that for the purpose of determining the jurisdiction of the Justice's Court on January 1, 1941, the population of the township is to be deemed the population shown by the 1930 census, that the census used as a basis of jurisdiction is the last announced census and

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

that such jurisdiction, determined upon the basis of the last census is not affected by the fact that a subsequently announced census may disclose that a material change in population over the last census had taken place prior to the commencement of the prosecution, and that, hence, the Superior Court had jurisdiction.

People vs. Dorius, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 259. 

JURY-GENERAL 

Where it was stipulated that because of the probable nature of the evidence, all female members of the panel be excused, defendants consenting in person, the point cannot be raised on appeal that the jury was composed of men only. 

People vs. Crimin, 19 Cal. (2d) 315.

JURY-PEREMPTORY CHALLENGES 

A departure from the order of exercising preemptory challenges as prescribed in Section 1088 of the Penal Code is not ground for reversal where there is no showing of prejudice. 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 315.

JURY-VOIRE DIRE 

Question inquiring of a juror in a murder case whether, if satisfied beyond all reasonable doubt that the defendant was guilty of murder in the first degree, he had any conscientious scruples against the infliction of the death penalty “as to the defendant who did not actually participate in the killing" held permissible and, if answered in the affirmative, sufficient basis for allowance of a challenge. 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306.

JURY-WAIVER 

The right of trial by jury is one which may be waived. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 576.

No stereotyped language need be used by defendant as long as his intent to submit the case to the court without a jury appears. 

People vs. Bostio, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 690. 

JURY ROOM, TAKING EXHIBITS, ETC. TO 

Held no error to permit the jurors to take certain exhibits (checks) in evidence to the jury room. (Note:-The court refers to the practice as though it were purely discretionary with the court when in fact Section 1137 of the Penal Code provides that "Upon retiring for deliberation the jury may take with them all papers (except depositions) which have been re- Iceived in evidence . . ." The Appellate Court fails to mention this section but cites Code Civ. Pro. 612, identical in language with 1137 P. C. indicating that the court did not know of the existence of the Penal Code section. No cases cited. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Descant, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 343.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

57 There is no error in allowing the jury to take the instructions given to the jury room even though they include instructions requested by the defense which the court had modified by striking out portions thereof. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620.

JUVENILE COURT ACT 

A 15 year old girl left her home and went to the cottage of defendant and lived there for six weeks during two weeks of which another man, Wallace, lived in the cottage with defendant and the girl. During this two weeks defendant and the girl occupied the bedroom the door being closed. 

The girl stated to Wallace that she was defendant's step-sister. During the period defendant told a police officer and a brother of the girl, who were looking for her, that he did not know her whereabouts. Conviction of contributing to the delinquency of a minor sustained. "It is not necessary for the prosecutor to establish that defendant's acts or omissions resulted in the minor's actual entry upon an idle or immoral course of conduct. The prosecutor establishes his case when he proves acts or omissions which tend to cause or encourage the minor to lead an idle, dissolute, lewd or immoral life.” "The question whether the acts established by the prosecution were such as are branded criminal by Section 702 of the code was primarily for the determination of the jury and a reviewing court may not interfere with the verdict adverse to the defendant if the jury could reasonably conclude from the evidence that the defendant was guilty." 

People vs. Calkins, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 33.

Defendant Porello and two others were charged with robbery and attempted robbery. After the pleas were entered the cause as to Porello was, on April 16, transferred to the Juvenile Court for further proceedings by the judge sitting in a criminal trial, Department No. 44 of the Superior 

Court. The record did not disclose what action was taken by the Juvenile 

Court. On April 17th, in Department 44, which was not a Juvenile Court, Porello was allowed to withdraw his plea of not guilty to Count 1, admitted being armed with a deadly weapon, and the court found the robbery to be of the first degree upon a stipulation of facts. Appellant waived time for sentence and was allowed to file an application for probation. On the date set for the hearing the record showed that "The court recesses as a Criminal Court and reconvenes as a Juvenile Court. It is ordered that proceedings be suspended. . . . pursuant to the provisions of the Welfare and Institutions 

Code, Sections 834 and 835, defendants are now declared wards of the Juvenile Court under Subdivision M, Section 700 of said Code. It is further ordered that the defendants be committed to the Preston School of Industry during their minority cause continued to May 22, 1941 at 9:00

A. M. and transferred to Department 39 (Juvenile Court) for further hearing relative to financial responsibility and reimbursement . . ." Upon recommendation of the probation officer, it appearing that Porello was unfit due to physical disability to profit by the training school at Ione, the Judge of the Criminal Master Calendar Department set aside the order committing Porello to the Preston School and ordered that he be returned to Los Angeles County and, after his return, sentenced Porello to the state prison.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"We are persuaded that the Superior Court sitting in the exercise of its general jurisdiction was without authority to recess in such capacity, reconvene as a Juvenile Court and thereupon dispose of this case in the latter capacity."

The court further says that the court having found that defendant was 17 years of age and Section 825 of the Welfare and Institutions Code providing that no court shall have jurisdiction to try the case of any person under the age of eighteen years at the time of the alleged commission of the offense unless the matter has first been submitted to the Juvenile Court and said court has ordered that such person be prosecuted under the general law and that whenever it is suggested or appears that a person, charged by indictment or information was at the date of the offense under the age of eighteen years the judge of the Superior Court must forthwith certify the case to the Juvenile Court which may, if it deems the juvenile not a fit subject for juvenile procedure, return the case to the Superior Court which then may proceed without regard to defendant's age. Section 833 of the Code, while providing that a Superior Court may recess and reconvene as a Juvenile Court, further provides that if there are two or more judges of the Superior Court the procedure is different in that in such counties the case shall be transferred to the Juvenile Court Judge designated as such by the judges.

Also held that while Section 834 permits the Superior Court to recess as a Criminal Court and reconvene as a Juvenile Court, such authority is restricted to those cases in which defendant is over 18 and under 21 years of age.

Appellant had apparently never been certified as unfit by the Juvenile Court and the court holds that, his age having come to the court's attention, the Superior Court was without jurisdiction to dispose of the case. 

Judgment reversed and cause remanded with directions to proceed in accordance with Sections 825 and 826 of the Code. 

People vs. Sanchez, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 305.

KIDNAPING 

It is not necessary to prove the intent or purpose with which the kidnaping was committed except in those cases in which such intent is an element of the corpus delicti. 

People vs. Hunter, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 243. Defendant, posing as a police officer, compelled prosecutrix by exhibiting a gun to enter his car and drove a short distance and stopped and made improper advances to her. Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Brazil, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 754.
