59 

LEADING QUESTIONS 

“On direct examination leading questions may be asked in the discretion of the court when it appears that the interests of justice so require." 

People vs. Sparks, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 748. 

People vs. Wilson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 218.

LESSER OFFENSE-GENERAL 

Defendant, a female, was charged with aiding and abetting her codefendant in the commission of the crime of statutory rape. The court sitting without a jury, found defendant guilty of contributing to the delinquency of a minor. "There is some slight conflict in the decisions as to the law on this subject but it is the better rule to hold that, where the acts committed tended to show the commission of the acts charged and where such acts in themselves actually constituted the lesser offense . . . . a conviction of the lesser offense should be sustained." (Note: Court does not pass on the point that a conviction can only be had where the elements of the lesser offense are covered by the pleading. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Lopez, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 857.

A prosecution of defendant by the prosecutrix on a charge of battery which resulted in his conviction is a bar to his trial upon the charge of attempt to commit rape where the indictment specifically alleges an assault and shows that the assault formed the basis of the battery charge and formed the gravamen of the attempted rape and was the act constituting such attempt. (Lengthy discussion) 

People vs. Mendoza, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 707.

LESSER OFFENSE-INSTRUCTIONS 

Where from all the evidence, the murder was of the first degree, it was proper for the court, by instruction, to limit the jury to a verdict finding defendant guilty or not guilty of first degree murder. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 270. 

People vs. Mandell, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 806.

Defendant contended that since under the evidence he was either guilty of assault with a deadly weapon or not guilty that the court erred in giving an instruction that the jury might convict of simple assault, such instruction having been given at the request of the prosecution. 

Held that while it is proper to refuse an instruction as to a lesser offense where the evidence warrants a conviction, if at all, of the offense charged it is also the law that the error in giving such an instruction is not ground for a reversal unless defendant has been prejudicially affected thereby and that, the defense here being merely that of an alibi, defendant was benefitted rather than prejudiced by the conviction of the lessor offense. 

People vs. Cota, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 555.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Complainant testified that defendant struck him with a gun and knocked him down and demanded his money which he did because there was nothing else he could do. Defendant denied this, but said that he had struck complainant with a club in self defense. Appellant contended that in addition to the robbery charge the court should have allowed the jury to consider also the crimes of assault and assault with a deadly weapon. Held there was no error. (Incidentally the refused instruction was misleading. Also the only assault of which defendant could be convicted was that involved in complainant's testimony and if no property was taken defendant was entitled to an acquittal under the instructions given). 

People vs. Driscoll, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 748.

LEWD ACTS-GENERAL 

Constructing Section 288a the court says: "A mere contact of the mouth with the sexual organ of another, either by a 'kissing' or a 'licking' cannot be construed to mean a copulation. The word copulation has never had the meaning of mere contact. It has always had the significance of the verb 'to couple' which is an English derivative." (Here the court discusses the origin and meaning of the word.) "Therefore the legislature, in framing 

Section 288a of the Penal Code, must have intended to punish only those who participated in an act whereby they are united or joined by the perverted act of one's holding in his mouth the sexual organ of another for the purpose of gratifying their sexual desires." 

People vs. Angier, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 417. (Distinguished in People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.)

Upon the girl pointing to the place where she claimed appellant had touched her the prosecutor stated, "She is indicating the crotch." The court says that this does not sufficiently establish the touching of the sexual organ of the child. 

People vs. Angier, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 417.

Whether complainant reported the acts and conduct of defendant is a matter merely going to his credibility. 

People vs. Slaughter, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 724.

One of the elements of the crime defined in Section 288 is the specific intent. 

People vs. Kearney, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 672. 

Defendant contacted prosecutrix and another girl who were seated at a playground on some board seats and commenced a conversation with them and put his hand on the leg of the prosecutrix below the knee and asked her if she cared, to which she made no reply, and defendant then put his hand further up under her dress and upon her bare skin, at the same time admonishing the girls not to tell anyone. A woman passed by with a dog and defendant got up and left. Case tried by the court without a jury. 

Evidence held sufficient to show the necessary specific intent required by 

288 Penal Code. 

People vs. Hyche, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 661.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

61 

Where defendant, though stating he was merely going to kiss the girl's privates, kept his mouth against her sexual organ for five or ten minutes the jury was justified in convicting him of a violation of Section 288a of the 

Penal Code. (People vs. Angier, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 417 distinguished; actually overruled.) 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

The offense described in Section 288a of the Penal Code may be committed by a male upon the sexual organ of a female. 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

LEWD ACTS-GENERAL 

In a prosecution under Section 288a of the Penal Code it is not necessary that the person upon whom the offense is committed be corroborated unless he is an accomplice. 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

November 4, 1941, was the fourteenth anniversary of the birth of the prosecutrix. The offense occurred on November 3, 1941. Held that since the prosecutrix was fourteen years old on the latter date and hence not under the age of fourteen years at the time there could be no violation of 

Section 288 of the Penal Code. Order of trial court granting a new trial on this ground affirmed. (Note: Both trial and appellate court overlooked the fact that, if defendant had committed the act, instead of granting a new trial, the court should have denied the motion and reduced the conviction to one of contributing to the delinquency of a minor.) 

People vs. Dudley, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 187.

LEWD ACTS-COMPLAINT OF PROSECUTING WITNESS 

Defendant was convicted of violating Section 288 and 288a of the Penal Code. Where the mother of prosecutrix testified that the latter had made complaint to her it was error to permit the mother to testify on direct examination that she had made a complaint to the police a week or so later as such testimony would carry the implication that the mother believed the defendant was guilty. 

People vs. Hubbell, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

While the fact of complaint may be proved in a rape case to corroborate the prosecutrix and tend to prove lack of consent the reason for the rule is absent where the illegal act is performed with the legal consent of the prosecuting witness or where the complaint is made a considerable time after the alleged offense. "This type of evidence escapes exclusion under the hearsay rule only because it portrays a verbal act manifested while the victim is influenced by the shock of mistreatment and because the fact of complaint expresses a spontaneous reaction to an unusual and disturbing experience." (Note: If the court means that the declaration must be part of the res gestae of the offense it goes too far and it may be noted this statement in the opinion is not supported by any authority cited. C.W.F.) 

Evidence of complaint, by a 13 year old victim of a violation of section

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

288a, ten months after the alleged offense, the evidence strongly indicating that he was an accomplice, the complaint not being made until the boy had himself been arrested in the commission of an act of personal misconduct in a service station held erroneously admitted. 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

LEWD ACTS-INSTRUCTIONS 

An instruction that to constitute a violation of Section 288 of the Penal Code it is not necessary that the bare skin of the minor be touched and that the conduct prohibited by the section constitutes the offense even though such touching, fondling, rubbing or feeling was through the clothing of the minor is in conformity with the provisions of the section. 

People vs. Schultz, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 38.

LIMITATIONS, STATUTE OF 

The offenses charged were committed in June of 1937, and the informacion was filed November 28, 1940, three years and five months later. Defendant testified that his absence from the state during this period did not exceed ninety days. There was evidence, however, that defendant, in a conversation with the district attorney and arresting officers, had admitted being outside of the state from June of 1938 to August of 1939. "Obviously, this evidence not only constituted impeachment of appellant but also constituted admissions against interest which, if believed by the jury, as they apparently were, constituted direct evidence on the issue. The mere fact that the prosecution should have introduced evidence on the issue in its case in chief, but did not, and did not introduce such evidence except in rebuttal, in no way detracts from the evidence as direct evidence on the issue." 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735.

It is not necessary that the jury return a special verdict on the issue of limitations. A general verdict of guilty "implies a finding on all facts necessary to a conviction of the charge contained in the information." 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735. "There is no provision of the Penal Code requiring or permitting a special plea in reference to the statute of limitations." 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735. Defendant was charged with assault with a deadly weapon and convicted of simple assault. Held that since the information was filed within a year (actually a month) after the assault, there was no bar by reason of the statute of limitations, even though the verdict was returned more than a year after the offense was committed. 

People vs. Brooks, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 610.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

63 

LIMITATIONS, STATUTE OF-INSTRUCTIONS 

In a case in which there was a conflict of evidence which, if resolved in favor of defendant, entitled him to the benefit of the statute of limitations the refusal of a proper instruction as to this defense is not cured but aggravated by a composite instruction on alibi, the rule that it is immaterial when the offense was committed provided it was committed within three years prior to the filing of the information but without stating the rule of the statute as a defense. The court recommends the discontinuance of the use of this "stock" instruction. (Note: The court evidently did not have in mind that the stock instruction on the point that it was immaterial when the offense was committed provided it was within three years is a proper instruction where the statute is not a defense but the precise date is not fixed by the evidence. See cases under DATE OF OFFENSE. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

MANSLAUGHTER-INVOLUNTARY 

Evidence of causing the death of a human being while driving an automobile while under the influence of intoxicating liquor will sustain a conviction of manslaughter. 

People vs. Crow, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666. "For an act to be without due caution and circumspection, as required for involuntary manslaughter . . . . it is not necessary that such act go to the extent of being wanton or reckless. The statements in People vs. Driggs, 111 Cal. App. 42, 47 and in People vs. Hurley, 13 Cal. App. (2d) 208, 214, to the contrary have been overruled by our Supreme Court in People vs. 

Pociask, supra," 14 Cal. (2d) 679. 

People vs. Crow, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666.

MANSLAUGHTER-VOLUNTARY 

Deceased, sixty-one years old, shook his finger at defendant and told him he could not marry the lady who was accompanying him. Appellant struck deceased who backed away threatening to call the police and appellant then pursued the deceased across the street and administered a beating causing the latter's death. Deceased struck no blows and offered no violence. 

Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Lee, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

Conviction of two jailors sustained in a case in which in capturing and subduing a misdemeanor prisoner who appeared to be attempting an escape unnecessary force was used to subdue him and blows were struck on his head which might have caused death after he was knocked to the floor and non-resistant. 

People vs. Peirce, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 731.

MISCONDUCT IN OFFICE 

"The procedure under Section 758 does not require the strict application of the rules which govern civil or criminal actions. It is not required that the misconduct charged against an official should necessarily constitute a crime. It is exempt from technical rules of procedure. Wilful misconduct in office includes any wilful malfeasance, misfeasance or non feasance. Such official conduct does not necessarily imply corruption or criminal intention. 

The officer who, by exercise of the powers of his office, does an act forbidden by a penal statute is guilty of the conduct contemplated by Setcion 758." "The volation of the ordinance (making it unlawful for any person to

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

use any motor vehicle belonging to the City of Los Angeles for any purpose other than official business) or of the (embezzlement) statute is, at least, a misdemeanor which betrays in the heart of the malefactor, a contempt for the law and is therefore sufficient to constitute wilful misconduct in office." 

To drive a city-owned automobile on a 4000 mile pleasure jaunt was so clearly an appropriation of the vehicle to private use that illustration and authority seem supererogatory." 

People vs. Harby, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 759. 

MISCONDUCT IN OFFICE-PLEADING 

Accusation by grand jury held sufficient. Accusation set forth in opinion. 

People vs. Harby, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 966. (Note: The court calls attention to the fact that defendant received not only a copy of the accusation but a copy of the testimony before the grand jury.)

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTONREY— ARGUMENT-GENERAL 

In a prosecution for rape committed upon two young girls the prosecutor made a remark in argument to the effect that the only pleasant thing about the case was the fact that it was possible for the details of the case to come "from the mouths of the victims themselves instead of from an autopsy surgeon." No request was made to the court to instruct the jury. Held that the remark was not such that its effect could not have been removed by instruction and hence no reversible error. 

People vs. Giminiani, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 535.

In a case of statutory rape it is proper for the district attorney to mention and argue the subject of punishment since, in such cases, the jury must recommend the place of imprisonment. 

People vs. Vivian, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 533.

A statement by the district attorney that he could not call defendant's wife as a witness against him held not prejudicial error. 

People vs. Briggs, 20 Cal. (2d) 31.

Misconduct assigned held not such as could have influenced the jury in the light of the clear proof of guilt and defendant's conviction of manslaughter under evidence which would have warranted a verdict of murder. 

People vs. Smith, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 615.

In arguing in a prosecution of a wife for her perjury whereby her husband escaped prosecution for a violation of Section 288a of the Penal Code the district attorney said "this filthy man here (referring to the husband) has been dismissed and goes scot free and this woman ought to be punished for it." The court says, "We do not believe that the district attorney exceeded the bounds of legitimate argument in this case." 

People vs. Yecny, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 574.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

65 

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY— ARGUMENT-FACTS NOT IN EVIDENCE 

In a prosecution for selling narcotics the district attorney, in argument stated that he did not know whether defendant was a user of narcotics or not, that the characteristics of a user were pretty well known and that defendant had many of these characteristics. Misconduct severly criticised and conviction reversed. 

People vs. Duvernay, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 823.

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY—ARGUMENT- COMMENT ON FAILURE OF DEFENDANT TO TESTIFY 

"The law is established in this jurisdiction that it is not error for the district attorney to comment upon the fact that a defendant has failed to take the stand in his own behalf or that the defendant has failed to deny any of the incriminating evidence received against him. (People vs. Perry, 14 

Cal. (2d) 387)." 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 265. 

See also: People vs. Amaya, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 656.

Defense character witnesses having been asked concerning certain alleged unfavorable reports as to defendant's character did not warrant argument by the prosecutor upon defendant's failure to testify as to these matters. Held, however, that in this particular case there was no miscarriage of justice. 

People vs. Haley, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 618.

Comment or refusal of a defendant to testify before the grand jury must be limited to the subject of impeachment of the defendant as a witness and may not be commented upon as pertaining to defendant's guilt or innocence. 

Error here held not to have prejudiced the codefendant of defendant witness in question. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2) 1.

Where the claimed improper argument was in reply to argument of defense counsel it will not be considered unless appellant includes in the transcript what his own counsel said which may have prompted the prosecutor's statement. 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY- ARGUMENT-EPITHETS 

Reference in a murder case to defendant as a "killer" did not constitute prejudicial misconduct. 

People vs. Young, 17 Cal. (2d) 484.

Referring to the defendants, on trial for larceny by trick and device, as "rats" does not constitute reversible error. 

People vs. Alba, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 859.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

In a case involving two charges of kidnapping young girls and one charge of having raped one of the girls, thirteen years of age, the court says that "the comparison of the defendant to a vulture was inappropriate and improper but not prejudicial since defendant made no objection. 

People vs. Hunter, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 348.

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY- DURING THE TRIAL 

Where a defendant was asked on cross-examination whether he had been previously convicted of a felony, the question being asked in good faith, and defendant answered that he had not and no proof of such conviction was offered and, at the prosecutor's request the entire matter was expunged from the record, held there was no prejudicial misconduct. (Note: Why didn't the court say there was no misconduct at all? C. W. F.) 

People vs. Reed, 17 Cal. (2d) 420.

After swearing the complaining witness in count three and defendant's objection to his testifying on the ground that defendant had not had a preliminary examination on such charge the district attorney dismissed this count. 

Held no error. 

People vs. Karpinski, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 545.

In questioning defendant as to his prior conviction of a felony the prosecutor included questions as to whether defendant had not "skipped" his parole, that his application for pardon had not been granted, whether the warden would be glad to have him back. Held reversible misconduct. (No objection was made by defense-reversed on this ground alone. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Wynn, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 723.

Error in asking a question assuming facts is corrected where the question is not answered and a question in proper form is put by the court and answered. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 576.

A question to a prospective juror as to whether, considering the presumption of innocence, the juror believed it the job of the district attorney to prosecute an innocent man while improper does not prejudice defendant where objection to the question was sustained. 

People vs. Buck, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 558.

In questioning a police inspector concerning statements made to him by defendant the prosecutor asked the witness whether defendant had stated what his occupation was and receiving an affirmative answer, asked what defendant said to which the officer replied that defendant had told him that he was unable to work, "he had been a thief all his life since nine years old." The court, on motion, struck out the answer and properly admonished the jury. Held that since there was no showing that the prosecutor must have known what the answer would be and the trial court did not believe such to be the case there was no misconduct and, since defendant on the witness stand admitted a series of larcenies and burglaries in the past, there was no prejudice. 

People vs. Collins, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 281. (Note: Dissenting justice says the prosecutor was chargeable with knowledge of what the answer would be and hence was guilty of misconduct. C. W. F.)

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY- DURING TRIAL 

During the cross examination of defendant defense counsel objected to the prosecutor "holding up a paper in front of the jury" while examining the witness and said, "If he is going to do that let's find out what the paper is." To this the prosecutor replied that counsel knew he could not call defendant's wife as a witness against him and that the paper was a statement of the wife. The court, though one judge dissented, holds the error and misconduct not ground for reversal. 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

On his direct examination a police officer related a conversation had with the defendant concluding with "I think that about covers it." On crossexamination he was asked whether, in relating this conversation at the preliminary examination, he had not given a certain answer relating to the subject matter of the conversation. On redirect examination the district attorney, over objection, read at length from the preliminary transcript "the testimony read relating but briefly to the matter inquired about on cross-examination but extensively to defendant's alleged statements to the officer relating to many other matters and containing statements strongly indicating, if not admitting, guilt." The district attorney contended he could read all of this because it all constituted a single answer to a question. 

The court struck out a portion of the testimony read and stated that the reading had gone far enough but the prosecutor continued reading testimony "having no relation whatever to the cross-examination and containing further damaging admissions of the defendant." The court again struck out testimony read and suggested "you had better abstain from reading all that balance." The prosecutor continued reading. Held error which was not cured by either striking out portions or the court's admonition. 

People vs. Hubbell, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 84.

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY— INSTRUCTIONS, EFFECT OF 

Where the jury is admonished to disregard the alleged misconduct "it must be presumed that the jury heeded the admonition and that no harm resulted from such misconduct." 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 87.

Where the court instructs the jury to disregard the remarks or the remarks are withdrawn by the prosecutor any error in mis-statement of evidence is cured. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1. "While we must concede that the deputy district attorney exerted every effort within his power to commit prejudicial misconduct, by his persistence in asking immaterial and incompetent questions, nevertheless his efforts in that regard were successfully thwarted by the prompt and decisive rulings of the trial judge made upon the timely and appropriate objects interposed by defendant's counsel, all of which served to keep out of the record the improper testimony sought to be elicited ... The court also instructed the jury to draw no inference from any question which was not permitted to be answered and the jury was properly admonished to disregard the misconduct. Held no ground for reversal. 

People vs. Shack, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 16.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY- OBJECTION, NECESSITY FOR 

Where the misconduct is such that any harmful effect could have been cured by an instruction from the court, appellant can not raise the point an appeal if he did not object and request such instruction at the time the alleged misconduct occurred. 

People vs. Marvich, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 858. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620.

MISCONDUCT OF DISTRICT ATTORNEY— OPENING STATEMENT 

In a prosecution for robbery the district attorney in his opening statement said, "The evidence will show, I think, that this defendant boasted to people down there, especially to those he had working for him, that he had killed seventeen people." The statement apparently was made in good faith but when a question concerning this subject was asked, the court sustained defendant's objection and the court admonished the jury to disregard the remark made in the opening statement. Held defendant suffered no prejudice. 

People vs. Knight, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 887.

MISCONDUCT OF JUDGE DURING TRIAL 

Defense counsel, preceding his cross-examination, stated to the witness "I am going to ask you a very frank question and I hope you'll be just as frank." The court said that it wasn't necessary to reprimand the witness. 

Held no misconduct, "the witness was entitled to protection from such insinuations. . . . 

After defendant was found not guilty and before the trial of the insanity defense a juror stated that he felt he was disqualified as he had visited insane wards and could not vote to send defendant to an insane ward. The district attorney offered but defense counsel refused to stipulate to the use of an alternate juror. The court remarked, "Well, if you don't want Mr. Minton, why didn't you stipulate that he be dismissed?" The court admonished the jury to disregard the entire incident. Held no misconduct. 

People vs. Mandell, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 806. In responding to the argument of the prosecutor in which he mentioned that he could not call the wife of the defendant as a prosecution witness with the inference that defendant had not called her defense counsel stated that "calling the wife is a question of privilege; the defendent could exercise the privilege if she were called by the prosecution but I don't think we would have exercised the privilege." At this point the trial judge said, "There is a rule of law which provides that the wife cannot testify against the husband without his consent. But it has also been held misconduct for the district attorney to call the wife to the witness stand and force the defendant into a situation where he has either to accept the testimony of the wife or make objections." 

The court calls attention to the case of People vs. Moore, 111 Cal. App. 

632 where it is said to be unfair to ask defendant, in the presence of the

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

69 jury whether she would object to her husband being called as a witness against her and that under such circumstances the spouse should be called as a witness, subject to the objections of defendant. Held that while "not entirely accurate" the remark of the trial judge did not prejudice appellant's rights. (Note: The suggested procedure in People vs. Moore is likely to prejudice a defendant since his objection would in most instances cause the jury to believe he was afraid to have the wife testify and, in cases in which it would appear that the wife would, if allowed, testify in a manner highly detrimental to defendant, this would be of considerable effect and it would almost be certain that defendant would object. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Briggs, 20 Cal. (2d) 42.

A statement by the court that the use of the word "gentleman" was not appropriate to the defendants was "unquestionably erroneous." (Court then presents other cases in which the same judge also committed misconduct). 

People vs. Williams, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 790.

MISCONDUCT OF JUDGE-EXAMINATION OF WITNESS 

“A reversal may not be had upon a mere showing that the trial judge examined a witness at unusual length and without apparent necessity therefor." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1. "A trial judge certainly is authorized on cross-examination of a defendant to call his attention to apparently conflicting statements which he has made so as to give him an opportunity to explain or reconcile them if possible." 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39. (Quoting with approval from People vs. Butterfield, 40 Cal. App. 22d) 

725, 731).

MISTAKE OF LAW AS DEFENSE 

While ignorance or mistake of law is not a defense, still, where a person in good faith, takes property legally that of another, but which he believes legally to be his own or that he has a right to its possession, he is not guilty of larceny, since that crime has an essential element the specific intent to deprive another of property; that is, there must be the intent to take property belonging to someone other than the taker. (Note: It must be borne in mind, however, that if the elements of the crime of larceny are established, defendant could not defend on the grounds that he had the legal right to permanently deprive another of his property. 

In other words, if the act of the defendant was criminal, it is no defense that he believed it was lawful. C. W. F.). 

People vs. Photo, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 345.

MISTRIAL See also: FORMER JEOPARDY 

While prosecutrix in a case under section 288 of the Penal Code was under cross-examination, she burst into tears, whereupon an aunt of the witness went to the witness stand and put her arms around the girl. The court directed the aunt to go back and sit down. Refusal of court to order a mistrial sustained. "A motion for a mistrial is addressed to the sound discretion of the trial court. It may properly be refused where the court is satisfied that no injustice had resulted or will result from the occurrences of which complaint is made." 

People vs. Grant, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 319.

MOTION TO SET ASIDE INDICTMENT 

Defendant indicted for murder moved to set aside indictment. The court set the indictment aside but, under sections 997 and 998, Penal Code, held defendant to answer for manslaughter. Held that, since the evidence was sufficient to show probable cause for holding defendant, the action of the court was proper. (Opinion fails to state upon what ground the motion to set aside was made; apparently insufficiency of evidence before grand jury. 

The case apparently and probably, though not too clearly holds that in such case the court, instead of releasing defendant, could properly direct the district attorney to file an information charging manslaughter. C. W. F.) 

In re Crowley, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 365.

MOTION TO SET ASIDE INFORMATION 

In the absence of evidence in support of the motion, it is properly denied. 

Two codefendants were held to answer before appellant and their commitment was endorsed upon the original complaint. Without objection, the proceedings against appellant were conducted under a copy of the complaint. Held that the magistrate had not lost jurisdiction in sending up to the Superior Court the original complaint on the first commitment, since the filing of the complaint gave jurisdiction; also, that the commitment is not required to be endorsed upon the original complaint and that, at most, the matter was an irregularity of procedure. 

People vs. Thomas, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 128.

MOTION TO SET ASIDE JUDGMENT 

Where there is a conflict in the evidence as to whether defendant pleaded guilty upon a promise that no charge of prior convictions would be filed against him the trial court did not abuse its discretion in denying the motion to set aside the judgment. 

People vs. Carson, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 554.

The indictment "not being void on its face, any errors or uncertainties could have been reached by appropriate action before judgment and are, therefore, not reviewable on appeal from an order denying a motion to vacate the judgment." 

People vs. Marr, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 41.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

71 Defendant plead guilty in Orange County to the crime of escape. On motion to vacate the judgment he contended that in fact the escape had occurred in Los Angeles County and that, therefore, the Orange County court had no jurisdiction. Held a collateral attack on the judgment, valid on its face, that the plea of guilty included an admission of every element of the defense including the allegation that the escape occurred in Orange County and that the proof offered by appellant was not conclusive. Judgment sustained. 

People vs. Marr, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 41. "It is now well settled in this state that where on account of duress, fraud, or other fact overreaching the free will and judgment of the defendant he is deprived of a trial on the merits, the court in which he was sentenced may after judgment and after the time for appeal has passed, if a properly supported motion is seasonably made, grant him the privilege of withdrawing his plea of guilty and of reassuming the situation occupied by him before a plea of any kind was entered. It should be noted, however, that where statutory remedies are provided this method of relief may not be resorted to, and that this exceptional remedy applies only where no trial has been had on the merits and only upon a strong and convincing showing of the deprivation of legal rights by extrinsic causes." 

Appellant Wallington at the instigation of her employer, Bernard Schwarz, approached jurors trying Schwarz and by statements attempted to influence their verdict. It appeared that in indicting appellant the grand jury and the district attorney did not intend to prosecute her but did so to induce her to testify for the prosecution in return for which she would be granted immunity. Finally a member of the grand jury, with the consent of the district attorney, induced appellant to surrender and make a statement to the district attorney. Appellant pleaded guilty, was denied probation and sentenced to state prison. She had been promised by the grand juror either absolute immunity or at most no greater punishment than her codefendant Schwarz who, upon his plea of guilty, was fined $250. Motion of appellant, after judgment, to withdraw the plea of guilty was denied by the trial judge who was familiar with all of the facts. 

People vs. Schwarz, 201 Cal. 309.

The general rule is that a party who has the right of appeal from a judgment may not, after such right has expired, move to set the judgment aside and then appeal from the order denying such motion, based on a ground which could have been raised on appeal from the judgment. 

People vs. Lototoff, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 360.

A motion to set aside a judgment based upon errors and rulings which might properly be considered upon the appeal from the judgment is properly denied and the order denying such motion is not appealable where the motion merely asks the court to repeat or overrule the former ruling on the same facts. 

People vs. Palmer, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 567, 579.

Although designated as a motion to set aside the judgment the court will consider it as a petition for coram nobis if it has that legal effect. 

People vs. Harincar, 49 Cal.. App. (2d) 594.

122 

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Where defendant had, upon arraignment, admitted three prior felony convictions and having served terms of imprisonment therefor in a state prison and thereafter application is made in the nature of a petition for a writ of coram nobis to set aside the judgment and his adjudication as an habitual criminal upon the ground that such prior convictions were not such as came within the scope of the habitual criminal law and that, at the time, defendant was not aware of the legal effect of these prior convictions nevertheless the writ of error coram nobis does not furnish defendant a remedy and since defendant had omitted to avail himself of the remedies provided by statute and the judgment had become final and in such case there is no further legal recourse by any form of judicial review. 

People vs. Moore, 9 Cal. App. (2d) 251. 

People vs. Lumbley, 8 Cal. (2d) 752, 760. 

People vs. Harincar, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 594. "Ordinarily a party who has a right to appeal from a judgment or order is not allowed to move to set it aside and then appeal from an order denying his motion." (In other words if he claimed error in judgment he should have appealed therefrom and, not having done so, can not revive the right of appeal, lost by failure to appeal at the proper time, by a motion to set the judgment aside made thereafter. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Scranton, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 492.

While defendant's appeal from the judgment was pending defendant made a motion to vacate the judgment because of illegality in the drawing of the grand jury which returned the indictment. Held that the remedy was on appeal from the judgment and that this was the "only available relief from a final judgment of conviction." "A defendant cannot try out his contentions piecemeal by attacking the validity of the judgment against him in successive proceedings." 

People vs. Montgomery, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 444, 777.

Defendant moved to set aside the judgment on the grounds that he had a meritorious defense and that his plea of guilty was induced by duress, fraud and coercion. The affidavits of defendant and two women friends was directly contraverted by affidavits of the officers and defendant's counsel. 

The evidence preponderating strongly against the motion its denial "must be affirmed." 

People vs. Norton, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 712.

Defendant made affidavit, contradicted by answering affidavits, that she has pleaded guilty because she had been informed that her attorney had influence with the court and district attorney and that her attorney had told her that he had spoken to the district attorney and that in his opinion she would not receive more than a thirty day sentence. She was sentenced to prison. "The exceptional remedy of a motion to vacate a judgment of conviction 'was not designed to enable the guilty to escape punishment but to purify and keep pure the administration of justice, and the same may be utilized only upon a strong and convincing showing of the deprivations of legal rights by extrinsic causes.' (People vs. Gottlieb, 25 Cal. App. (2d) 

411, 415)"

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

73 1233 The fact that defendant has received a more severe penalty than he expected or than his attorney told him he would receive or that he plead guilty on advice of counsel presents no occasion for vacating the judgment and permitting the entry of a plea of not guilty in lieu of the plea of guilty previously entered.

People vs, Donegan, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 221. 

MOTOR VEHICLES-GENERAL 

"Mere speed may be so excessive as to afford proof of the elements necessary to make out the more serious crime of reckless driving. "Of course it is plain that a person who so drives that he knowingly runs the risk of a probable accident (we are not attempting a full definition) is guilty of wilful misconduct. But it does not follow that if he does not know that an accident is probable, that he is for that reason to be freed from the charge of reckless driving. A course of conduct constituting wilful misconduct, alternative to that of knowing the probable result to be a disastrous one, is recognized in a host of cases, and, in our opinion, must be taken into account adequately to define reckless driving." "We conclude, further, that one may be found guilty of reckless driving if he drives so as to show a wanton and reckless disregard of possible consequences to persons or property of his manner of driving, even though it may not be a fact, and so he may not know, that the possible result of his driving will be to injure a person or property." "We conclude, further, that the rate of speed at which a car is driven may of itself be so great that injury to persons and property is a distinct possibility and that in this case the trial court was warranted in determining that a speed varying between seventy-five and eighty-five miles per hour did endanger persons and property that it was intentional, deliberate and revealed that the driver pursued his course in a wanton and reckless disregard of the possible consequences." 

People vs. Norvell, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 811.

Conviction sustained for failure of defendant to drive his car as close to the right hand edge of the highway as possible. 

People vs. Hugon, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 817.

Defendant drove a motor bus, past about one hundred vehicles going in the same direction and forty going in an opposite direction, and went 2½ miles at the rate of 73 miles per hour. Held that "mere speed constitutes a violation of Section 510, and the absence of an accident, actual or near, neither in law nor logic tends to prove that an excessive rate of speed was reasonable or prudent or that it did not of itself endanger the safety of persons and property." Held, also, that the fact that defendant was carrying 

12 sacks of U. S. mail and was late afforded no defense. 

People vs. Carlos, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 863.

One who has, as a pedestrian, advanced into the roadway, even though but a few steps, before the appearance of the "Stop" signal cannot be said to cross against the signal whether he returns to the curb or continues to the opposite curb.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"We conclude that a pedestrian may not, where traffic control signals are in operation, enter upon the roadway until the green or 'Go' signal is shown, but that when it is shown, he may not only enter upon but proceed across to the opposite side of the roadway, even though the signal changes to red or 'Stop' before he completes his passage.”

People vs. Hawkins, 51 Cal. App. Supp. (2d) 779. 

After conviction of violating sections 481 and 502 of the Vehicle Code, the clerk of the Municipal Court certified an abstract of the conviction to the Motor Vehicle Department which notified petitioner that his driver's license was suspended until he filed proof of ability to respond in damages. 

Petitioner never filed such proof. At the time of suspension the conviction under Section 481 had not been received by the Department, hence the suspension was under the Section 502 conviction. Under section 304 of the 

Vehicle Code a probationary license was issued without proof of ability to respond in damages. When the record of the 481 Vehicle Code conviction was received by the department petitioner was again notified that his license was suspended until proof of ability to respond in damages. 

Held that the Department was justified in its last action. 

Ellis vs. Department of Motor Vehicles, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 753. 

MOTOR VEHICLES-DRIVING WHILE INTOXICATED 

Section 501 of the Vehicle Code is not unconstitutional because it makes it a felony to do an act forbidden by law while under the influence of liquor which act, if done by a sober person, would only be a misdemeanor. "An automobile is a dangerous instrumentality, and in the hands of a drunken man is likely to become a deadly weapon." 

People vs. Chatham, 43 Cal. App. 298.

MOTOR VEHICLES-DRIVING WHILE INTOXICATED-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court refused an instruction that a person, without negligence on his part, suddenly confronted with unexpected and imminent danger is not required or expected to use the same degree of care that would be required in calmer moments and that his conduct and duty must be measured by the circumstances and that, if he does what is apparently the best thing to do under the circumstances, viewed from the standpoint of the ordinarily prudent individual, he does all that the law requires even though, in the light of after events, it appears that a different course would have been better and safer. Held reversible error to fail to give such an instruction.

People vs. Boulware, 41 Cal. App. 268. 

MOTOR VEHICLES-FAILURE TO STOP, ETC. 

"Knowledge that a rider on a bicycle which has been struck by an automobile has been thrown to the ground is sufficient evidence of injury to the rider to call into operation the provisions of the statute." 

People vs. Roche, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 459.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

75 Defendant in full daylight was driving her automobile which overtook and struck the rear end of a bicycle and passed to its left. The bicycle and rider were thrown to the ground, appellant speeded up her car. The noise of the collision was heard by a witness 50 feet away and a witness in another car. Proof of corpus delicti held sufficient to permit reception of defendant's extrajudicial admissions. 

People vs. Roche, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 459.

Held also that the evidence, apart from the admissions, was sufficient to show defendant had knowledge that her car had been involved in a collision causing personal injuries. 

People vs. Roche, supra.

MOTOR VEHICLES-FAILURE TO STOP-PLEADING 

A charge in substantially the language of the statute is sufficient. An allegation that defendant "wilfully" failed to stop his car "is equivalent to a statement that he knew his car had injured or killed a man and that he deliberately refrained from stopping to render aid." 

People vs. Dallas, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 596.

MOTOR VEHICLES-NEGLIGENT HOMICIDE-GENERAL 

"By the enactment of this section (500 Vehicle Code) the legislature defined a crime different from any of the crimes which had been defined in any of the various provisions of the Penal Code." 

People vs. Beckhard, 14 Cal. (2d) 690, 692. 

People vs. Amick, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 662.

In sustaining conviction the court explains how decedent, riding as a passenger with defendant, could have been the person whose body crushed the steering wheel and the windshield in front of it and that this would not be inconsistent with defendant having been the driver. 

People vs. Van Osdoll, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 456. 

Section 500 of the Vehicle Code does not set up the same standard of wrongful driving as does section 403 of the Vehicle Code, which requires the driving, to entitle a guest to recover damages, to amount to "wilful misconduct."

A street car came to a gradual stop at an intersection and when it had come to a complete stop a 13 year old boy got out and as he took his second step on the pavement was struck by an automobile driven by defendant. The accident occurred in the afternoon of a clear day. Defendant's automobile was going about 25 miles per hour at the time of the impact and she made no effort to stop until after the impact. The trial judge, a jury trial having been waived, found that the facts showed reckless disregard of or wilful indifference to the safety of others and found defendant guilty.

"If appellant was not observing the street car with sufficient attention to see that it was coming to a stop; if she took no notice of the amber stop lights on the rear of the street car as she approached to pass it, if, when she was about to pass it, she failed to observe that the door was opening or that a passenger was alighting it may be fairly said that all of

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

these acts combined on the part of the appellant constituted such 'reckless disregard of, or wilful indifference to the safety of others' as to imply a high degree of probability that such harm would result as did, in fact, result in the instant case." (NOTE: The court does not define the language of section 500 V. C.-C. W. F.). 66 

People vs. Young, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 394.

But the supreme court reversed the conviction and analyzed the sections. "There can be no doubt that the Legislature intended that something more than ordinary negligence was to be required by the 1941 amendment." . . . .We believe that the Legislature intended that the terms used in Section 500 should be interpreted in accordance with the definitions given by the courts to other similar terms if those definitions may reasonably be said to fit the particular words used. . . If the definition of wilful misconduct 403 Vehicle Code) is consonant with the words used in Section 500 their meaning may be said to be similar." The court here quotes the definition of "wilful misconduct" from Howard vs. Howard, 132 Cal. App. 124, 129. "But wilful misconduct as used in this statute means neither the sort of misconduct involved in any negligence nor the mere intent to do the act which constitutes negligence. Wilful misconduct implies at least the intentional doing of something, either with a knowledge that serious injury is a probable (as distinguished from a possible) result or the intentional doing of an act with a wanton and reckless disregard of its possible result." The court also says that "While it is true that with reference to the disregard of others appearing in section 500 is doing an act while recklessly ignoring the safety of others. It is thus equivalent to, quoting from the definition, the 'intentional doing of an act with wanton and reckless disregard of its possible result.' Wilful indifference to the safety of others, as an intentional lack of regard concerning the safety of others or 'an intentional doing of something with knowledge that a serious injury as a probable result.' "The court also says that "While it is true that with reference to the disregard for the safety of others in defining reckless driving (505 V. C.) the character of the disregard is described as 'wilful or wanton' rather than 'reckless' as appears in section 500, there is no substantial difference." Court holds facts insufficient to measure up to this standard. (NOTE, however, that the court seems inclined to view the case in the light of defendant's testimony that she didn't know that the car was going to stop and didn't see decedent until he stepped into the fender of her car, ignoring that the jury was not obliged to believe her testimony and would have been justified in drawing the opposite conclusion; neither do the facts warrant the court's statement that defendant did not have an opportunity to see the deceased in time to avoid the collision a conclusion not a necessary inference since the facts would warrant a different conclusion if one considers the relative speeds of the street car and defendant's automobile and the time during which deceased must have been visible to an approaching driver. While agreeing as to the law I do not agree that the evidence did not sustain the conviction. C. W. F.). 

People vs. Young, 20 Cal. (2d) 832.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

77 

MOTOR VEHICLES-NEGLIGENT HOMICIDE-PLEADING 

"The charge of wilfully and unlawfully violating that statute (Sec. 530, Subdv. 2, Vehicle Code) constituted an allegation of negligent operation of the automobile. Since it was also alleged that the negligent manner of driving the machine proximately resulted in the killing of Giovanni Musanti, it constituted a charge of 'negligent homicide' as that crime is defined in section 500 of the Vehicle Code." 

People vs. Dallas, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 596.

MOTOR VEHICLES-503 V. C.-TAKING IN ABSENCE OF OWNER 

Defendant was found by complainant sitting in the latter's car where he had parked it and behind the wheel; defendant was in the act of searching the glove compartment and had evidently pried open the vent in the cowl. In considering the question of defendant's conviction of felonious assault in resisting the complainant's effort to arrest him and the lawfulness of such an arrest the court says: "When a person temporarily deprives the owner of a vehicle of temporary possession even without intent to steal, he is guilty of a felony. (Vehicle Code, Sec. 503)." (NOTE: Since the car was not moved it is difficult to see how the court could have formed the conclusion that defendant had violated section 503 since, to commit that offense, the defendant must drive or take the automobile or vehicle. (C. W. F.). 

People vs. Score, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 495. 

MURDER-GENERAL 

Where accused heard deceased, who was drunk, make insulting remarks and threats of violence toward him and shortly thereafter the two were seen engaged in a scuffle, the deceased mortally stabbed and having ahold of both of defendant's wrists, defendant having a knife in his hand and deceased being unarmed and defendant admitting that he had inflicted the wounds upon the deceased but there being no evidence of the circumstances under which the wounds were inflicted the evidence would support a conviction of murder and a conviction of manslaughter is proper. 

People vs. Campanella, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 697. 

Conviction of first degree murder, in an attempt to perpetrate mayhem, sustained. (Fricke on Criminal Law, quoted and cited). 

People vs. Vaiz, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 817.

MURDER-GENERAL-INSTRUCTIONS 

An instruction defining murder and its degrees in the same language as People vs. Nichol, 34 Cal. 211 (the long time used instruction covering all phases) held proper. 

People vs. Walsh, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 164. 

The court instructed the jury: "Malice aforethought, either express or implied, is manifested by the doing of an unlawful and felonious act intentionally, deliberately and without legal cause or excuse. It does not imply a pre-existing hatred or enmity towards the individual injured." "The given instruction is a correct statement of the law and is invariably given in all homicide cases. (C. W. F.). (People vs. Fallon, 149 Cal. 287; Peopls vs. 

Balkwell, 143 Cal. 259)." 

People vs. Coleman, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 592.

CRIMINAL DIGEST MURDER-FIRST DEGREE 

Evidence that defendant planned to shoot his victims and, armed with a rifle, went to a place where he expected his victims to come and, upon their approach shot them and expresses no regret after the shooting, held sufficient to sustain a conviction of first degree murder carrying the death penalty. 

People vs. Wong Don Kay, 17 Cal. (2d) 678.

To constitute murder in the first degree an intent to kill is not necessary when the killing occurs in the commission of a robbery. 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802.

In a case where a man and a woman were attacked, gagged and fatally stabbed, death penalty verdict sustained and proof that defendants were the guilty parties held sufficient. 

People vs. Clark, 18 Cal. (2d) 449.

Evidence that the deceased, a sixteen year old girl, had been attacked and raped, that the victim had been choked, that death was caused by a blow on the head with some hard object causing a gaping wound in the right temple and brain injury sustained a conviction of first degree murder. (NOTE: Appellant's only point apparently was as to the sufficiency of the evidence to connect defendant as to the perpetrator. C. W. F.). 

People vs. Mandell, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 806. 

Conviction sustained. Interesting case in which a nice chain of circumstantial evidence established that it was defendant who committed the murder. 

People vs. Walsh, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 164.

Where defendant was fleeing from the scene of the robbery, carrying what he thought was the bag and money of the victim when, being pursued by a police officer, defendant fired upon the officer and killed him, such killing was committed in the perpetration of the robbery. 

People vs. Dowell, 204 Cal. 109, 118.

Though no brief was filed conviction of defendant of first degree murder carrying the death penalty affirmed. 

People vs. Wells, 20 Cal. (2d) 191. "It is true that in one version of the crime he (defendant) related facts tending to show a killing which was either unintentional or accidental, but as it was committed in the perpetration of a robbery, the offense is murder of the first degree. 

People vs. Williams, 20 Cal. (2d) 273.

MURDER-FIRST DEGREE-PENALTY 

Where defendant, with others, planned to rob an old man and, in carrying out the plan fatally beat the victim with a hammer handle, the fixing of the death penalty upon plea of guilty sustained. 

People vs. Hawk, 17 Cal. (2d) 853.

Where the verdict of the jury fixes the penalty of death "the trial court has no alternative but to impose the death penalty." 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

79 

MURDER FIRST DEGREE-INSTRUCTIONS 

An instruction that "by all the evidence in this case upon the point" the death of the deceased occurred as a result of the commission of murder of the first degree and that if the jury found defendant guilty it would be their duty to find him guilty of murder in the first degree held proper where such was the legal effect of the entire evidence. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 270. 

MURDER-SECOND DEGREE 

About 9 P. M. one Corillo was observed standing in front of a billiard parlor, appellant entered the place, took down a cue and chalked it. When next observed appellant was standing two feet from Carillo who was falling to the ground, appellant was holding a part of the broken cue and the balance of the cue was close to the body of Carillo. Carillo died as the result of the blow. Defendant admitted to the officers that he had struck Carillo with the cue. Evidence held sufficient to sustain a conviction of second degree murder. 

People vs. Amaya, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 656.

Defendant was employed as a cook in the restaurant of deceased and as the result of an argument was discharged. A fist fight between them followed shortly thereafter in which defendant knocked the deceased down, started across the street but came back to where deceased lay and kicked him. Deceased died of a fractured skull and also had a fractured jaw. 

Appellant argued that if death was occasioned by the head of deceased striking the sidewalk the crime could not be second degree murder of which offense appellant was convicted. "The consequences which would follow a fall upon a concrete walk must have been known to appellant. Conceding that the cause of death was the contact with the walk after the blow had been struck, the jury could reasonably found the verdict in question. There is the further fact that malice is indicated by the pursuit of deceased by appellant. (Deceased had left the restaurant and appellant followed and attacked him)." 

People vs. Efstathiou, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 441.

NARCOTICS 

Section 11036 of the Health and Safety Code provides that "no person shall have in his possession any narcotics except as provided in this division." "Manifestly, then, the possession is unlawful at the outset and in any case, unless it can be shown to be otherwise under and by virtue of some exception contained in this 'division.' In other words, lawful possession is the exception." 

People vs. Barton, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 565.

The provisions of the Health and Safety Code relative to the subject of Narcotics are not new statutes (generally speaking) but merely the codification of former enactments. 

People vs. Housman, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 619.

CRIMINAL DIGEST NARCOTICS-PLEADING In charging violations of section 11160 of the Health and Safety Code in furnishing opium to a third person and unlawfully transporting opium it is not necessary to plead the exceptions. 

It is proper to charge the contraband as a "preparation" of opium. 

People vs. Smith, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 744.

NEGATIVE-PROOF OF 

In a prosecution under the Deadly Weapons Act for possession of a pistol by an alien the People may rely on section 1983 Code of Civil Procedure placing the burden of proof as to citizenship upon defendant. (Morrison vs. California, 291 U. S. 82, distinguished as there Morrison was a citizen but his codefendant was an alien). 

People vs. Cordero, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 146.

NEW TRIAL-GENERAL 

The court says that the point that defendant's counsel at the trial "showed gross ignorance or incompetence at the trial" is "a novel one" and decides that such contention is contrary to the record. 

People vs. Ives, 17 Cal. (2d) 493, 510. "It is the general rule that it is a matter resting in the sound discretion of the trial court whether to permit oral argument on a motion for a new trial, and its refusal to do so will not in the absence of a showing of abuse of discretion be grounds for a reversal. . . . In fact it is a practice to be commended for trial judges, when satisfied that they are fully advised as to the law and the facts, to conserve the time of the court and counsel by declining to listen to further oral argument." 

People vs. Norton, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 789. "Unless contrary to the record, a reviewing court must rely upon the conclusions reached by the trial judge on the hearing of a motion for a new trial." 

People vs. Zuccaro, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 417. 

NEW TRIAL-SEC. 42, ARTICLE VI 

"This section is not intended to serve as authority for reversal of a judgment, but its saving grace is directed toward affirmance of a judgment notwithstanding errors in law or procedure unless after examination of the entire cause, including the evidence, the appellate tribunal shall be of the opinion that the errors complained of have resulted in a miscarriage of justice." 

People vs. Pianezzi, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 576.

A conviction will not be set aside upon a hypertechnical attack on some instructions of the trial court where defendant suffered no prejudice. 

People vs. Norton, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 789.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

81 

NEW TRIAL DENIED BUT DEGREE OF CRIME REDUCED 

Section 1181 of the Penal Code providing that the court may deny the motion for a new trial but modify the judgment by reducing the degree of the crime when the evidence warrants such action is not violative of the federal constitution. 

People vs. Cowan, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 155.

NEW TRIAL-FAILURE TO SENTENCE IN TIME 

Defendant plead guilty February 25, 1929, and date of sentence was set for February 29, 1929. Defendant failed to appear on that date and was not apprehended and returned to court until March 12, 1941, at which time he applied for probation and the passing upon the application and the pronouncing of judgment was continued from time to time without objection until May 6, 1941, at which time motions for a new trial and for leave to withdraw his plea of guilty were denied and defendant was sentenced fo State Prison. Held there was no error due to the lapse of time between plea and sentence. Rule reiterated with citation of authorities that failure to sentence within the time specified in "section 1191 of the Penal Code does not automatically entitle defendant to a new trial pursuant to section 1202 of the Code, nor is jurisdiction voided by such delay in every case but only in cases where a miscarriage of justice results." (NOTE: The court might also have said that where no trial has been had there can be no new trial. C. W. F.).

People vs. Palmer, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 569. 

NEW TRIAL-INSUFFICIENCY OF EVIDENCE 

Before a verdict can be set aside upon the ground of insufficiency of the evidence "it must be made clearly to appear that upon no hypothesis whatever is there sufficient substantial evidence to support the conclusion reached in the court below." 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802.

It is axiomatic that an appellate tribunal cannot disturb the findings of the triers of fact unless it be made clearly to appear that upon no hypothesis whatever is there sufficient substantial evidence, circumstantial or otherwise, to support the conclusion reached in the lower court." 

People vs. Hannon, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 484. 

See Also People vs. Johnson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 63.

Where a witness has absolutely discredited his own testimony "by swearing to opposite statements so that one or the other must be false under our laws his testimony is not of necessity to be rejected" and the jury, while they are bound to look upon it with suspicion, may accept as true one or other of the contradictory asservations. 

People vs. Durrant, 116 Cal. 200.

People vs. Burke, 18 Cal. App. 72. 

"If the circumstances reasonably justify the verdict of the jury the opinion of the appellate court that those circumstances might also reasonably be reconciled with the innocence of the defendant will not justify the appellate court in interfering with the determination of the jury.” 

People vs. Flint, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 203.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"Where, however, the evidence relied upon by the prosecution is so improbable as to be incredible and amounts to no evidence, a question of law is presented which authorizes an appellate court to set aside a conviction. Under such circumstances an appellate court will assume that the verdict was the result of passion and prejudice. To be improbable on its face the evidence must assert that something has occurred that it does not seem possible could have occurred under the circumstances disclosed. The improbability must be apparent; evidence which is unusual or inconsistent is not necessarily improbable."

Evidence in case of kidnapping, robbery and rape held inherently improbable. 

People vs. Headlee, 18 Cal. (2d) 248. 

An appellate tribunal cannot disturb the findings of the triers of fact unless it be made to clearly appear that upon no hypothesis is there sufficient substantial evidence, circumstantial or otherwise, to support the conclusion reached in the lower court. 

People vs. Stangler, 18 Cal. (2d) 760.

SEE ALSO: People vs. Kabakoff, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 170; 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39;

People vs. King, 53 App. (2d) 143. 

"It has been repeatedly held that a reviewing court should not reverse a judgment on the ground that the story of the prosecuting witness is inherently improbable unless it is so apparently false and unbelievable that reasonable minds may not differ in that regard." 

People vs. Mitchell, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 422.

The trial court, (i. e. the jury or judge where jury trial is waived) is authorized to reject the defendant's version of the affair and accept as true the testimony of the complaining witness and other evidence against the defendant and therefrom draw the inference of guilt. 

People vs. Robinson, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 576. "To justify a reversal because of the incredibility of the testimony, its improbability must be so obvious as to leave the court no recourse without self-stultification, except to reverse the judgment. . . . Although the testimony of the prosecutrix contains contradictions and although her testimony be contradicted by other witnesses, it is still the duty of the jury to determine whether they will believe or disbelieve the prosecutrix. But even though the testimony of the prosecutrix contains glaring inconsistencies and contradictions, the appellate court must credit to the decision of the trial judge a familiarity with the witnesses and their testimony and that in denying a new trial he acted with understanding and with a regard for the function of the jury." 

People vs. Holquin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 551.

It is within the province of the jury to base their verdict upon that particular portion of the prosecutrix' testimony bearing on the commission of the offense "although they might have regarded the statements of the child on other points as of doubtful verity." 

People vs. Schultz, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 38. "It is only where there is no substantial evidence to support the conclusion reached by the court below (case was tried without a jury, C.W.F.) that we can set aside its determination." 

People vs. Shapiro, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 60.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

83 "We must keep in mind and be guided by the rule that after the finding of the jury has been approved by the trial court on a motion for a new trial, it is conclusive upon appeal and the appellate court may disturb it only when it can be said as a matter of law that there was no sufficient and substantial evidence to support it, and we must assume in favor of the decision of the jury the existence of every fact which the jury could reasonably have deduced from the evidence and then determine whether guilt is deductible therefrom." 

People vs. Walsh, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 164. "Before testimony can be construed as inherently improbable or unbelievable per se, it must appear therefrom that something has been done that it would not seem possible could be done under the circumstances described." 

People vs. Roberts, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 558.

It is only where the testimony challenged is so "inherently improbable as to be impossible of belief" and that therefore it "must be considered in effect no evidence,” that an appellate court would be justified in disturbing the conclusion of the trial judge in passing upon the sufficiency of the evidence to support the verdict when considering the motion for a new trial. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620. "It has been held that such evidence must be so apparently false that reasonable minds may not differ regarding that conclusion before a judgment may be interfered with on appeal because of inherent improbability." 

People vs. Battalina, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 685. 

"Since the decision in People vs. Newland, 15 Cal. (2d) 678, the rule applicable in cases of circumstantial evidence has been definitely established. 

If the circumstances shown in evidence reasonably justify the verdict of the jury, the opinion that those circumstances might also be reconciled with the innocence of defendant will not warrant interference by a reviewing court." 

(NOTE: In People vs. Newland, supra, the point is discussed at some length. The court points out that in People vs. Staples, 149 Cal. 405, so frequently cited the court held that the corpus delicti of murder had not been established and hence the evidence was not sufficient and that as to People vs. Lamson, 1 Cal. (2d) 648, “nothing was decided in that case except that the judgment be reversed," and that the portion of the first opinion which quoted and approved language in the Staples case "cannot be considered an adjudication or an effective approval of the declaration in the prior case" since the majority of the court did not concur therein. "The holding in the 

Staples case is to be strictly confined to situations where the reviewing court as a matter of law must conclude that there is no evidence, circumstantial or otherwise, that a crime has been committed. . . . " Other cases discussed). (2d) 11.

People vs. Arnold, 53 Cal. App. 

"Inconsistencies and discrepancies in the testimony of the prosecuting witness are matters for the sole consideration of the trial judge (jury trial had been waived, C.W.F.) and where, as in the instant case, there is substantial evidence to support the trial judge's findings, this court will not disturb them. 

People vs. Huston, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 537.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

The mere fact that the witnesses testified that defendant committed the violation in the front seat of an automobile where it was seen by the child's mother who was sitting in the back seat does not make their testimony inherently improbable. 

People vs. Beatty, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 268.

NUISANCES 

The "basis for an action such as this (to restrain the operation of a gambling house) must be found in our statutes rather than by reference to the common law definitions of public nuisance". (P. 881). 

People vs. Lim, 18 Cal. (2d) 872. 

OBJECTIONS 

Objections to the qualifications of alienists who testified at the trial cannot be raised for the first time on appeal. 

People vs. Messerly, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 718.

The fact that inadmissible evidence was presented without objection while defendant was under cross-examination will not excuse the error of receiving such evidence over objection at an earlier stage of the trial. "In view of the earlier rebuff, it was not thereafter incumbent upon appellant to object repeatedly to such evidence when later sought to be elicited by the prosecution." 

People vs. Zemavasky, 20 Cal. (2d) 56. 

"If a general objection to the introduction of evidence is overruled, such objection cannot avail the objector on appeal, unless it appears that such evidence was inadmissible for any purpose whatsoever, and a specific objection overruled will be effective to the extent of the grounds specified and no further." 

People vs. Conterno, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 167. 

Objection to the use of a photograph of a co-conspirator for purposes of identification, the photographs including a prison number, is waived by failing to call the court's attention to it. 

People vs. Deccant, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 343. "When a defendant in a criminal case makes no objection at the trial of the charge against him to any testimony offered against him, it is presumed that if such testimony is legally improper and objectionable, he waives any objection he might have interposed thereto. He certainly cannot make his objection for the first time on appeal." 

People vs. Thourwald, 46 Cal. App. 261, 270. 

Quoted with approval: 

People vs. Costello, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 517.

Upon his second trial defendant waived a trial by jury and stipulated to submit the case upon the transcript of the evidence at the first trial excepting therefrom the evidence which had been held, on appeal from the conviction at the first trial, inadmissible. "By this stipulation defendant waived any objection to any other evidence which was contained in the transcript." (In other words evidence not objected to at the second trial could not be considered ground for reversal. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Huston, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 537.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

85 Where the subject matter of a question is objectionable and there is no objection until after the question has been answered the right to complain of the admission of the testimony upon appeal is waived. 

People vs. Brazil, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 754. "Where as in the instant case, hearsay evidence is admitted and there is neither objection nor a motion to strike the same, such objectionable feature of the testimony is waived and may not be raised for the first time on appeal.” 

People vs. Brazil, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 754.

Where a case is tried and submitted to the court on the preliminary transcript objection made and overruled at the preliminary examination must be renewed at the trial or it cannot be relied on an appeal. 

People vs. Allen, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 74.

OFFENSIVE SUBSTANCES-PLACING, ETC. 

Section 375 of the Penal Code substantially provides in subdivisions 1 and 4: • • "1. It shall be unlawful to throw, drop, pour, deposit, release or expose in, upon or about any theatre, restaurant, place of business, place of amusement or any place of public assemblage any liquid, gaseous or solid substance or matter of any kind which is injurious to person or property, or is nauseous, sickening, irritating or offensive to any of the senses. 

"4. Any person who, in violating any of the provisions of subdivision 1 of this section, wilfully employs or uses any liquid, gaseous or solid substance which may produce illness or permanent injury through being vaporized or otherwise disbursed in the air, or who, in violating any of the provisions of subdivision 1 of this section, wilfully employs or uses tear gas. fully employs or uses acid or explosives shall be guilty of a felony... or wil- 29 Appellants claimed the section was so indefinite and uncertain as to prohibit the use in business of acid and that the offense was not dependent upon the malicious use of acid, etc., with criminal intent and therefore was unconstitutional. 

of an acid which "The crime is the wilful throwing, dropping, etc., is injurious to person or property or is nauseous, sickening, irritating or offensive to any of the senses. The element of wilfulness set forth in subdivision (4) is expressly made applicable to subdivision (1). . . . The objection that the word 'acid' is too indefinite since in its ordinary meaning it includes many non-harmful liquids and substances is fully met by the construction which we have placed upon such section. The acid must be such as is injurious to person or property or is nauseous, sickening, irritating or offensive to any of the senses. By employing the term wilfully . . . . the legislature has expressly manifested an intent to exclude from the operation of the statute the innocent use of acid in the normal course of business." 

Held also that appellants could not raise the constitutionality of subdivision (1) since only a violation of subdivision (4) was charged. 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 87. (Affirmed by State Supreme Court; U. S. Supreme Court denied hearing).

CRIMINAL DIGEST OFFER OF PROOF 

"If counsel desired to predicate an appeal upon error in the exclusion of evidence he was duty bound to fully and clearly state the purpose of such offered testimony to the trial court. This he did not do. It would result in a miscarriage of justice to permit counsel to tell the trial court that testimony is offered for one purpose and then, after the trial court has ruled, permit it to be urged on appeal that it was offered for an entirely different purpose." 

People vs. Brown, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 430.

OFFICERS-INTEREST IN CONTRACTS 

The Superintendent of the Mendocino Farm and County Hospital is not an "officer" within the meaning of Section 71 of the Penal Code as that term refers only to the duly elected or appointed officers of the state, county or other subdivision of the state and the county board had failed to appoint petitioner as a county physician but appointed him as Superintendent, an office for which it had no power to appoint and furthermore because he was merely appointed to serve at the will of the board and no term was fixed. 

Cleland vs. Sup Ct., 52 Cal. App. (2d) 530.

OTHER ACTS AND OFFENSES-GENERAL 

It is not necessary that offenses other than those charged be proved beyond a reasonable doubt. 

People vs. Lisenba, 14 Cal. (2d) 403, 429.

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620. 

OTHER ACTS AND OFFENSES-ADMISSIBLE 

In a prosecution for bigamy it was permissible to prove a first valid and existing marriage and the last, the third, and bigamous marriage but also a second marriage even though the evidence showed that it was bigamous. 

People vs. Burke, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 316.

In a prosecution for murder (committed in a robbery) and two robberies evidence of a robbery, committed under similar circumstances two days before the offenses charged by two of the five defendants and one person not identified and proof that another of the defendants pawned the watch taken in the first robbery held "clearly admissible as against these three appellants." 

The court does not, however, cite any authority nor state the theory of admissibility. 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802.

Defendants Bingham and Hancock were charged with a robbery of a service station. A gun was received in evidence similar to the gun used together with evidence that the gun belonged to Bingham. "Evidence was, therefore, admissible that defendant on the day following the robbery of which he here stands charged committed another robbery armed with a gun similar to the one received in evidence for the purpose of showing that defendant possessed such a revolver."
