(NOTE: In the robbery charged, Hancock (sole appellant) held the gun of which the victim saw only part of the barrel. In the other robbery each defendant had a gun, each taking turns in holding it and the victim testified that it was similar to the gun in evidence.)

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

87 "The law is established in California that when a defendant denies that he possessed a particular instrument alleged to have been used in the commission of an offense, evidence is admissible to show that such instrument was in fact possessed by the defendant at other times, and this is true whether the possession of the instrument by defendant is shown to have been prior or subsequent to the offense upon which he is being tried; nor is such evidence inadmissible because it tends to prove that defendant committed another crime, the evidence being relevant to an issue in the case on trial." 

People vs. Bingham, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 667. 

(NOTE: In concurring Justice Wood holds the evidence of the second robbery admissible since it tended to refute the testimony of defendant's wife in her attempt to establish an alibi she having testified he was in bed at 6:30 A. M. Aug. 12, and the second robbery having occurred at 7:50 A. M. the same day a considerable distance from where defendant lived. This was the theory upon which the trial judge received the evidence. C.W.F.) 

In a prosecution for conspiracy evidence of criminal acts not charged is admissible to show the existence of the conspiracy and it was for the jury to determine from the evidence whether the acts were committed by one or more of defendants in pursuance of the common plan. 

People vs. Black, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 87. 

In a prosecution for theft and violation of the Corporate Securities Act evidence of a prior investment by one of the complainants in a venture of defendant is admissible where such prior investment was used as a sales argument in the offense last prosecuted. Also admissible as tending to prove that appellants had knowledge of the necessity for a permit to sell stock. 

People vs. Flumerfelt, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 1. 

In a prosecution for theft by fraud, deceit and trickery, "Evidence of other similar transactions with the same common design as the criminal enterprise charged . . . is admissible to prove a general plan or scheme to defraud as well as to prove consciousness of guilt." 

People vs. Selk, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 140. "Inasmuch as appellant opened up the subject of difficulty in the other transactions (by his testimony on deceit examination, C.W.F.) appellant himself cannot close the door to an inquiry concerning those other transactions." 

People vs. Selk, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 140.

Evidence that tools, such as were used in the burglary, and a gun were found in the weeds outside of the building burglarized is admissible even though it would also bring out the fact that the gun had been stolen. 

People vs. Wilson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 218.

In a prosecution for bookmaking (337a, P. C.) defendant claimed error because the evidence, notebooks of defendant containing notations of bets on horse races upon days prior to his arrest, tended to prove him guilty of a crime not charged. "The rule against the admission of evidence of other crimes does not exclude such evidence when it tends to prove any fact necessary or pertinent to the proof of the crime for which a defendant is being tried." 

People vs. Hart, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 230.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Where defendant was charged with occupying a place for the purpose of receiving wagers on horse races and had in his direct examination testified that he was not taking bets at that place it was proper to ask him on crossexamination whether he had not some six months previous, admitted taking bets at that location as the prosecution was not limited to proof of defendant's conduct on the day of his arrest. 

People vs. Greco, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 628.

OTHER ACTS AND OFFENSES-ADMISSIBLE 

In a prosecution for selling stock without a license and grand theft by false pretenses evidence that defendant made similar representations to persons other than the complaining witness "was admissible to show a common plan, scheme and design to defraud in connection with the sale of worthless stock". 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735.

Evidence of other instances of subornation of perjury than were charged was received. "This evidence was admissible to show guilty knowledge and design and the intent of the defendant and to establish a definite prior design or system . . . and it is not essential that such similar transaction shall have resulted in the commission of a crime. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620. (Citing In a prosecution for abortion evidence of other acts and offenses is admissible to show defendant's intent in committing the act charged. 

People vs. Coltrin, 5 Cal. (2d) 649). 

People vs. Marineau, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 893. (NOTE: In this case the evidence of the other offense consisted in proof that defendant had been accused in his presence by a woman of having performed an abortion upon her and that defendant had not denied the accusation. The effect of this decision is that an admission by defendant of his commission of another offense is as admissible as would be direct proof of such offense. C.W.F.)

OTHER ACTS AND OFFENSES-INADMISSIBLE 

In a prosecution for murder defendant having killed one Perks with a Miss Lorraine, with whom defendant was living present, evidence that on the day before the homicide defendant and one Busch each had sexual relations with Miss Lorraine and defendant had indulged in an unnatural sexual act with her, there being no connection between these acts and the events of the homicide, constituted prejudicial error. 

People vs. Baylor, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 34.

OTHER ACTS AND OFFENSES-SEX CASES-ADMISSIBLE 

In a prosecution under Section 288 of the Penal Code "Proof of similar crimes against the same child is admissible to show motive and intent." 

Defendant contended that all he had ever done was to innocently rub the backs of (his) the children when they requested. "When a defendant maintains that an act of which he stands accused is an innocent act, it is proper to call witnesses who can testify to prior attempts by the defendant to commit the same offense with which he stands charged or similar offenses." 

Held that it was permissible to call a child, not named in the information and to show indecencies committed upon her to prove intent. 

People vs. Harrison, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 779.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

89 In a prosecution for incest evidence of other acts of sexual intercourse between the parties is admissible for the purpose of showing the "incestuous disposition of the defendant and the probability of his committing the act charged." 

People vs. Smithle, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 693.

OTHER ACTS AND OFFENSES-SEX CASES— INADMISSIBLE 

"The law is established in California that, in a prosecution for committing lewd and lascivious acts prohibited by Section 288 of the Penal Code, evidence that defendant has perpetrated similar acts with a person or persons other than the prosecuting witness is inadmissible and, if received, is prejudicial error." 

People vs. Huston, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 596.

PANDERING-GENERAL 

"Section 1 of that act (Act 1906, Statutes of 1911 defining pandering) recites a number of alternative situations under any one or more of which a person is to be deemed guilty of pandering. The alternatives recited in the statute are plainly not all materially exclusive of each other. For example, the second clause provides in effect that any person who 'by promises, threats, violence or by any device or scheme, shall cause, induce, persuade or encourage' a female person to become an inmate of a house of prostitution, is guilty of pandering. But the term 'procure' as used in the first clause of Section 1 of the statute necessarily implies the use of persuasion, solicitation, encouragement and assistance in achieving the unlawful purpose, and it follows that if a defendant is charged with 'procuring' a female as an inmate for a house of prostitution, he may be found guilty of such procurement if it is properly established that he either assisted, induced, persuaded or encouraged her to become an inmate." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1. "It is immaterial whether the female 'procured' is an innocent girl or a hardened prostitute of long experience; nor is it material that the procurement was either at her request or upon the defendant's own initiative. In either case the defendant has 'procured' a female within the meaning of the act and is guilty of pandering." 

People vs. Montgomery, supra.

The crime of pandering does not necessarily involve the question of specific intent. . . . Anyone who knowingly takes a part in the evil practice condemned is guilty of a violation of the law regardless of the state of his mind with relation to the act in question." 

People vs. Montgomery, supra.

To constitute the offense it is not necessary that the defendant should have been acting as an agent for a house of prostitution. 

People vs. Montgomery, supra.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"Appellant concedes that 'all appellant did was to permit Brenda Burns to work as a prostitute in houses of appellant.' This admission of appellant effectively disposes of all her contentions as to the sufficiency of the evidence, for if appellant knowlingly permitted Brenda Burns to work for appellant in appellant's houses of prostitution appellant was guilty of having procured the said female person as an inmate for a house of prostitution within the meaning of the first alternative description of pandering contained in Section 1 of the pandering act." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

PANDERING-PLEADING 

Information charging that defendant wilfully and feloniously did procure a female inmate, one X, for a house of prostitution, held good following 

People vs. Wright, 26 Cal. App. (2d) 197. 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

PAROLE 

Petitioner was sentenced to five years in the Washington State Penitentiary and was granted parole and permission to leave the state and go to California. Later his parole was revoked and he was arrested here. California and Washington are two of the states which have enacted the Uniform Act for Out of State Parolee Supervision under which the receiving state assumes the duties of supervision and provision is made for retaking parolees. 

It appeared that the compact had been fully ratified and authorized by the two states. Held that the compact was valid and constitutional, that the retaking of petitioner was not without due process of law but in accordance with the parole conditions and that this method of returning petitioner as a parolee, with parole revoked, to the State of Washington was proper and not affected by the statutes relating to extradition. 

In re Tenner, 20 Cal. (2d) 670. 

PERJURY-GENERAL 

A prosecution for perjury consisting of false testimony given upon his prior prosecution for a criminal offense is not barred because such former prosecution resulted in an acquittal. The former decision is not res judicata upon the perjury trial. 

People vs. Housman, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 627.

Defendant was charged with perjury in connection with his former trial on a narcotic charge in that at that trial he produced card records of narcotic prescriptions issued by him to one Black and one Brandon, which cards were not original records as represented but were deliberately fabricated by defendant after that prosecution was begun by the filing of complaints. Appellant contended that it was incumbent upon the state to produce two witnesses who would testify directly to the falsity of the cards and that the extra-judicial statements of the accused were not admissible for that purpose. "Of course, this is not the law. The rule applicable here is stated in People vs. McGee, 103 Cal. App. 149, 150 as follows: 'It is true that perjury must be proved by the testimony of two witnesses, or one witness and corroborating

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

91 circumstances. 

It is... necessary to have positive testimony as to facts that are absolutely incompatible with the innocence of the accused'." The case cited holds also that it is not necessary to produce a witness who was present and able to testify directly that what defendant had sworn to was not true. Held also that "the extra-judicial statements of the accused were admissible to supplement the direct evidence of perjury.” 

People vs. Housman, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 627.

Where two or more assignments of perjury are contained in the charge it is not necessary that all be proved. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620.

PHOTOGRAPHS 

Where it appears that a more acceptable photograph is not available the prosecution may use a police photograph of an alleged coconspirator of defendant for the purpose of identification. 

Case distinguished from People vs. Covington, 121 Cal. App. 61, where the use of a Rogues Gallery photograph of defendant on trial was produced as in the latter case, defendant being in court, there was no need to use his photograph for the purpose of identification. 

People vs. Marvin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 180.

Where one of two robbers had not been apprehended it was proper to show one of the victims of the robbery a photograph and to permit her to testify that it was a picture of the man who was with defendant in the perpetration of the robbery. 

People vs. Stein, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 281. 

Photographs of the deceased in a murder case showing the bullet holes and head injuries inflicted with the butt of the pistol held properly admissible. (Note: The court interjects the idea that there was nothing shocking, obscene or inflamatory about the pictures as though that had a bearing on admissibility. The court overlooks the fact that admissibility depends not upon these factors but upon relevancy, materiality and competence. C.W.F.) 

People vs. Coleman, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 592.

PICKETING 

A statute which makes it a crime "for any person to loiter, stand, walk or sit upon any public highway, alley, sidewalk or crosswalk so as to in any manner hinder or obstruct the free passage.... of persons or vehicles...." is so broad as to prohibit conduct of an obviously lawful character (such as peaceful picketing, shoppers looking at window displays, invalids in wheel chairs, etc.) and is therefore invalid.

A provision by ordinance making it a crime to picket by acts of violence is valid and the fact that such acts could be punished under the general laws of the state relating to assault, battery, riot, disturbing the peace and unlawful assembly does not create a conflict. 

In re Bell, 19 Cal. (2d) 488.

CRIMINAL DIGEST PLEA-GENERAL 

Defendant, upon arraignment in Municipal Court on a charge of robbery, represented by counsel, pleaded guilty and, in conformity to the provisions of Section 8 of Article 1 of the State Constitution and Section 859a of the 

Penal Code, the proceeding was certified to the Superior Court where he was duly arraigned and sentenced. Code section and constitutional provisions held valid and not violation of the Federal Constitution. 

People vs. Zolotoff, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 360. 

"Section 1017 of the Penal Code requires only that the plea be entered in the minutes and that it need not be taken down in shorthand by the reporter unless so ordered by the court." (Note: The sections says that the plea "shall not be taken down in shorthand by the official reporter unless so ordered by the court." C.W.F.) 

People vs. Palmer, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 567, 579. "There is no provision of the Penal Code requiring or permitting a special plea in reference to the statute of limitations." 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735.

PLEA-CHANGE OF 

Defendant was arrested with the body of deceased in his automobile, she having been dead about ten days. Defendant confessed freely to the officers and related the circumstances of the killing. He later entered pleas of guilty and not guilty by reason of insanity. Later, upon advice of his counsel, he withdrew his pleas, and pleaded guilty to murder in the first degree. Appellant contended that he entered this plea upon the assurance of the district attorney that if he pleaded guilty he would get a life sentence, appellant's counsel stating to the court that he had been so assured by the prosecutor. 

This statement, made in the presence of the prosecutor, was not denied. The evidence against defendant as to the circumstances of the killing consisted of defendant's version and hardly measure up to a case of first degree murder. 

Order denying defendant permission to change his plea to one of not guilty reversed. 

People vs. Griggs, 17 Cal. App. (2d) 662.

September 25, 1939, a complaint charging robbery was filed and on the same day defendant, in Municipal Court, pleaded guilty. September 27, 1939, in Superior Court defendant applied for probation. September 29, 1939, the court directed defendant be taken to the psychopathic hospital for examination and October 4, 1939, the insanity commission certified to the court that defendant was insane and he was committed to a state hospital. 

Whether paroled or discharged, the record does not show, the case was restored to the calendar September 18, 1940. Defendant's motion to withdraw plea of guilty and enter a plea of not guilty was denied. No showing was made other than appear on the court's record and personal knowledge. Held that nothing appearing to the contrary it must be assumed that defendant was insane at the time he pleaded guilty and that the denial of the right to change the plea called for a reversal of the judgment. 

People vs. Gallantier, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 148.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

93 Defendants moved to set aside pleas of guilty upon the ground that they were under the impression that the facts would show the burglary to be of the second degree only and would not have pleaded guilty had they known there was a possibility of the degree being fixed as first degree; they also claimed their pleas had been induced by promises or leniency, a claim denied by counter affidavits. Refusal of court to permit withdrawal of pleas of guilty sustained. 

People vs. Martin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 514.

Denial of leave to change plea of guilty to one of not guilty sustained. 

In addition to defendant's claims being controverted by testimony of his own counsel it appeared that following his plea of guilty defendant was allowed at large on bail, failed to appear and fled the jurisdiction and was not apprehended and returned for further proceedings 'til twelve years later. 

People vs. Palmer, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 567, 579. "Appellant's claim that he entered his plea of guilty solely on the advice of his counsel.... avails him nothing." 

People vs. Palmer, supra. "A motion to withdraw a plea of guilty and substitute a plea of not guilty is directed to the discretion of the trial court." Where no affidavit was filed in support of defendant's motion to change his plea and he did not offer to take the witness stand and the sole basis was defendant's unsworn statement to the probation officer that the prosecuting attorneys had assured him that they would recommend probation, denial of motion affirmed. 

People vs. Price, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 716.

POSSESSION OF MEANS TO COMMIT CRIME (SEE ALSO: IDENTIFICATION AND IDENTITY-PROPERTY; 

EXHIBITS). "The law is established in California that when a defendant denies that he possessed a particular instrument alleged to have been used in the commission of an offense evidence is admissible to show that such instrument was in fact possessed by the defendant at other times and this is true whether the possession of the instrument by defendant is shown to have been prior or subsequent to the offense upon which he is being tried; nor is such evidence inadmissible because it tends to prove that defendant committed another crime, the evidence being relevant to an issue in the case on trial." 

People vs. Bingham, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 667.

Evidence of possession of a gun similar to that used is admissible even though it appear that the gun had been stolen. 

People vs. Wilson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 218.

POSSESSION OF STOLEN PROPERTY 

"It appears from the evidence that defendant was driving a car containing stolen property shortly (seven hours) after the theft; that when stopped by the officers after pursuit at high speed) he immediately fled and abandoned the car, later denying that he had at any time driven the car. 

The evidence is sufficient to sustain the verdict." (Grand theft case). 

People vs. Latham, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 35.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

In addition to finding shortly after the burglary a large part of the stolen property in defendant's hotel room it was established that defendant had registered at the hotel room under an assumed name, engaged in sales of part of the stolen property under the name "Spot;" that he negotiated the sale of property such as was stolen before the burglary and checks stolen in the burglary were found in his room. Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Shaw, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 768.

PRELIMINARY EXAMINATION 

Where a defendant pleads guilty before the magistrate the latter may immediately commit defendant to the custody of the sheriff and certify the case to the Superior Court and may, but is not required to take any testimony before such certification. (Sec. 859a P. C.)

People vs. Donegan, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 221. 

PRESENTING FALSE CLAIMS FOR INSURANCE 

Reversal for insufficiency of evidence to prove falsity. 

People vs. Nichols, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 31.

PRESUMPTIONS 

"The instructions declare that the presumption of innocence is the only presumption allowable in a criminal case, that it is not overcome by any other presumption but does overcome all other presumptions of whatsoever kind or nature. Such, however, is not the law. All presumptions are evidence. 

Conclusive presumptions (Code Civ. Pro. Sections 1961, 1962) are not overcome by the presumption of innocence, nor are many disputable presumptions so overcome."

Section 1963 of the Code of Civil Procedure does not list all of the disputable presumptions. Thus the common law presumption of unlawfulness in unlawful imprisonment cases still exists in this state though not enumerated in the statute. 

People vs. Le Doux, 155 Cal. 535, 553, followed and quoted: 

People vs. Agnew, 16 Cal. (2d) 655, 662.

PRESUMPTIONS-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court refused to instruct that "defendant is presumed not only to be innocent of the offenses charged in the indictment, but of any other offense, and you must presume that the defendant was innocent in all of his other acts and transactions revealed by the evidence, unless you are convinced to the contrary by evidence beyond a reasonable doubt." Held that it is not necessary that offenses, other than those charged, must be proved beyond a reasonable doubt. (Note: The court cites no authority but an 

Indiana case, evidently not knowing that the point was decided in People vs. Lisenba, 14 Cal. (2d) 403, 429, in which our Supreme Court follows and quotes the Indiana case and discusses the point. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

95 55 

PRINCIPAL AND ACCESSORY-GENERAL 

Where two women picked up the victim and his companion in a drinking place and suggested that he accompany them to another drinking place; that one of them made a telephone call and shortly thereafter the party went outside where the victim and his companion were introduced to three young men in a roadster and all got into the car; that they drove to another drinking place where the victim and one of the women became separated from the companion and the other woman; that the companion was given a drink, evidently drugged and later came to discovering that he had been slugged and robbed and it appeared that the victim had been slugged and robbed and died from the slugging and that the woman Shirley Kay in another robbery involving at least two of the defendants had pawned part of the stolen property. Held sufficient to connect Shirley Kay as a principal though not present at the actual robbery and murder. 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802.

Acts of defendant in assisting her codefendant brothers in disposing of property stolen in a burglary and cashing a check received therefor, possession of the key to a garage from which tools were stolen which tools in part were found in the burglarized house and in part were found in an automobile occupied by her codefendants, etc. held proof of aiding and abetting. 

People vs. McCarthy, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 278. "The word 'abet' implies knowledge of the wrongful purpose of the perpetrator of the crime." 

People vs. Stein, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 470.

PRINCIPAL AND ACCESSORY-INSTRUCTIONS 

Defendant contended it was error to instruct on the rule of aiders and abettors since, under the evidence, he was described as the principal actor in the robbery. Held there was no error. "To 'abet' another in the commission of a crime implies a consciousness of guilt in instigating, encouraging, promoting or aiding the commission of such criminal offense." 

People vs. Best, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 100.

PRIOR CONVICTION-GENERAL 

(See also: Former Jeopardy, Habitual Criminal, Petit Theft with Prior

Conviction, Second Offense, Impeachment). 

While section 1025 of the Penal Code provides that when a prior conviction is admitted upon arraignment it shall not be alluded to during the trial this rule has recognized exceptions. Hence where the evidence is otherwise admissible it is not to be excluded because it incidentally tends to prove such prior conviction. 

People vs. Murray, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 209.

Where defendant admits, prior to the trial that he has suffered the prior conviction charged testimony as to defendant's prior conviction is violative of Subd. 1, Section 1093 of the Penal Code and prejudicial error but the effect of such error is removed where defendant thereafter on cross-examination testifies to such prior conviction. 

People vs. Jeffries, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 801.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

PRISONERS 

Prosecution of supervisor of Whittier State School for inflicting corporal punishment on inmates. Section 681 (Laws 1933) prohibits all forms of corporal punishment of prisoners and inmates of institutions. 

Force may, however, be used if necessary to the maintenance of order, or to prevent misconduct by inmates but this power does not include corporal punishment after the act has been completed. 

Since corporal punishment of inmates is itself a violation of the section an allegation that the health of the inmate was thereby injured and impaired is surplusage and need not be proved. 

People vs. McMillan, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 821.

PRIVILEGED COMMUNICATION-ATTORNEY AND CLIENT 

In support of his petition for coram nobis defendant testified that his attorney had told him that it would be necessary to waive a jury trial to secure a continuance. The attorney testified, without objection, that he had not made such a statement. "No objection was made to the testimony of Mr. 

Lipman and the privilege, if any existed after appellant testified to his version of his conversations with his attorney, was waived." 

People vs. Corsalini, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 704.

Where defendant's counsel was the statutory agent and the custodian of the books and records of the corporation whose stock the defendant is charged to have sold without a permit the facts acquired by such attorney from such corporate records were not acquired by him as the result of the relation of attorney and client and his testimony based on such records is not privileged. 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735.

To be privileged "the communication must be confidential, and so regarded, at least by the client at the time. If it clearly appears that the same was not intended by the client to have been confidential, it is not privileged. 

Communications made in the presence of a third party who is present as a witness are not privileged. 

People vs. Hall, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 386.

PROBATION 

The authority to revoke a prisoner's probation does not even depend upon his violation of the specific conditions of the probation. The order suspending his sentence may be set aside if the court has reason to believe from the report of the probation officer or otherwise that he is associating with lawless or improper persons or that he is engaged in criminal practices. 

People vs. Hunter, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 87.

The fact that, for violation of probation, defendant was sentenced and committed to prison subsequent to the expiration of his period of probation is immaterial where his probation has been revoked prior to the expiration of the probationary term. It was not necessary for defendant to be present when probation was revoked. 

People vs. Hunter, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 87.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

97 Following a plea of guilty to petty theft in the Municipal Court of Los Angeles, defendant's counsel asked leave to file an application for probation. 

This the court denied and then proceeded, “The sentence will be as follows: 

As to each defendant, I hereby sentence the defendants and each of them to 180 days in the City Jail; 90 days of said sentenced is ordered suspended; 

90 days to be served. "In the absence of a denial of probation, the suspension of a sentence or a part thereof has been recognized as in fact the granting of probation, although not accompanied by any other evidence that that was the court's purpose. (In re Herron, 217 Cal. 400). The proceedings just preceding the imposition of the sentence in this case, viewed in the light of the immediate subsequent action taken by the trial court, may possibly be explained as not a denial of probation, but as expressive of a desire on the part of the court to avoid referring the matter to the probation officer and thereafter having to consider his report before granting probation. If this explanation is the correct one, then we have an error of procedure, which occurred before judgment and so is reviewable on an appeal from the judgment. 

The error of procedure consists in disregarding the mandatory provisions of Section 1203, Penal Code, which, after providing that probation may be summarily denied, continues 'if probation is not denied, the court must immediately refer the matter to the probation officer to investigate ... 

"We are of the opinion, in view of these clear provisions, that while probation may be summarily denied, it is erroneous summarily to grant it." "But if probation was not being granted, then the trial court had no authority to suspend a part of the sentence (Ex parte Howard, 72 Cal. App. 

374 and cases cited) and tested on habeas corpus the suspension would be held a nullity and the sentences would be regarded as straight jail sentences of 180 days each."

In another case considered at the same time and in the same opinion the court, without any attempt to proceed under section 1203 sentenced a defendant to 180 days “suspended two years; probation granted, defendant to serve one hundred and seventy-eight days." As to this the court says "What has actually been ordered, of course, is the suspension of only two days of each 180 day sentence, and if we recognize this as the 'act of grace and clemency' which the granting of probation is supposed to be and not as an attempt to deprive the County Board of Parole Commissioners of the authority vested in it to grant parole, then we alone of all those charged with responsibilities in the matter are blind to the true state of things. 

These 'orders of probation,' moreover, were made without reference to and report from the probation officer, and for this reason, too, should be set aside." 

People vs. Lopez, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 854. "The fact that the defendant spent the first thirty days of her probationary period in the county jail does not amount to her having served a term of imprisonment in a penal institution.” 

People vs. Wallach, 8 Cal. App. (2d) 129, 133.

The granting or denying of an application for probation is a matter within the discretion of the trial judge and his reading aloud of a statement of the probation officer from his report in denying probation does not show an abuse of discretion. 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 690.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Although defendant, who had pleaded guilty to burglary, had suffered two prior convictions of burglary, admitted by him on the probation hearing though not charged, the court placed him on probation and several years later revoked probation and sentenced him to the state prison. Held that though the court erred in granting probation, defendant was not prejudiced by such error. (Note: The court also expresses the opinion that "neither the order granting probation, nor the order revoking probation nor the judgment of conviction was void. C. W. F. ) 

People vs. Scranton, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 492.

After imposing a sentence of a fine and 90 days imprisonment the City 

Judge suspended the jail sentence. "Justice's and police courts may now grant probation summarily. Thus, by suspending the jail sentence the court granted the defendant probation." 

In re Goetz, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 848.

An order placing defendant on probation, even though it include as a condition a period of imprisonment in jail or the paying of a fine, is not a judgment and sentence. (Note: While correctly announcing the above rule, the court evidently failed to appreciate the fact that defendant was sentenced to pay a fine and to serve a term of days in the county jail prior to the making of the order resulting in defendant being placed on probation. Hence the court's statement, "It is clear that here we do not have a judgment " is not correct. C. W. F. ) 

In re Goetz, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 848, 851. • • • 9 Defendant having been convicted in Municipal Court of violating Sections 502 and 481 of the Vehicle Code the court, on hearing of the application for probation made the order, "Probation denied. Sentence on the 502 

V. C. charge is 30 days in the city jail and a fine of $100, 20 days of said sentence suspended upon the payment of the $100 fine; sentence on the 481 

V. C. charge is 1 day in the city jail, suspended . . . . 

The appellate court says: If the words 'probation denied' had been excluded from the order of the court here, leaving only the suspension of a part of the sentence imposed, its action would have been regarded as an informal order granting probation and given effects as such. (In re Herron 

217 Cal. 400; Ex parte Slattery, 163 Cal. 176; People vs Wallach, 8 Cal. App. (2d) 129.) But here we have an application for probation, a hearing upon it and an express denial of probation. Upon such a record there is no room for an implication or construction of the immediately following orders suspending part of one sentence and all of the other as an informal granting of probation." "Where the court does not act under the probation law it has no power to suspend any part of the sentence and an order purporting to do so is void." "Since there was no probation in these cases, the attempt to dismiss them under the probation law is void; and the convictions still stand....

Ellis vs. Dept. Motor Vehicles, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 753. 

The previous conviction of a felony which prevents the granting of probation must be a conviction suffered prior to the commission of the offense for which probation is asked. A conviction subsequent to the instant case will not preclude probation.

People vs. Superior Court, 208 Cal. 688.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

99 

PRODUCTION OF DOCUMENTS 

The court refused to permit defense to see a police report of an interview by a police officer with the complaining witness. This report had not been used to refresh the memory of a witness, he stating merely that such report had been prepared and was in his possession. Held that the report was confidential, not admissible and the request properly refused. 

People vs. Santora, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 707. 

PROHIBITION, WRIT OF 

The writ of prohibition is a proper remedy if it appears that the facts charged do not constitute a criminal offense. 

Cleland vs. Superior Court, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620. (Citing Greenberg vs. Superior Court, 19 Cal. (2d) 319, in which insufficiency of evidence before the grand jury was held basis for granting the writ. See GRAND JURY.) PUBLIC RECORDS 

"As stated in People vs. Tomalty, 14 Cal. App. 224, and the authorities there cited. In order that an entry or record of the official acts of a public officer shall be a public record it is not necessary that such record be expressly required by law to be kept, but it is sufficient if it be necessary or convenient to the discharge of his official duty'." 

People vs. Shaw, 17 Cal. (2d) 820.

RAPE-GENERAL 

"Nor is it essential that the testimony of the prosecutrix be corroborated for it has long since been the law that such uncorroborated testimony, considered alone, may be sufficient to sustain a conviction, even though discrepancies appear therein." 

People vs. Zemavasky, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 847.

The fact that prosecutrix had been carrying on intimate relations with a man other than defendant would not serve as a defense. 

People vs. Zemavasky, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 847. (Case reversed on other point in 20 Cal. (2d) 56).

RAPE-FORCIBLE-GENERAL 

"As to the element of violence the girl testified in effect that the reason she did not offer greater resistance was that she believed he had a 'real gun'; that she was afraid and fearful of being shot, which was sufficient to establish the element of violence." (Note: Defendant denied any sexual act. The pistol which defendant displayed later proved to be a toy pistol. 

C. W. F.) 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

RAPE, FORCIBLE CHASTITY OF PROSECUTRIX 

A witness being asked as to the reputation of the prosecutrix for chastity answered "Well, if you call a drinking person bad I would say it was bad."

Held that the court properly struck out the answer as reputation for sobriety was not in issue and was utterly foreign to reputation for chastity. 

People vs. Bumbaugh, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 791.

Evidence that the prosecutrix had had sexual intercourse is not admissible as tending to reflect upon the credibility of the prosecutrix. 

People vs. Murphy, 53 Cal. App. 472. 

Cited and followed: People vs. Vivian, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 533.

Where want of consent is an element of the rape charged evidence of prosecutrix' want of chastity is admissible to show probability of consent. 

People vs. Battalina, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 685.

RAPE, STATUTORY-CHASTITY OF PROSECUTRIX 

Evidence that prosecutrix had had sexual intercourse with other men is not admissible as showing a want of credibility of her testimony. 

People vs. Vivian, 50 Cal. (2d) 533.

Nor is such evidence admissible on the issue of consent since the girl was 15, under the age of consent, and voluntarily engaged in the act with defendant. 

People vs. Vivian, supra.

RAPE 

STATUTORY-COMPLAINT OF PROSECUTRIX 

"It is true that failure to make a complaint is strong evidence against a claim of unwillingness, but it is not necessarily conclusive. Furthermore, the rule is predicated upon cases where rape by force and violence is charged.... Want of consent is not an element in the crime charged. Hence it follows that failure to make complaint has little weight or relevancy in a case of this character, although it is a fact which the jury may consider in connection with the credibility of the witness." 

People vs. Fremont, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 341.

REASONABLE DOUBT-INSTRUCTIONS 

"Complaint is made of the refusal of the court to instruct the jury that in case of two theories, one pointing to innocence and the other to guilt, they should adopt the theory pointing to innocence. Such an instruction might be applicable to a case where the evidence is circumstantial, but where, as here, the evidence is direct there is no occasion for giving the instruction." 

People vs. Marvich, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 858.

Case reversed because of failure of the court to give the first sentence of the statutory instruction. (The court expresses the opinion that the reasonable doubt instruction is one which must be given even if there be no request therefor. C. W. F.). 

People vs. Soldavini, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 460.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

101 (Note: The case does not refer to but states a rule contrary to People vs. Cuin, 27 Cal. App. 316, People vs. Ohern, 93 Cal. 518, and People vs.

Christensen, 85 Cal. 570, in which it was held not error to fail to give a reasonable doubt instruction in the absence of a request therefor. The rule of reasonable doubt is, however, so basic that the Soldavini case is supported by better reasoning. C. W. F.)

In addition to reading section 1096 of the Penal Code the court added: "The law does not require demonstration or that degree of proof which, excluding all possibility of error, produces absolute certainty, for such degree of proof is rarely possible. Only that degree of proof is necessary which convinces the mind and directs and satisfies the conscience of those who are bound to act conscientiously upon it." 

The court says: "Although the reading of section 1096 was sufficient to inform the jury on the subjects covered therein, the court was not restricted to the reading of the section but could in the exercise of its discretion amplify the definition of reasonable doubt. (People vs. Medalgi, 94 Cal. App. 543). The additional instruction correctly states the rule which has long been followed in this state." 

People vs. Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 411. 

...

An instruction that the prosecution must prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the death of the deceased "could not have been caused . . . . by any person other than the defendant" is properly refused where there is no evidence to support the theory that some person other than defendant committed the murder.

People vs. Mandell, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 806. 

An instruction that offenses other than those charged if provable must be established by proof beyond a reasonable doubt is properly refused as that degree of proof is required only for the offense charged. 

See also: 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620. 

People vs. Lisenba, 14 Cal. (2d) 403, 429.

Where the court has instructed the jury in the language of section 1096 of the Penal Code any further instruction on the subject of reasonable doubt is rendered unnecessary. 

People vs. McEvers, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 548.

RECEIVING STOLEN PROPERTY-GENERAL 

Circumstances held sufficient to justify conviction. 

People vs. Murray, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 209.

One Detter, 14 years old, stole a watch and sold same to appellant. In making out the report of sale, instead of describing the boy, appellant described a fictitious female and the boy signed the name Dolores Dodge. 

The boy had presented, at request of appellant's mother, a letter purporting to give him, under the name of John Dodge, authority to sell the watch, the note being signed Dolores Dodge, ostensibly the boy's mother. Appellant made no investigation other than questions asked the boy. "If the inquiry of the seller alone were sufficient in and of itself, the requirement of the section (496bb Penal Code) would be of little force

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

and effect. The appellant suspected the boy's right of sale. but made no inquiry to ascertain the legal right of sale. The presumption that the defendant knew that the property was stolen, therefore, had to be rebutted by proof as required by Penal Code, Section 496bb, Subdivision 2. Convic tion sustained. 

People vs. Seerman, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 506.

A group of men who were engaged in the business of stripping automobiles and had participated in some 600 transactions had, during the course of their activities, arranged with defendant to sell for them all of the goods so stolen and defendant received many automobile parts and accessories from the thieves and sold the same for their mutual benefit. 

The evidence warranted the conclusion that defendant was a party to a conspiracy to steal such goods. Held as to defendant's conviction that, while perhaps guilty of theft he could not be guilty of receiving stolen property, that "While it is true that he could not have received from himself the fruits of his own larceny yet his failure to participate in the actual asportation left him in the position to feloniously receive the stolen property from his companions in crime. Although the evidence would have warranted the charge of theft upon the theory that the defendant was an accessory thereto, still he was guilty of receiving stolen property taken and asported by his confederates." (Note: In using the word "accessory" in the last sentence the court evidently meant an accessory before the fact, in other words a principal. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Stoddard, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 86. There was evidence by police officers that defendant called them in connection with property he had received. 

Conviction sustained even though under defendant's version he was only proceeding to aid the police in apprehending thieves. 

People vs. Steinberg, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 221.

The word "hot" as used with reference to personal property which a person is trying to sell as in that sense it was used in the declaration of the defendant Chaus conveyed one of Webster's definitions, "recently stolen knew it. The court also quotes one of Webster's definitions, "recently stolen or otherwise illegally obtained." 

People vs. Knott, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 439.

REFRESHING THE MEMORY 

"But, in view of the apparent reversal of the witness from her testimony at the preliminary hearing, the district attorney was within his rights." 

People vs. Nobles, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 422.

A deputy sheriff who spoke both English and Spanish questioned defendant in Spanish and the questions and answers were interpreted by him into English and so taken down by a stenographer. Held that the deputy sheriff could refresh his memory from the stenographer's transcript. "The law is established that one who is present at a conversation and has heard the same and knows the facts may use any writing whether made by himself or another to refresh his memory when subsequently testifying." 

People vs. Amaya, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 656.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

103 A witness seventy-six years of age was obviously confused at the trial. 

Frequently after she failed to testify on certain matters, the deputy district attorney would refresh her recollection by first letting her read portions of her testimony given at the preliminary examination. After reading this former testimony the former testimony was read aloud to her and she answered that her former testimony did refresh her recollection and that it was true. "The law is clear in this state that where a witness called by a party fails to testify to matters previously within his recollection, or gives evidence somewhat at variance with his former evidence, the party producing the witness may with propriety refresh the recollection of the witness by using the transcript of his previous testimony. (People vs. Durrant, 116 Cal. 179; 

People vs. Duncan, 8 Cal. App. 186; People vs. Izlar, 8 Cal. App. 600; People vs. Selby, 198 Cal. 426.)" 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. 735.

ROBBERY-GENERAL 

Defendant, under menace of a gun forced a taxi driver to pay $2.50 for the rent of two rooms in an auto court, the driver paying the money to the proprietor of the court. Conviction sustained. (Note: Case is authority on the point that if the victim is deprived of his property by force or fear and against his consent it is robbery whether the defendant personally received the property or whether the property was disposed of by the victim as defendant commanded. C. W. F.)

People vs. Headlee, 42 A. C., A. 266. 

"The other elements being present the crime is made out even though the property taken be of slight value." "Proof of motive or conspiracy to commit the robbery is not essential to a conviction for neither deliberation nor premeditation is made an element of the crime." 

People vs. Thomas, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 128. "On the charge of robbery, the facts that the perpetrator was unarmed, or, being armed, made no use of the weapon, do not necessarily establish that personal property was taken from the person of another without the use of force or fear." 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39. 

Defendant approached an army lieutenant and his girl companion who were seated near a park entrance when defendant approached them, pointed a pistol and said, "This is a stickup," and robbed the lieutenant of his wallet. At the time the victims believed it to be a real gun but it later develops that it was a metal cap pistol weighing a little over a pound, about 64 inches long and 4 1-4 from the butt of the grip to the top of the barrel, approximating the size and general appearance a real automatic pistol. 

Appellant claimed that there was nothing to sustain the conclusion that the robbery was of the first degree. The court in reviewing earlier cases comments: "To fix it as of the first degree it is not necessary that the weapon should be a deadly weapon, if, in fact it is a 'dangerous' weapon. 

Robbery committed with an unloaded pistol may be robbery of the first degree. The pistol need not be used nor intended to be used as a firearm.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

· • If it is capable of use as a club or as instrument to produce bodily harm it may be classified as a 'dangerous' weapon . . It is immaterial whether such weapon is actually used. ." Conviction of first degree robbery sustained. (Naturally where there can be a difference of opinion under the particular circumstances the question as to whether the instrument used is a "dangerous weapon" is one for determination by the jury. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Coleman, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 39.

SEARCH AND SEIZURE 

Defendants were indicted for grand theft by fraudulent representations regarding an alleged money making machine. Officers, without any warrant, entered the apartment of one of the defendants in his absence and took therefrom certain bottles of liquid, used in connection with the alleged money making, and the machine. Defendants objected to the introduction in evidence of these exhibits, claiming a violation of both State and Federal Constitution. Before the trial, the trial court granted a temporary injunction restraining the officer from testifying regarding the entry of the apartment, but at the trial the trial court refused to enforce the injunction and permitted the officer to testify. 

"The Fourth Amendment to the Constitution to the United States pro hibits unreasonable searches and seizures by federal officers. Pursuant to this mandate the federal courts forbid the introduction in court of evidence obtained by an illegal search or seizure if a timely motion for its exclusion is made by the accused. (Cases cited. C. W. F.) The California Constitution contains an identical provision (Cal. Const. Article I, Sec. 19) but the accepted rule in this state, as in many others, permits the introduction of improperly obtained evidence on the ground that the illegality of the search and seizure does not affect the admissibility of the evidence." (Cases cited. C. W. F.) "The Fourth Amendment to the Constitution to the United States is not a limitation upon the states (Cases cited C. W. F.) and California is free to interpret its own constitution. The use of evidence obtained through does not violate due process of law for it does not affect the fairness or impartiality of the trial." an illegal search and seizure ... 

People vs. Gonzales, 20 Cal. (2d) 165.

An injunction will not lie to prevent the trial court from receiving such evidence. 

Chierotti vs. Police Dept., 20 Cal. (2d) 895.

Some of defendant's papers were taken from his office without a search warrant. Defendant brought an action for the return of these papers and at the commencement of the trial in the case at bar was informed that the case had been decided against him. He moved for a continuance of the criminal case until he could get a decision or appeal in the claim and delivery case and also asked the trial court in this case to order the papers returned to him, both of which requests were denied and over objection the papers were received in evidence. Held the trial court did not abuse its discretion. 

People vs. Beilfuss, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 641.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

105 

SECOND OFFENSE 

Defendant pleaded guilty to charges of receiving stolen property, in the Municipal Court, and the cases were certified to the Superior Court where the complaints were amended on their face to charge defendant, in the language required by section 644 of the Penal Code, with five prior felony convictions, the amendments being made pursuant to an order of the court. Later the allegations as to three of the priors were dismissed as not within the scope of section 644. Defendant admitted the remaining two priors and was sentenced as an habitual criminal. Held that the amendment was authorized by section 969% of the Penal Code and, also, that it was not necessary that the amendments be signed as they became an integral part of the complaint. 

People vs. Carson, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 554.

A certified transcript of the prison records plus defendant's admission that he had served time held sufficient to sustain the allegation of prior conviction. (Note: Case does not disclose whether defendant's answer came in by way of impeachment or how it was adduced. C. W. F.). 

People vs. Fletcher, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

The fact that the defendant spent the first thirty days of her probationary period in the county jail does not amount to her having served a term of imprisonment in a penal institution. 

People vs. Wallach, 8 Cal. App. (2d) 129, 133.

One defendant was charged with the possession of a deadly weapon, a pistol, after conviction of robbery and burglary in March, 1930, and the co-defendant was charged with possession of a sawed off shotgun after a forgery conviction in January, 1931. The clerk in reading these charges read those portions reciting said prior convictions but did not read the portion of the information which separately alleged prior convictions of defendants which allegations they had admitted. Held there was no error and that section 1025 of the Penal Code refers only to those separate allegations of prior convictions pleaded for the purpose of enabling the imposition of greater punishment and not to an allegation of a prior conviction which is one of the elements of the offense for which defendant is on trial. 

People vs. Schunke, 47 C. A. (2d) 542. 

While the judgment entered by the clerk included the charge of a prior conviction and appellant's admission thereof neither of these facts was mentioned in the oral judgment of the court. Held there was no merit in the claim that the judgment entered was not the judgment rendered, since section 1207 of the Penal Code requires the clerk in entering the judgment to state "briefly the offense for which the conviction was had and the fact of a prior conviction." 

People vs. Shack, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 16.

SELF CRIMINATION 

During the trial the court ordered defendants to rise in order to be observed by witnesses testifying to their identity. Death penalty murder case. One of defendants was also required to remove a visor he was wearing in court. Held that such orders did not violate the right of a defendant not to be a witness against himself. Cases reviewed briefly. 

People vs. Clark, 18 Cal. (2d) 449.

CRIMINAL DIGEST SEPARATE OFFENSES (See also: VERDICTS, INCONSISTENT). 

A charge of grand theft pleaded separately from a count charging a violation of Section 503 of the Vehicle Code will support a conviction of grand theft even though defendant is acquitted on the charge of violating section 503 based upon the same transaction. 

People vs. Jeffries, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 801.

Defendant was charged with one count of statutory rape and two counts of violation of Section 288 of the Penal Code. Prior to the submission of the case the count charging rape was dismissed because there was no evidence of penetration. Held that the dismissal did not operate as an acquittal of defendant of the charge of violating section 288 as those offenses were separate and distinct from the charge of rape and made up of different elements. The court adds the contention is answered by section 954 of the 

Penal Code which provides that "A verdict of acquittal of one or more counts shall not be deemed or held to be an acquittal of any other count." (Cases cited). 

People vs. Kearney, 20 Cal. (2d) 435. 

(See also cases under VERDICT-INCONSISTENT). 

SELF DEFENSE-INSTRUCTIONS 

The court refused an instruction that the appellate court considers as being an instruction that to entitle defendant to an acquittal on the ground of self defense it is only necessary for him to create a reasonable doubt as to his guilt. The court gave no instruction to the effect that if they had a reasonable doubt as to whether the killing was in self defense they should acquit. Held reversible error. (Note: The instruction refused was poorly drawn and referred only to reasonable doubt of guilt rather than to a reasonable doubt as to whether the killing was in self defense. The instruction refused amounts to no more than a reasonable doubt instruction with the words "and when a defendant produces evidence of self defense" inserted. The case does not hold that the court should have instructed on its own motion. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Carson, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 40.

Held error to refuse instruction that a person in exercising his right of self defense may not only stand his ground and defend himself when attacked but may pursue his adversary until he has secured himself from danger. (Note: Facts in opinion do not clearly show that the instruction was applicable to the facts, viz. that defendant pursued the deceased and, unless the evidence did so show, it would seem that the instruction was properly refused. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Carson, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 40.

The court refused to add, to the instruction that, in self defense, the person assailed may pursue his adversary until he has secured himself from danger, the words, "and he may pursue him in like manner, though the assailant has withdrawn from the combat, when he has reasonable ground to apprehend that the assailant will presently return and renew the assault." Such is not the law. 

People vs. Giovanni, 45 Cal. App. (2) d) 302.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

107 Where as the result of one fraudulent representation the defendant received sums of money on a number of occasions each such receipt was a separate offense. (See this case under Theft-General).

People vs. Serna, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 106. 

Defendant was charged, based upon one act of sexual intercourse with the same female, with forcible rape in one count and statutory rape in a second count. Held that but one offense had been committed. 

People vs. Craig, 17 Cal. (2d) 487.

Where after committing an act in violation of section 286 of the Penal Code defendant committed acts which would constitute by themselves a violation of section 288 of the Penal Code a conviction under both counts is proper. 

People vs. Karpinski, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 545.

Where there was but one transaction and one unlawful taking there was but one theft even though the offense was charged in three counts under three different theories of theft. In such a case the conviction on more than one count cannot be sustained and only one conviction can be sustained. 

People vs. Selk, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 140.

The crime of receiving stolen property is separate and distinct from the crimes of burglary and robbery and the dismissal of a count charging such offense does not have any effect upon a conviction of the latter two offenses. 

People vs. Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 411. 

Defendants were charged with and convicted of the crimes of conspiracy to commit robbery, robbery and murder, the murder conviction carrying with it the death penalty. The separate counts were connected in their commission, the conspiracy to rob accomplishing the robbery which in turn involved the murder. Held that there were three separate and distinct crimes. 

People vs. Hoyt, 20 Cal. (2d) 306.

Since the crime of soliciting a person to commit perjury is complete with the act of soliciting but subornation of perjury would not be complete without action by the intended witness the crime of soliciting and subornation are separate offenses. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620.

Defendant was charged in count I with forcible rape under subdivision 3 of section 261 of the Penal Code and in Count II with rape committed under the influence of threats contrary to subdivision 4 of the section.. "Clearly the code designates those offenses as separate and distinct crimes.” (It must be noted, however, that defendant had, on the occasion in question, committed the sexual act more than once, hence the acts would be separate offenses. C. W. F.)

People vs. Battlina, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 685. 

Defendant was charged with and convicted of grand theft, extortion and robbery. "The accused could have been guilty of all three offenses if he intended to commit them. An examination of the facts recited above shows that the defendant and his associates intended but one offense and that there was but one taking. one offense and not three were committed." But, since the sentences ran concurrently the judgment was affirmed. 

People vs. Seigel, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 325.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Based upon the facts conviction of assault by force and means likely to produce great bodily injury and forcible rape sustained. The force element in rape need only be sufficient to overcome resistance and when in addition to the rape, a defendant commits a felonious assault, over and above the assault involved in the rape, both offenses are committed. 

People vs. McIlvain, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 366.

SEPARATE OFFENSES-INSTRUCTIONS 

Where but one offense was committed but it was charged on three theories-grand theft, extortion and robbery, it was not incumbent upon the court, of its own motion, to instruct the jury that they could convict under only one count. (Jury convicted on all counts, but, as sentences were made to run concurrently, the judgment was affirmed. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Seigel, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 325.

SEXUAL PSYCHOPATH 

"The court has a sound discretion under Section 5501, supra (Welfare and Institutions Code) to determine from the affidavits filed and the evidence adduced whether the petitioner is in fact a sexual psychopath as defined by the preceding section of that code . . . The court is not ousted of jurisdiction over the criminal proceeding by the mere filing of an affidavit asserting that the accused is a sexual psychopath. It is not mandatory that the court shall adjourn the criminal proceeding when the affidavit is filed. 

The word 'may' as it is used in that section should not be construed as a mandatory direction to the court regardless of the sufficiency of the showing. It is merely discretionary. Unless there is an abuse of that discretion the ruling of the court in that regard may not be disturbed on appeal." "The language of Section 5501, 'If when any person is charged with a crime', was intended to mean charged with a crime having some relation to the type of offense referred to in Section 5500." (Sexual offenses against children).

Defendant was charged with robbery, kidnaping and mayhem, the latter charge being based upon the fact that defendant, after kidnaping some boys between 17 and 21 years of age, tortured them by maiming and injuring their privates. The court says these crimes are not related in any manner to sexual offenses against children and that there was no abuse of discretion in the court's refusal to go into a trial as to whether defendant was a sexual psychopath. 

People vs. Haley, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 618.

SOLICITING PERSON TO COMMIT CRIME- 

653f, Penal Code

In order for a person to be guilty of soliciting a person to commit perjury it is not necessary that the person solicited shall actually commit perjury. 

People vs. Gray, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 620.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

109 

STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION-GENERAL 

"Language prohibiting conduct that may (viz. lawfully, C. W. F.) be prohibited and conduct that may not, affords no reasonable and ascertainable standard of guilt and is therefore too uncertain and vague to be enforced. A conviction based upon such a statute cannot stand even though the acts of misconduct in the particular case could be validly prohibited by properly drafted legislation." 

In re Bell, 19 Cal. (2d) 488.

STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION-INCONSISTENT STATUTES 

In considering Section 435 of the Penal Code making it a misdemeanor to engage in any business or calling without the license required by law and the Small Loan Act providing a special and different penalty for making loans without a license, the court states the rule: "When an inconsistency exists between a statute dealing generally and one dealing specifically with a phase of the same subject matter, the special statute is controlling." 

People vs. Vanderpool, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 685.

STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION-LOCAL AND STATE LAW COVERING SAME SUBJECT 

While a city or county may not pass an ordinance covering and punishing the same act covered by a state law, both enactments utilizing the same corpus delicti elements in whole or in part this does not preclude the passage of an ordinance which covers an act which might also be punishable under some state law where the corpus delicti of the offense is different. 

Thus an ordinance prohibiting picketing by violence would be valid even though an act in violation thereof might also constitute an assault, battery, riot, breach of the peace or unlawful assembly; in such case the ordinance prohibits the use of violence for the purpose of picketing. 

In re Bell, 19 Cal. (2d) 488.

STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION—EFFECT OF REPEAL 

Under Section 329 of the Political Code the repeal of a law creating a criminal offense does not constitute a bar to the indictment or information and punishment of an act already committed in violation of the repealed law. 

People vs. Barton, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 565.

STRIKING OUT EVIDENCE 

"When it is apparent from the question that an answer will contain evidence necessarily inadmissible, then a motion to strike out comes too late, unless preceded by an objection to the question." 

People vs. Costello, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 517.

CRIMINAL DIGEST TELEPHONE CONVERSATION 

In a prosecution for conspiracy to commit theft, the money being obtained by the sale of seals through telephonic solicitation, a witness was permitted to testify that he had received a telephone call from a voice which stated that it was a certain judge and witness was thereby solicited to purchase seals of the American Safety First Association, that the seals were delivered by a messenger who received his check which was returned in due course bearing the rubber stamp endorsement of said association, which endorsement was stipulated by counsel to be authentic. "Under the circumstances it was no more necessary to establish the actual identity of the voice than it would have been for the witness to have established that of an unknown person who had accosted the witness on the street, held himself out to be a certain person, and sold the witness the seals." 

People vs. Gaertner, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 388. After raiding a bookmaking establishment the officers answered when the telephone rang and voices at the other end of the line endeavored to place bets on horse races. Evidence held admissible as tending to prove that the premises were occupied for the purpose of recording wagers on horse races. 

People vs. Joffe, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 233. 

See also: People vs. Kelley, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 150. "There was no error in the admission of part of a telephone conversation heard by a witness. The part heard by the witness appears intelligible and the witness was properly allowed to testify to the part he heard, namely that part carried on by appellant." 

People vs. Montgomery, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 1.

After entering defendant's apartment the police reconnected the telephone wires which had been severed (quite obviously by defendant) and then a number of calls came in in which the senders declared that they wanted to place certain bets on horse races. 

Defendant contended it was error to receive evidence of the contents of the telephone messages since Section 605 of the Federal Communications Act provided that no person not authorized by the sender shall intercept or divulge the contents of a telephonic communication or receive the same. 

Held that merely because the United States Supreme Court had held that the act covered intrastate messages such decisions are not authority for controlling the procedure in a state court nor has Congress the power to control such procedure. "It is only when an intrastate communication gained by tapping telephone lines is offered in a federal court that it is inadmissible; we conclude that there is no constitutional inhibition upon the courts of this state to admit into evidence telephonic communications obtained by the interception thereof. Neither does section 605 of the Federal Communication Act constitute a restraint upon the state courts." "Even though section 605 applied to the interception of intrastate messages, it would not avail appellant here for the very good reason that there was no proof made of lack of consent of the sender of any of the messages." (The court evidently considers the burden of proving lack of consent to be upon the person raising the objection. C. W. F.) 

Held also that even though the rights of defendant were violated in securing the evidence it would still be admissible. 

People vs. Kelley, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 150.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

111 

TESTIMONY AT FORMER TRIAL OR PRELIMINARY EXAMINATION 

Where the evidence is sufficient to justify the conclusion that the witness is outside of the state and the witness was subjected to crossexamination at the preliminary examination, his testimony there given may be read upon the trial.

The question as to whether the facts establish a case permitting such reading of former testimony is within the discretion of the trial judge and his decision will not be reversed, except where such discretion is abused. 

People vs. Latham, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 35.

Proof that the witness had stated that she was going to Colorado and left taking with her all her possessions and that witness' mother had written from Colorado that witness had arrived and was in Colorado excused further search for witness in this state and her testimony at preliminary can be read. 

People vs. McIvain, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 366.

THEFT GENERAL 

Defendant was charged with grand theft on the theory that, having made a false statement in applying to the State Relief Administration for relief he received sums totaling in excess of $200. "Whether a series of successive acts of taking constitutes several thefts or one single crime must be determined by the particular facts and circumstances of each case. "While the general intent to deprive another of his possessions may have been framed in the mind of the accused before he completed his first offense, yet subsequent acts whereby the accused appropriated different sums of money or separate items of goods or chattels are distinct offenses. 

If the defendant had the fradulent intent to obtain property by false representations, then his obtaining property on the first occasion, under the inducement of his false representations, constituted a completed crime. 'When at a later date other property was obtained under the influence of the fraudulent representations which had earlier been made, a separate offense was committed.' (People vs. Ellison, 26 Cal. App. (2d) 496). In the instant case, the receipt of money from the State Relief Administration was in each instance a separate offense." 

People vs. Serna, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 106. "It will be noted that the act (Sec. 484 Penal Code) reads in the disjunctive and any one of the acts denounced constitutes the crime of theft. 

The offense of theft has been substituted for the former offense of larceny, embezzlement and obtaining money by false pretenses."

Defendant was charged in Count I with larceny, in Count 2 with false pretenses, and in Count 3 with procuring complainants to report falsely of their financial standing. Held that since there was but one transaction and but one unlawful taking only one crime was committed. 

People vs. Selk, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 140. "When one has knowingly and designedly by fraudulent representations defrauded another person, such representation shall be treated as continuing so as to cover any money, property or services as a result thereof. The receipt and appropriation of the moneys to his own use on the five several dates constituted five separate acts, each a complete crime in itself." 

People vs. Caldwell, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 268.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

To the contention that the court should have been instructed that to convict they must all agree upon a particular one of the three theories of theft the opinion states, "It was not necessary to require the jury to agree upon the theory. If, under any one of the theories set forth, they believed appellant had gained possession of and appropriated to his own use the moneys. he was guilty of theft." (Note: If the court means that if part of the jury convicted on the larceny theory, a part on the embezzlement theory and part on the theory of false pretenses, this would make a valid conviction of theft, would there be in fact a unanimous verdict? C. W. F.) 

People vs. Caldwell, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 268.

Evidence of necessary intent held sufficiently proved. 

People vs. Hopper, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 378.

THEFT-EMBEZZLEMENT THEORY 

"Having invested these funds as he was supposed to do the appellant was not required to immediately turn over a corresponding sum of money but both a proper demand and a sufficient compliance therewith involved his turning over the things in which the money had been invested. He not only did this but offered if it was desired and reasonable time were given, to convert the investments into cash and return it in that form. The absence of a fraudulent intent up to the time of the demand is conceded and we are unable to find any evidence either showing such an intent at that time or disclosing a failure to comply with a proper demand that he turn over the things in which the money had been invested." Evidence held insufficient to prove embezzlement. 

People vs. Wilde, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 482.

Defendant, a clerk and secretary in the park department of the City of Santa Barbara, whose duties were to be in charge of the office, make out payrolls and keep the minutes of the board and who by custom received funds from the recreational facilities and deposited same with the city treasurer, fraudulently appropriated during the period embraced in the indictment $1475.74. It was contended that she could not be guilty of embezzlement in the absence of a statute or ordinance imposing upon her a duty to receive and account for such funds. Held that the position was untenable, that she was guilty under Section 504 of the Penal Code. "There is no requirement in the statute that the funds misappropriated be received by the clerk or servant pursuant to a duty imposed by ordinance or statute." 

People vs. Kirk, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 136.

Defendant while acting as agent to exchange real property obtained an assignment of a mortgage which he sold and converted the property to his own use. Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Atwater, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 138.

Where defendant collected moneys for a judgment creditor under an agreement to remit one-half of the net proceeds of the collection "when and as collected" the fact that the settlement with the judgment debtor had not been completed does not excuse the failure to remit when a collection is made.

Held also that a demand by the creditor was not necessary where it appeared that the time for remittance under the agreement had long passed and there was no satisfactory explanation of the default. 

People vs. Hall, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 386.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

113 me 2 

THEFT-FALSE PRETENSE THEORY 

Defendant, with the cooperation of an employee of a finance company, secured loans on wrecked automobiles through the basic representation that the cars were in good condition and ample security for the loans. At the beginning of the relationship the employee was himself deceived as to the facts but later learned the true state of affairs but was induced to continue the fraudulent loaning by defendant. The owner of the loan company was deceived at all times and suffered a net loss of $18,000. Held that the conditional sales contracts executed by defendant and chattel mortgages upon worthless automobiles presented by defendant in connection with the various loans were sufficient memorandums in writing and false tokens within Section 1110 of the Penal Code. 

Held, also, that "the offense is complete when the money is obtained by false means and the defendant is not purged of culpability even by subsequent restoration, restitution or repayment." 

People vs. Wynn, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 723.

Conviction for theft of automobile affirmed. 

People vs. Alexander, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 480.

In a prosecution under the false pretense theory there must be the cor roboration required by Section 1110 of the Penal Code. 

People vs. Beilfuss, 55 A. C. A. 641. 

THEFT-LARCENY THEORY 

Defendant, left alone for a few minutes in his lawyer's office, took from the latter's desk an envelope containing money left by another client. When the money was missed the next day the lawyer questioned defendant, who said that he had taken it because he was afraid someone else might take it and said he would return the money but never did. At the trial he denied everything even that he had been in the lawyer's office. Conviction sustained. 

People vs. O'Neal, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 318.

The owner of property which is in the lawful possession of a lien holder entitled to such possession thereof to the exclusion of all others is, the lien holder, in the law of larceny the owner thereof and the actual owner of the property may be convicted of theft if the taking is otherwise such as to constitute larceny, but where such lien claimant has surrendered the property to the actual owner his right to a lien ceases, cannot be revived by any subsequently reacquired possession. 

People vs. Photo, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 345.

If one in good faith, takes the property of another, believing it legally to be his own or that he has a legal right to its possession, he is not guilty of larceny although his claim is based on a misconception of the law or of his rights under it, for although ignorance of the law and honest intentions cannot shield a man from civil liability for a tresspass committed by him, yet they do protect him from criminal liability by divesting the act of the felonious intent without which it cannot be a crime. 

People vs. Photo, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 345.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

THEFT-LARCENY BY TRICK AND DEVICE
