"Where one obtains possession of a thing of value from another upon the representation that it is to be used for a designated purpose and the statement is false, the victim of such representation is not thereby deprived of the title to his property. It follows that if at the time of gaining possession of property by means of such fraudulent statements the defendant cherished a secret purpose of converting the property to his own use and did so, he was guilty of theft." 

People vs. Hunter, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 87.

Prosecution on theory that defendant obtained money of complainant for investment in various business ventures. Evidence that complainant held defendant's notes for the money held to support the conclusion that he did not receive the money as a gift. 

People vs. Crandall, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 238.

Where money was given upon the representation that it would be used in a business venture, evidence that part of the money was used for personal expenditures, feminine jewelry and gifts was admissible. 

People vs. Crandall, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 238.

Defendants induced complainant to loan them $300.00, putting up as security what was represented to be a sack of gold worth $2400, the money to be used, with money of defendant's (actually a "fake bank roll") to pay the owner of the gold who it was represented was selling it to defendants. 

The "gold" was actually brass. Conviction of grand theft sustained. 

People vs. Alba, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 859.

Proof that defendants secured three hundred dollars from the victim without specifying in what form the money was is sufficient to sustain the allegation that the property stolen was "three hundred dollars lawful money of the United States," as pleaded. 

People vs. Alba, supra. "Grand theft is accomplished by fraudulently procuring a sum of money in excess of two hundred dollars from the owner by means of artifice, trickery or misrepresentations even though it is obtained as a pretended loan if it appears to be the intention of the accused person at the time of the transaction not to repay the money." 

People vs. Alba, 46 Cal. App. 2d) 859.

The fact that the victim subsequently recovered a judgment against defendants and satisfied it by execution does not relieve them from prosecution and conviction." Not even a voluntary repayment of the money after the crime was committed would relieve them of criminal liability." 

People vs. Alba, supra. Defendant induced the victim to play cards for money upon the assurance that he, defendant, would stand behind the other player and signal to the victim what cards his opponent held. After winning by this system the victim was induced to bet a large amount of money and lost, defendant having given a signal contrary to the cards held by victim's opponent. Evidence indicated that such opponent was in league with defendant. Conviction sustained. 

People vs. Solano, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

115 In a prosecution for theft in the nature of larceny by trick and device the corroboration required in a case of obtaining property by false pretenses is not required.

People vs. Santora, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 707. 

THEFT EMBEZZLEMENT-FALSE PRETENSES AND LARCENY DISTINGUISHED 

"If one is induced by false representation as to a past or existing fact to part with title to personal property, the law recognizes such a crime as the obtaining of that property by false pretenses chargeable as theft. On the other hand, where possession merely has been parted with and the transaction is fraudulently secured with the present felonious intention to convert the property so acquired, the offense is recognizable as larceny." 

People vs. Selk, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 140. 

People vs. Sasstora, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 707.

Distinctions as discussed in People vs. Fawver, 29 Cal. App. (2d) 775, People vs. Beilfuss, 55 A. C. A. 641. 

THEFT-PLEADING 

The allegation that defendant "took the personal property of another” means property in the possession of another who is entitled as bailee, lien claimant or otherwise, to retain possession thereof for some benefit or profit to himself to the exclusion of others, rather than the absolute ownership, defined in Section 679, Civil Code." 

People vs. Photo, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 345.

An information charged the theft from the person of the victim of described personal property but did not allege that such property was "the personal property of another. Held an example of “careless pleading" which would have required a reversal of the judgment prior to the 1927 and 1929 amendments to Section 952 of the Penal Code but which under the present law would not warrant a reversal. 

People vs. Price, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 59.

The allegation that the stolen property was taken "from the person of one Burdette Cleary" is a sufficient allegation that the stolen property was in the possession of Cleary at the time of its taking. 

People vs. Price, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 59. 

"Compliance with subdivision 2 of Section 950 of the Penal Code does not require that the information state the kind of grand theft with which the defendant is charged." (Citing People vs. Fewkes, 214 Cal. 149). 

People vs. Alexander, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 393.

TRANSCRIPT OF TESTIMONY (See also: IMPEACHMENT, TESTIMONY AT FORMER TRIAL OR PRELIMINARY EXAMINATION). 

Where the shorthand reporter who took down the statement, questions and answers, of the defendant and transcribed the same and at one point inserted the note "no answer," such note is not a part of the statement and is merely the declaration of the reporter and can not be read as a part of the statement. The fact that no answer was made to the question should be established by the testimony of someone present. 

People vs. Lee, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 199.

116. 

CRIMINAL DIGEST TRIAL GENERAL 

Where defendant had been arraigned and went to trial on an amended information the statement, by defense counsel, that she waived the reading of the information is a waiver of the reading of the amended information upon which defendant was tried as that was the only information before the court. 

People vs. Shack, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 16.

TRIAL PUBLIC 

In a prosecution for statutory rape and 288 the court, being informed that there was no stipulation to the exclusion of spectators, made a statement that "Our rule is that we will have to go to a room too small to let them in, if that was your desire." The trial proceeded, however, without the spectators being excluded. The court says that had the judge "put into effect his declared intention and excluded all spectators, his action in doing so would have been in direct violation of . . . . the Constitution . . . . and would have required a reversal of the judgment," and says that the propriety of suggesting the exclusion of spectators "is open to serious question. Indeed, in conceivable circumstances, such a suggestion could be highly prejudicial." Held that in the instant case defendant was not prejudiced. 

People vs. Stangler, 43 A. C. A. 671. 

18 Cal. (2d) 688.

Before instructing the jury the court declared that no one would be permitted to leave or enter the court room while the jury was being instructed and during the giving of the instructions the door of the courtroom was locked. Defendant made no objection. "Here we cannot say that the order in question prejudiced the defendant's rights or that he was thereby deprived of a fair public trial." 

People vs. Buck, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 558.

TRIAL REOPENING CASE 

Defendant was tried for having, together with one Bartlett, issued a check without funds. "The defendant was not prejudiced by the reopening of the cause, after the prosecution announced that its evidence was closed, for the purpose of permitting an officer to identify certain checks which were relied upon to show the purpose or intent with which the particular one which is involved in this case was uttered. The granting or denial of a motion to reopen a case for further evidence after a party has rested, is within the sound discretion of the trial judge . . . all of these checks had been previously referred to by the prosecution. . . . and witnesses had been examined in regard to them." 

People vs. Descant, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 343.

TRIAL SEPARATE 

Two aiders and abettors in the murder moved for separate trials. Held the court was correct in denying the motion. (Note: Apparently no reason for the severance was advanced other than the fact that the moving parties were not the active perpetrators of the murder. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Ives, 17 Cal. (2d) 493.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

117 Appellant Bonzani urged she should have been granted a separate trial basing the claim upon her acquittal under one of the counts. Held, however, that there was evidence upon which she could properly have been convicted under the count of which she was acquitted and that there was no error. (Note: Case suggests that, had there been no evidence warranting Bonzani's prosecution on the charge of which she was acquitted a severance of some sort should have been granted. C. W. F.) 

People vs. Kaye, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 802.

The matter of granting separate trials to defendants jointly charged is a matter resting "solely in the sound discretion of the court." 

People vs. Wilson, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 218.

Whether a motion for a severance shall be granted is a matter entirely within the discretion of the trial judge. 

People vs. Eudy, 12 Cal. (2d) 41, 46. 

Quoted People vs. Duane, 21 Cal. (2d) 65.

VALUE 

Part of the property stolen had been consigned to complainant for sale. 

Defendant contended that the value of this property was the price at which it had been consigned. Held not the proper test of value. "The value to be placed upon stolen articles for the purpose of establishing a felony charge is the fair market value of the property and not the value of the property to any particular individual." 

People vs. Latham, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 35.

VARIANCE-IMMATERIAL 

Where the information alleged, in a case of receiving stolen property, that defendant received the property from two named persons but the proof showed that he had received the property from only one of such persons, the variance is immaterial. 

People vs. Murray, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 209.

Where the thefts were alleged to have been from two persons while the evidence showed in some instances that the money was delivered to the defendant by but one of them, the erroneous allegation as to person injured was immaterial (citing 956 P. C.), especially in this case where the two persons had an agreement by which all of the transactions were for their joint account.

People vs. Beilfuss, 55 A. C. A. 641. 

VENUE 

Proof that the crime was committed in Laguna Beach held sufficient since the court could take judicial notice that Laguna Beach was in 

Orange County. 

People vs. Andrew, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 126.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

"Venue, like any other fact in a criminal case, may also be established by circumstantial evidence, and proof of venue is sufficient if the circumstances shown in evidence lead to a conclusion that the crime was committed in the county as charged."

Proof showed that defendant picked up a six year old girl, drove her around and sexually attacked her. The nearest point outside the county was 

25 or 30 miles from where the child was picked up. According to the child's estimate it took not to exceed 25 minutes to drive from the place of beginning to the place where the crime was committed. Defendant testified as to his whereabouts on the evening in question and though denying the commission of the crime, testified that he never left the county. Proof of venue held sufficient. "As was said by this court in People vs. Carter, 10 Cal. App. (2d) 387, "The state gives no assurance to its feloniously insubordinated citizens that the venue of their crimes will be fixed beyond a reasonable doubt; that doctrine applies only to the issue of guilt." 

People vs. Harkness, 51 Cal. App. (2d) 133.

Also see: People vs. Bastio, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 615. 

Where there was no evidence that defendant had taken the victim out of the county and he testified that he never left the county. Proof that the offense was committed in the county was sufficiently established. 

Also see: People vs. Bastio, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 615. 

VERDICT-IMPEACHMENT BY JUROR 

A verdict cannot be impeached by the statement of a juror whether such statement takes the form of a sworn statement of the juror or is sought to be establish by proof of an extra-judicial statement of the juror. 

People vs. Siminiani, 45 Cal. App. (2d) 535. ((See this case more fully set forth under New Trial-Misconduct of Juror).

VERDICTS-INCONSISTENT (See also: Dismissal;-Separate Offenses) 

Defendant was found guilty of two counts of rape and acquitted of two counts of violating section 288, the latter based on the same incidents involved in the rape counts. Verdicts held not inconsistent. 

People vs. Stangler, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 671. 

18 Cal. (2d) 688

At a joint trial of two defendants for one robbery one was convicted of first degree robbery and appellant was convicted of second degree robbery. Held that appellant could not complain because the verdict was more favorable to him than it should have been. 

People vs. L'Hommedieu, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 27. 

Where at defendant's request, the court instructed the jury to elect between and not convict on both counts defendant cannot be heard to complain of alleged inconsistency of the verdicts. (It is doubtful if this is a rule which can be safely followed and it would seem that where actual and vital inconsistency exists between verdicts, as where an acquittal upon one count would of necessity compel a like verdict as to the one on which a guilty

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

119 verdict was returned, that the conviction could not stand even though the defendant had requested an instruction to the effect that he could be convicted under one count only. In the case at bar there was no inconsistency, the charges being as to crimes in which the elements were different, viz. violation of section 681 Penal Code and battery. C. W. F.) 

People vs. McMillan, 45 Cal. App. 821.

Where based upon one act, defendant is charged with statutory rape and a violation of section 288 of the Penal Code an acquittal of violating the latter section has no effect upon the verdict finding defendant guilty of rape. 

People vs. Stangler, 18 Cal. (2d) 760.

Defendant was charged, based upon the same act with negligent homicide (500 Vehicle Code) and manslaughter, convicted of negligent homicide and found not guilty of manslaughter. Verdicts held not inconsistent. The court reviews the decisions and points out that People vs. Andursky, 75 Cal. 

App. 16 and People vs Day, 199 Cal. 78, were prior to the 1927 amendment of section 954 to include the words, “A verdict of acquittal of one or more counts shall not be deemed or held to be an acquittal of any other count," and that the later cases which follow the Day case did not discuss the amendment to section 954. The court cites People vs. Rawney, 123 Cal. 

App. 403, which follows and enforces the amendment to section 954; also 

People vs Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 438, in which a dismissal of one count was held not to bar a conviction on another count. The court also points out that the two offenses charged are not identical. 

People vs. Amick, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 662; 20 Cal. (2d) 255.

Where, based upon the same transaction, defendant is charged with a violation of Section 503 of the Vehicle Code and grand theft, an acquittal on one count is not inconsistent with conviction under the other. 

People vs. Jeffries, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 801. "The law is established in California that section 500 of the Vehicle Code (negligent homicide) defines a crime different from involuntary manslaughter, or from any other crime defined in the Penal Code." (Case arose prior to 1941 amendment).

People vs. Crow, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666. 

A verdict finding defendant not guilty of negligent homicide does not affect a verdict finding defendant guilty of manslaughter even though both charges are based upon the same facts and evidence. 

People vs. Crow, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 666.

Where defendant was charged with kidnapping the prosecutrix and then raping her and her story was sufficient in law to show both offenses the jury might still convict of kidnapping and acquit of the charge of rape, they might believe part and reject part of her testimony. 

People vs. Hunter, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 243.

A verdict convicting defendant of conspiracy to commit rape is not inconsistent with an acquittal of the charge of kidnapping even though the overt acts alleged in the conspiracy count are identical with the acts alleged to constitute kidnapping, said acts being the taking of the prosecutrix outside of the city of Salinas. Decision based upon Section 954 Penal Code as amended to provide that an acquittal upon one count shall not operate as an acquittal of any other count. (People vs. Derenzo, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 

411, and People vs. Kearney, 20 Cal. (2d 435, cited). 

People vs. Guerrero, 53 Cal. App. (2d) 508.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

Where two persons are charged with robbery the jury may convict but one of them and an acquittal of one does not operate as the acquittal of the other. 

People vs. Wilson, 54 Cal. App. (2d) 520.

Even though but one offense was committed convictions of grand theft, extortion and robbery are not inconsistent. In such a case, where the sentences on the three verdicts were made to run concurrently the conviction was affirmed. 

People vs. Seigel, 55 Cal. App. (2d) 325.

VERDICT-SPECIAL 

There is no merit in the contention that in addition to the general verdict of "guilty" the jury should return a special verdict upon the point of statute of limitations since the general verdict covers all issues except those raised by a special plea such as former jeopardy where the Code provides for such a plea "This does not mean that it would have been improper for the jury to have brought in a special verdict on this issue. The Code confers on the jury the power to bring in a special verdict under the proper circumstances. the jury." (Pen. Code 1152). But that section makes it optional with 

People vs. Allen, 47 Cal. App. (2d) 735.

VERDICT-SUFFICIENCY 

Where defendant was charged, under Section 11160 of the Health and Safety Code, with selling marihuana, a verdict finding defendant guilty of "Violation of the State Narcotic Act, a felony, as charged . . . in the information" is valid and unmistakably expresses the intention to convict defendant of the offense charged. 

People vs. Grijalva, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 690.

WEAPONS-GENERAL 

Though there was no direct proof that as to one of the counts defendant was armed with a weapon, as charged, the jury returned a verdict finding that such charge was true. Held that this did not warrant the reversal of the conviction of the offense charged since the allegation as to defendant being armed was not part of the allegation of the offense but merely in accordance with the requirements of Section 969c and 1158a of the Penal Code. Appellate court merely directs trial court to make a new order directing whether the sentence on the count in question should or should not run concurrently with the sentences on the other counts and that the element of being armed be eliminated as to the count in which the evidence was insufficient. 

People vs. Duane, 49 Cal. App. (2d) 642.

Defendant was an unnaturalized native of the island of Luzon. Held that 

Deadly Weapons Act did not violate the constitutional due process of law provision; that the burden of proof under 1983 Code of Civil Procedure as to citizenship was on defendant. (Morrison vs. California, 291 U. S. 82, distinguished as Morrison was an American citizen, his co-defendant being the alien;) that the law includes natives of the Philippine Islands not citizens of the United States. 

People vs. Cordero, 50 Cal. App. (2d) 146.

WITNESS-GENERAL 

"We know of no authority requiring that the assent to the taking of the oath must be audibly given." 

People vs. O'Connor, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 301.

A mere question by the court to a child witness “Will you tell us the truth and the whole truth and nothing but the truth?”, and “Will you call on God to witness that you tell the truth?", is not sufficient to constitute the administering of an oath. Indicated that the oath must include words to the effect, "You do solemnly swear" or "You do affirm." 

People vs. Portlock, 118 Cal. App. 566.

Indicated that in the opinion of the court some sort of oath should be administered before voire dire examination of a child witness under 10 years of age but no authority is cited nor is such a conclusion stated. (Note: 

Since the very purpose of the voire dire is to determine the ability of the witness and his comprehension of an oath, it would seem that no oath should be administered until it appears that the child can comprehend the nature of an oath. C. W. F.) 

People vs. O'Connor, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 301.

An officer gave testimony in the absence of the reporter. When this was noticed defense counsel stipulated that the witness had not been on the stand before and made no objection when the testimony was repeated with the reporter present. Held defendant suffered no prejudice. 

People vs. Holquin, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 551.

WITNESS-BIAS OF 

'It is an unquestioned rule of evidence that when any witness admits bias and prejudice on cross-examination, on redirect the reasons for such prejudice cannot be gone into, at least where such reasons involve other alleged offenses outside the issue." 

People vs. Zemavasky, 20 Cal. (2d) 56.

WITNESS-COMPETENCY-GENERAL 

"Unsoundness of mind does not per se establish the incompetency of a witness. ... One may be insane upon a certain subject but sane as to others. 

One may be unable to distinguish legally between right and wrong, and still be able to narrate facts chronologically, correctly, intelligently and truly." 

People vs. Ives, 17 Cal. (2d) 493, 509.

WITNESS-COMPETENCY-CHILD 

"It is the law that children under ten years of age cannot be witnesses where they appear incapable of receiving just impressions of the facts respecting which they are examined or of relating them truly. (Sec. 1880, Code of Civil Procedure). The last section means nothing more than that if a child under ten years of age appears to the trial judge to be competent, i. e., to have the capacity to receive impressions and to relate them truthfully, he becomes a competent witness. After a judge has heard the child give his testimony and has determined that he was a competent witness, such determination is not a matter of review any more than his ruling upon the capacity of any other witness." 

People vs. Harrison, 46 Cal. App. (2d) 779.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

WITNESS-COMPETENCY-INSANE PERSON 

The complaining witness testified at the preliminary examination on June 24, 1940, at which time she was brought in from a sanitarium where she "was confined" after suffering a "collapse." On July 9, 1940, she was adjudged insane and committed to a state hospital. At the trial, it being shown that the witness was insane and confined in a state hospital, the court, over objection, permitted the reading of her former testimony in evidence. 

The court calls attention to the fact that "it has been held that a person who can apprehend the obligation of an oath and is capable of giving a fairly correct account of the things he has seen or heard, is competent as a witness, although he may be afflicted with some form of insanity."

Defendant sought to invoke the rule that the fact that a person has been recently found unsound by a court. . . . and that he is an inmate of an asylum for the insane is prima facie evidence that he is of unsound mind and imposes the burden on the party offering him to show his competency. 

The court here points out that the witness was not adjudged insane until after she had testified and hence the rule did not apply. The court also says, "Moreover, the character of Mrs. Lantz' testimony given at the preliminary hearing was of itself proof of her competency at the time; and an adjudication of insanity is not to be taken as conclusive upon the question of the competency of a witness."

Held the court did not err in admitting the testimony. 

People vs. Crandall, 43 Cal. App. (2d) 238. 

WITNESS-CREDIBILITY-INSTRUCTIONS 

The stock instruction that a witness "wilfully false in one material part of his or her testimony is to be distrusted in others," etc., held a correct statement of the law and not erroneous because of the inclusion of the word "wilfully." 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 270.

WITNESS-DEFENDANT AS-INSTRUCTIONS 

"It has repeatedly been held that the trial court need not single out the testimony of the defendant and give instructions with particular reference to it.' 

People vs. Marvich, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 858. 

To same effect: People vs: Knight, 44 Cal. App. (2d) 887.

WITNESS-DEFENDANT-FAILURE TO TESTIFY 

"Appellant did not take the witness stand to explain or deny any of the incriminating evidence introduced against him, and therefore, under the provisions of section 13 of Article I of the state Constitution such failure was proper subject matter for the jury's consideration. 

People vs. Murray, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 209.

CRIMINAL DIGEST 

123 1 

WITNESS-DEFENDANT-FAILURE TO TESTIFY-INSTRUCTIONS 

An instruction that "the defendants not having taken the witness stand to testify should be considered as a right that the defendants have and should in no way be considered against them" is properly refused. 

People vs. Pianezzi, 42 Cal. App. (2d) 265. 

Citing: People vs. Perry, 14 Cal. (2d) 387. 

WITNESS-EXCLUSION OF 

"Although there may be some conflict in the decisions of other jurisdictions, it has been held in this state that disobedience of a trial court's order excluding witnesses from the courtroom may be punished as a contempt but does not constitute ground for exclusion of the testimony of the witness. (People vs. Russell, 34 Cal. App. (2d) 665) 

But it is well settled that an appellate court is not warranted in reversing a judgment of conviction upon the ground of erroneous exclusion of testimony unless it affirmatively appears ... that the testimony was material, relevant, not too remote and not cumulative." 

People vs. Duane, 49 A. C. A. 835. 

(Note: Court here refused to permit the witness who remained in court after an exclusion order to testify. One judge dissented holding it reversible error) On hearing this the Supreme Court says:—“There can be no doubt that the court committed error in refusing to permit Mrs. Leep to testify. The violation by a witness of an order excluding witnesses from the courtroom does not make such witness incompetent nor furnish grounds for a refusal to permit him to testify. The proper recourse is contempt proceedings against the witness." (Court here notes that the excluded evidence was vital to the alibi defense.) Held reversible error to refuse to let the witness testify. 

Also that, where the court erroneously refused to permit a witness to testify under any circumstances regardless of admissibility or revelancy basing its ruling on the incompetency of the witness and it can be ascertained from the record that the witness' testimony was vital "no offer of proof is necessary to establish that such error is prejudicial." 

People vs. Duane, 21 Cal. (2d) 65.

WORDS AND PHRASES 

"IMPRISONMENT": The period of detention in jail imposed as a condition of probation is not the service of a term of imprisonment. 

People vs. Wallach, 8 Cal. App. (2d) 129, 133. "VIOLENT" as used in defining an assault. See, under ASSAULT BY

MEANS OF FORCE, etc. 

People vs. Bumbaugh, 48 Cal. App. (2d) 791. "HOT" as synonymous of "stolen." See, under RECEIVING STOLEN

PROPERTY, People vs. Knott, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 439. 

"CARNAL KNOWLEDGE" means sexual intercourse and both natural and unnatural copulation. 

People vs. Battalina, 52 Cal. App. (2d) 685, and cases cited.
